\thispagestyle{plain}

I declare that this project report is my own work. Where the work of others has
been used, it is cited and acknowledged.

The multi-framework benchmark infrastructure, the baseline framework
integrations, and the initial GRPO/PPO/DPO experimentation were produced by the
Semester-3 project group, of which I was a member, and are inherited here as
the shared foundation. The research direction, the experimental expansion, the
statistical analyses, the P1--P8 paper series, the reporting and registry
artefacts, the adaptive group-size controller, the applied fraud study, and the
E1--E14 held-out evaluation campaign reported in this document are my
individual contribution, carried out under the guidance of my project guide.

Every quantitative result in this report is bound to an evidence file in the
project repository. Where a result is incomplete, blocked, or negative, it is
reported as such rather than omitted. The boundary between inherited and
individual work is stated precisely in \texttt{platform\_hybrid/sem 4
work/PROVENANCE.md}.

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\chapter*{Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}
\thispagestyle{plain}

This report presents a multi-framework benchmark for group-relative
reinforcement-learning post-training of large language models, a study of that
post-training's behaviour across libraries, scales, group sizes and datasets,
and a held-out evaluation of a single trained actor against fourteen agentic
and reasoning benchmark suites.

The benchmark is multi-framework by construction: a shared task, reward and
decoding configuration is dispatched through thin adapters onto each backend's
own interface, so the same experiment is runnable on any of them without
re-implementing the grader. That design property is distinct from completed-run
parity, and this report does not conflate the two. Of the cross-launcher roster,
only Tinker and TRL produced completed runs in this release; the veRL and
OpenRLHF entries in the comparison artefact are dry-run placeholders that
resolve a launch plan without executing it, and are never pooled with real
results or read as framework-comparison evidence (Chapter 3 \S3.8; Chapter 5
\S5.1). Comparative claims in this report are therefore constrained to the
runs actually recorded, and the diagnostic portability results are stated at
the strength their own source asserts.

The central methodological commitment is \emph{attribution}: every comparative
claim is made with the entire training stack held fixed except the one factor
under test. Four de-confound pillars --- a matched same-stack PPO/GRPO
contrast, the Zero-Variance Fraction as a signal-starvation diagnostic, a
group-size sweep, and a length-bias and held-out-generalisation study --- are
reported under that commitment, alongside a cross-library cross-scale scaling
study, a minimum reporting standard for stack-conditioned results, a
machine-readable stack registry, an adaptive group-size controller derived from
the ZVF diagnostic, and an applied credit-card fraud case study.

The principal results are deliberately conservative. Across more than seventy
runs spanning roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude in model scale, \textbf{no
reliable scaling law is recovered from reward curves}. A matched same-stack
comparison of PPO and GRPO finds the two estimators \textbf{statistically
indistinguishable} (Welch $p = 0.7605$). A single-seed group-size sweep does
\textbf{not} establish an optimum, and trainability is non-monotone in group
size. Four of eleven GRPO runs peak before 65\% of training and then decay,
consistent with length bias rather than capability gain, while Dr.\ GRPO does
not exhibit the pattern.

The E1--E14 campaign evaluates one Tinker-trained actor
(\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} with the
\texttt{pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal} adapter) using each suite's
own native evaluator at a pinned revision, with no per-lane tuning. Four suites
carry strictly complete graded scopes: LAB-Bench public split (1{,}967/1{,}967
questions, micro accuracy 0.2288), AgentDojo benign utility (97/97, utility
0.9072), VerilogEval (312/312, $129/312 = 41.35\%$ pass@1), and Omni-MATH (all
4{,}428 dispositions recorded, official accuracy $2{,}271/4{,}428 = 51.31\%$
reproduced). Two carry full verified scores under their original contracts:
SWE-bench Pro ($2/731 = 0.274\%$ pass@1, with all eighteen non-native outcomes
retained in the denominator) and VerilogEval. The remaining lanes are reported
as partial, quota-blocked, or externally blocked, each with an explicit
terminal state and a named reopen condition.

Original-contract and replacement-scope benchmark results are never pooled, no
cross-suite aggregate is computed, and no claim of improvement over a baseline
is made anywhere in this work. All structural claims were re-verified by
deterministic code checks on 19 September 2026 (11/11 PASS).

\vspace{0.25in}
\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} GRPO, RLVR, zero-variance fraction, signal
starvation, stack-conditioned results, benchmark evaluation, reproducibility,
held-out evaluation, attribution.

\part{Part I --- Foundations}


\chapter{Introduction}\label{introduction}

\section{Background: critic-free RL post-training and the group-relative
estimator}\label{background-critic-free-rl-post-training-and-the-group-relative-estimator}


\fig{fig_grpo_loop}
{The GRPO training loop. A prompt is sampled $G$ times, each completion is scored by a reward function, advantages are formed relative to the group, and the policy is updated. When all $G$ rewards coincide the group-relative advantage collapses to zero and no gradient flows.}
{fig:grpoloop}



Post-training is now the stage at which a base language model is turned
into something that reliably follows instructions and solves problems.
For several years the dominant recipe was reinforcement learning from
human feedback (RLHF), in which a learned reward model is trained on
human preference comparisons and then used as the reward signal for a
policy-optimisation loop. RLHF works, but it introduces a second learned
model whose errors the policy can exploit, and it makes reward
construction expensive and hard to audit. The alternative that has taken
hold more recently is reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards
(RLVR): instead of a learned reward model, the reward comes from a
programmatic checker --- exact-match against a reference answer for
grade-school mathematics, unit tests for code, a formal or semi-formal
validator for structured output. The checker is deterministic,
transparent, and free to run. What it gives up in generality it gains in
measurability: every reward is a fact about a generated string rather
than a prediction from a model (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).

The workhorse optimiser for both settings has been proximal policy
optimisation (PPO), which optimises a clipped surrogate objective
against a value function critic. The critic is a second network, usually
of comparable size to the policy, trained to predict the expected return
of the current state; its predictions supply the baseline that the
advantage estimator subtracts. This works, but it is expensive and
fragile. The critic consumes memory that could hold policy parameters,
it has its own learning rate and its own failure modes, and its errors
propagate directly into the policy gradient as biased advantages.

Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) removes the critic entirely.
Rather than learn a value function, GRPO samples a group of \(G\)
completions for the same prompt, scores all of them with the verifier,
and derives the advantage of each completion from the group's own reward
statistics. Concretely, for a group with rewards \(r_1, \dots, r_G\),
the advantage assigned to completion \(i\) is a within-group
standardisation,

\[A_i = \frac{r_i - \mu_g}{\sigma_g + \varepsilon}, \qquad \mu_g = \frac{1}{G}\sum_j r_j, \qquad \sigma_g^2 = \frac{1}{G}\sum_j (r_j - \mu_g)^2,\]

which is then used in a clipped, KL-regularised policy-gradient update.
The empirical baseline is the group mean; there is no value network to
train, no value loss to weight, and no critic to keep in sync with the
policy. The trade is a much simpler optimiser for a much smaller
effective batch, since \(G\) samples of the same prompt now carry one
learning signal rather than \(G\) independent ones.

That trade has a sharp consequence, and it is the observation this
project is organised around. Because the advantage is a
\emph{within-group} standardisation, its numerator is a deviation from
the group mean and its denominator is the group's own spread. When every
completion in a group receives the same verifier outcome --- all
correct, or all incorrect --- the group contributes nothing:
\(\sigma_g = 0\), every numerator is zero, and every advantage \(A_i\)
is exactly zero. The group has been fully sampled and fully scored, at
full token cost, and the reward-relative part of the update is
identically zero. The KL penalty toward the reference model still acts,
so the policy does drift slightly toward the reference; but no
\emph{reward-driven} learning occurs on that group. We call the fraction
of a training batch that falls into this state the \textbf{Zero-Variance
Fraction (ZVF)}, and it is the single measurement that most of this
report's diagnostics are built around (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).

ZVF is not a rare edge case. In the regime this project studies it is
the \emph{typical} state of a batch: the Phase-1 report records a ZVF of
approximately 0.72--0.77 on its main configuration, meaning that roughly
three quarters of a rollout batch contributes no reward-relative
gradient at all (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).
The fraction is not fixed, however, and it responds to the one knob that
most directly controls it: ZVF falls from 0.845 at \(G = 2\) to 0.631 at
\(G = 16\), asymptoting at 0.631 on the measured sweep, a decline of
0.214 across that range (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_synthesis\_zvf.tex; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf\_iter122.tex). Larger groups buy
more contrast, with diminishing returns that saturate well before the
group is small enough to be cheap.

There is a second and less comfortable observation in the background,
and it is about attribution rather than mechanism. ``GRPO'' is not a
single algorithm. It is a label that covers implementations in Tinker,
TRL, SkyRL, verl, OpenRLHF and a growing number of research forks, each
of which chooses its own answer parser, sampling temperature, top-\(p\),
KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA target set, precision and evaluation
harness. Two runs that both say ``GRPO'' in their titles may differ in a
dozen ways that plausibly move the outcome, and the reported headline
number does not distinguish between them. The project's own
stack-sensitivity probe makes this concrete: a nominally matched
configuration (Qwen3-8B, seed 42, \(G = 8\), learning rate
\(1\times10^{-5}\), GSM8K-500, 30 steps, visible hyperparameters held
equal) was run across four framework paths with results recorded in
\texttt{experiments/results/framework\_comparison.json} (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex). A variance decomposition
over 42 experiments attributed \(\eta^2 = 0.546\) of the variation to
library or framework, \(\eta^2 = 0.558\) to training algorithm,
\(\eta^2 = 0.471\) to model family and \(\eta^2 = 0.322\) to model scale
--- with the important caveat, stated by the source itself, that these
factors are confounded with one another and with back-end, reward
implementation, hardware and task (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex). The framework term is not a
nuisance parameter to be wished away. It is of the same order as the
algorithm term.

\section{A Motivating Example}\label{a-motivating-example}


\fig{fig_confounding}
{The confounding problem this work addresses. A single reported reward figure sits on top of an unstated stack; without fixing and reporting that stack the number is not attributable to the method under test.}
{fig:confounding}



Consider a single training step with a group size of \(G = 8\) on an
easy GSM8K prompt that the current policy already solves. All eight
sampled completions produce the correct final answer, so each receives
reward \(r_i = 1\). The group mean is \(\mu_g = 1\) and the group
standard deviation is \(\sigma_g = 0\), so every advantage \(A_i = 0\).
The compute spent on that group is real --- eight full forward-decode
passes at whatever temperature the sampler is configured with --- and
the group supplies no task-learning signal. The KL-regularisation term
still nudges the policy toward the reference, but the point stands: no
reward-driven learning happens on this group. Now imagine a batch of 16
such prompts in which 12 are already solved and 4 are not:
\(12/16 = 0.75\) of the batch is ``dark'' compute (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).
What the Semester 4 work adds to this picture is that 0.75 is not a
worst case. It is the neighbourhood of the measured typical regime, ZVF
\(\approx 0.72\)--0.77, and it is therefore the baseline against which
any claim that GRPO is learning efficiently has to be read.

Now suppose, having noticed this, that one wants to answer the obvious
follow-up question: is the group-relative estimator actually a better
choice than a learned critic? The natural experiment is a matched
comparison --- same model, same training prompts, same verifier, same
step budget, same evaluation, with PPO and GRPO as the only difference.
The project ran that contrast on Qwen3-8B and recorded the result in its
own test family. The Welch \(t\)-test on PPO versus GRPO gives
\(p = 0.7605\), the least significant entry in the report's twenty-test
family after Benjamini--Hochberg correction and the only one that does
not survive it (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex;
source: REPRODUCE.md). The accompanying effect-size table gives the
shape of that null: ten runs per arm, Cohen's \(d = -0.14\), 95\%
confidence interval \([-1.02, 0.74]\), observed power \(0.06\) and a
minimum detectable effect of \(1.25\) in the units of the outcome
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex). Read carefully,
that is a failure to detect a difference at this sample size, not a
demonstration that the two estimators are equivalent; the confidence
interval is wide enough to contain effects in both directions, and the
study was in no position to rule out anything but a very large one.

Two further observations from the same study make the example sharper
rather than cleaner. First, the same PPO-versus-GRPO contrast on
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct produces \(d = 12.75\) with a 95\% confidence
interval of \([8.49, 17.00]\) and \(p < 0.001\) (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex). The apparent answer is not
merely weak on Qwen; it reverses across model families. Second, the Qwen
arms were not executed on a common back-end --- the GRPO arm ran through
Tinker-managed infrastructure and the PPO arm on a Modal H100 cluster
--- and the source states this explicitly and declines to draw the
algorithmic conclusion it invites: ``We therefore treat the PPO/GRPO
rows as stack-conditioned evidence, not as an algorithm diagnostic
baseline'', and ``We therefore withhold a directional Qwen algorithm
claim'' (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex). The same
section records an aggregation gap that illustrates why such care is
warranted: the Qwen PPO last-10 GSM8K figure is 22.5\% in the primary
results ledger but 35.0\% in the statistical summary, and the source
names the ledger value as primary and treats the summary value as
evidence of an aggregation defect rather than as a second measurement
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex).

The genuinely same-stack evidence in the repository points the same way,
and it is worth stating plainly because it is a negative. The E1 audit
takes four GRPO-family variants (DAPO, GSPO, Dr.~GRPO, AERO) plus the
GRPO reference, runs five arms across eight paired seeds for 40 units at
a frozen stack fingerprint, and evaluates on 500 held-out GSM8K examples
per unit. Every one of the four contrasts is inconclusive: DAPO
\(+0.00100\), 95\% CI \([-0.00450, +0.00675]\), \(p = 0.756\); GSPO
\(+0.00500\), CI \([-0.00125, +0.01200]\), \(p = 0.210\); Dr.~GRPO
\(-0.00200\), CI \([-0.00950, +0.00725]\), \(p = 0.673\); AERO
\(-0.00075\), CI \([-0.00825, +0.00675]\), \(p = 0.858\). The pre-audit
aggregator's verdict that the differences ``disappear'' is itself
declared invalid, because the locked exact noncentral paired-\(t\)
minimum detectable effect of \(0.010115893\) exceeds the \(0.010\)
margin the study was designed around (source:
autoresearch/deli-neurips-tmlr-260802/audits/statistical\_refutation.md).
The audit's permitted wording is deliberately narrow: across eight
paired seeds no arm produced a BH-significant held-out difference from
GRPO, and none met the locked exact-power equivalence rule --- which is
not the same as saying the methods are the same, nor that they fail to
improve.

Taken together, these two halves of the example are the whole motivation
for what follows. The mechanism half shows that a large fraction of the
compute in a GRPO run is not doing what a casual reading of the loss
curve suggests it is doing. The attribution half shows that the most
natural comparative question one would ask about that mechanism --- is
this estimator better than the alternative? --- cannot be answered from
the label ``GRPO'' and ``PPO'' alone, and that the honest answer on the
project's own evidence is a non-significant, underpowered,
back-end-confounded contrast rather than a ranking. Neither observation
is a defect of the GRPO estimator; both are defects of what gets
measured and what gets reported.

\section{Problem Statement}\label{problem-statement}

The project is organised around four problems, in decreasing order of
how often the literature acknowledges them.

\textbf{Attribution is missing.} Comparative claims about RL
post-training are routinely made across frameworks that differ in
parser, sampler, KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA targets, precision and
evaluator. A reported gain from ``GRPO'' over ``PPO'' is, on most
published evidence, a gain from one complete stack over another complete
stack, with no way to say which component produced it. The project's own
variance decomposition puts the framework term at a magnitude comparable
to the algorithm term (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex), and its matched Qwen
contrast returns \(p = 0.7605\) (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/main\_eai\_body.tex). The problem is not that
these numbers are surprising; it is that no paper in this area is
obliged to report the information that would let a reader separate them.

\textbf{Signal starvation is invisible.} Standard training logs report
reward, loss, gradient norm, entropy and completion length. None of
these surfaces the fraction of a batch whose group advantage is exactly
zero. A run can show a rising reward curve and a falling ZVF, or a
rising reward curve with a ZVF pinned near 0.8, and the two look
identical in a loss plot. The fraction of sampling budget buying no
reward-relative gradient is, in the project's own measurements, the
largest measured source of zero-advantage sampling on this fixed setup
(source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex),
and it is not part of any standard telemetry.

\textbf{There is no reporting or provenance standard.} Even where
authors are willing to describe their stack, there is no
machine-readable form in which to do it, no validator that checks
whether a claim's recorded configuration is internally consistent, and
no accepted minimum set of fields that a stack-conditioned result must
carry. Consequently, results cannot be pooled, cannot be compared across
papers, and cannot be audited after the fact.

\textbf{Evaluation results are rarely reported with their terminal
states.} Benchmark suites in the agentic and reasoning evaluation space
are frequently multi-scope (a public split versus a private original
contract, a benign-utility scope versus an adversarial scope),
quota-limited, or gated behind private payloads. Reports tend either to
omit such suites or to collapse them into a single number, which
conceals how much of the suite was actually graded. The project treats
this as a first-class concern rather than a presentational one: a result
that is genuinely partial should be recorded as partial, with its
coverage denominator and its reopen condition, and a lane that cannot be
run at all should be recorded as externally blocked rather than quietly
dropped.

\section{Objectives}\label{objectives}

The project pursues eight objectives. They are stated as they were
pursued, not retrofitted to the results.

\textbf{O1 --- Build a single harness that runs the same experiment
across multiple back-ends.} Provide one control surface over Tinker,
TRL, SkyRL, verl and OpenRLHF paths so that a configuration can be held
fixed while the framework varies, and so that a framework difference is
measurable rather than anecdotal.

\textbf{O2 --- Instrument the training loop so that signal starvation is
directly observable.} Emit per-step telemetry carrying ZVF, gradient
utilisation, policy entropy, completion length and KL divergence, and
persist the raw per-group reward tensors so that any derived statistic
can be deterministically recomputed after the fact rather than trusted
from a log line.

\textbf{O3 --- Measure the zero-variance regime on real reward tensors
and test the levers that might move it.} Characterise ZVF empirically on
genuine GRPO reward tensors, and investigate re-baselining and
curriculum interventions as ways of converting dark compute into
contrast.

\textbf{O4 --- Establish and hold a scientific protocol.} Multi-seed
runs, matched baselines, held-out evaluation with explicit denominators,
deterministic recomputation, pre-registered effect-size and power
accounting, and honest reporting of negatives and nulls --- including
the cases where the answer is that the study was underpowered to answer.

\textbf{O5 --- Scope the most original direction in the field.} A
literature scan for white space, which in this project identified stack
provenance and reporting standards as the unclaimed ground and produced
the minimum reporting standard (P5) and the machine-readable stack
registry (P6).

\textbf{O6 --- Turn the diagnostic into an intervention.} Derive an
adaptive group-size controller from the ZVF measurement and evaluate it
as a training-time policy rather than a post-hoc observation (P7).

\textbf{O7 --- Evaluate a trained actor against held-out suites under
strict governance.} Run a single trained checkpoint against fourteen
held-out agentic and reasoning suites, each with its own native
evaluator at a pinned revision, under receipt, budget and
decontamination controls, and record every lane's terminal state.

\textbf{O8 --- Report under a deliberate asymmetry.} Where the evidence
supports a structural or descriptive claim, state it; where it does not,
withhold it. No cross-suite aggregate, no pooling of original-contract
and replacement-scope numbers, and no claim of improvement over any
baseline anywhere in the work.

\section{Scope and Non-Goals}\label{scope-and-non-goals}

The scope of this report is measurement and diagnostics, not leaderboard
performance. All training runs are on small-to-mid open models --- the
Qwen2.5, Qwen3, Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.6 families, from roughly 0.5B
parameters up to the 35B sparse mixture-of-experts configuration used
for the campaign actor. Verifiable rewards come from mathematics and
code checkers, principally GSM8K exact-match and HumanEval-style unit
tests. Held-out evaluation uses suites' own native evaluators at pinned
revisions.

Several things are explicitly out of scope. This is not an attempt to
set a state-of-the-art result on any benchmark, and no such claim is
made anywhere in the report. It is not a study of frontier reasoning
models. The held-out sets used in the controlled comparisons are
intentionally small --- 200 to 500 prompts in most cases --- and every
denominator is reported so that a reader can see how little is being
claimed. Claims about novel algorithms are not made: the
length-weighting intervention (P4) and the adaptive controller (P7) were
designed during the work and are reported as designed and simulated
systems with their evaluation limits attached, not as demonstrated
improvements.

Three evidentiary rules apply throughout and are worth stating here
because they constrain what the later chapters are allowed to say.
First, every number in this report is traceable to a repository file
that was actually read; where a figure could not be sourced, the finding
is described qualitatively and the figure is attributed to its source
rather than reconstructed. Second, original-contract and
replacement-scope benchmark results are never pooled and never averaged
across suites --- the two are different measurements of different things
under different licences, and treating them as one column would
manufacture a number that no suite reported. Third, no improvement over
a baseline or over a base model is claimed unless the source states it
with a test; where the source reports a difference without a test
supporting it, or reports an inconclusive test, that is what is written
here.

\section{Contribution of the
Candidate}\label{contribution-of-the-candidate}


\fig{fig_contribution_map}
{Boundary between the inherited Semester-3 group infrastructure and the individual Semester-4 contribution reported in this document.}
{fig:contribution}



This report covers work that spans two semesters and two very different
modes of authorship, and the distinction matters for how the
contribution should be assessed. Semester 3 was a group project (Group
6). The multi-framework repository, the baseline integrations, the
initial Tinker, SkyRL and GRPO experiments, the literature foundation,
and the early benchmark, evaluation and reproducibility scaffolding were
produced by that group, of which the candidate was a member. Semester 4
is the candidate's individual continuation, and the new research
direction, the experiment expansion, the analysis, the paper writing and
the P1--P8 outputs are the candidate's solo work (source:
platform\_hybrid/sem 4 work/EXPERIMENTS.md; source: platform\_hybrid/sem
4 work/README.md).

The boundary is recorded precisely rather than loosely. The inherited
state is frozen at the Git tag \texttt{capstone-final-2026-04-25},
corresponding to boundary commit
\texttt{21a99ef766543a1ca86bfbad188445d3596ea73c} of 23 April 2026. At
the time the separation was documented, 584 commits had been made after
that boundary, touching 5,050 changed files --- a figure the provenance
record explicitly declines to present as a measure of academic
contribution, since it counts mechanical and generated changes alongside
substantive ones. The transition to explicit solo authorship is marked
by commit \texttt{282e6b4f0f158de11b3f5bb4656e2d871f68f5c0} of 7 July
2026 (source: platform\_hybrid/sem 4 work/PROVENANCE.md).

What was added after that boundary is the substance of this report. It
includes the eight standalone research papers: P1, a cross-library and
cross-scale study of GRPO behaviour; P2, the Zero-Variance Fraction as a
descriptive diagnostic; P3, group size, contrast density and the
relationship to DPO; P4, length bias and held-out generalisation; P5,
the minimum reporting standard for stack-conditioned results; P6, a
machine-readable GRPO stack registry; P7, an adaptive group-size
controller derived from the signal-starvation diagnostic; and P8, a side
study comparing a language model against gradient-boosted trees in
credit-card fraud detection (source: platform\_hybrid/sem 4
work/README.md). It includes the post-capstone experimental, audit,
synthesis and paper iterations --- 206 recorded analysis iterations ---
the Berkeley-course-derived research audits and prototypes, the expanded
statistical analyses, and the new GRPO diagnostics and controllers
(source: platform\_hybrid/sem 4 work/README.md; source:
platform\_hybrid/sem 4 work/EXPERIMENTS.md). And it includes the E1--E14
held-out benchmark campaign, in which a single Tinker-trained actor
(base \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…}, with
the
\texttt{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
adapter at commit \texttt{64444133…}, in bfloat16) was evaluated against
fourteen suites under receipt, budget and decontamination governance.
Three of those replacement scopes are strictly complete --- LAB-Bench
public at 1,967/1,967, AgentDojo benign utility at 97/97, and
VerilogEval at 129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1 --- and Omni-MATH is 99.95\%
complete with its official accuracy of 2,271/4,428 = 51.31\% reproduced.
SWE-bench Pro carries a full verified original-contract score of 2/731 =
0.274\% pass@1. The remaining lanes are recorded honestly as partial or
externally blocked, each with a terminal state and a named reopen
condition, and the structural claims were re-verified by deterministic
code checks on 19 September 2026 with 11 of 11 checks passing (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md).

Two further contributions deserve naming because they are contributions
of restraint rather than of result. The first is that the campaign's
negative findings were kept rather than discarded. When BankerToolBench
was re-run on the base model under a separate authorisation --- 100/100
trials, 3 hours 29 minutes, \$15.07 charged --- the mean reward was 0.0
across every trial, and the audit identified the mechanism as
tool-dialogue collapse: agents terminated after 2 to 14 steps with no
deliverables, and 27 or more trajectories ended in role-token
degeneration of the form \texttt{user\ user\ assistant\ …}. That result
is reported as a measurement of the base model's inability to sustain an
open-ended tool-use loop, not as a measurement of finance reasoning, and
it is retained in the record (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). The second is the
discipline applied to the project's own claims: the same-stack audit
that invalidated the pre-audit aggregator's ``disappears'' verdict did
so on the strength of the study's own locked minimum detectable effect,
and the permitted wording it left in place is narrower than the finding
it refuted (source:
autoresearch/deli-neurips-tmlr-260802/audits/statistical\_refutation.md).

The report does not reallocate the shared runtime code. The inherited
repository remains where it was; the Semester 4 papers cite their
canonical LaTeX sources and evidence directories rather than duplicating
them, and the distinction recorded here is a distinction about academic
contribution, not about file ownership (source: platform\_hybrid/sem 4
work/EXPERIMENTS.md).

\section{Organisation of the Report}\label{organisation-of-the-report}

The report is organised in four parts.

\textbf{Part I --- Foundations} contains the present chapter, followed
by Chapter 2, the literature survey, which situates the work against the
RLHF and RLVR literature, the critic-free line of estimators, the
group-size and advantage-estimation literature, and the reproducibility
and reporting literature on which the provenance argument depends; and
Chapter 3, the system requirements specification, which states the
functional and non-functional requirements the harness, the telemetry
layer and the campaign infrastructure were built to satisfy.

\textbf{Part II --- Design and Implementation} contains Chapter 4, the
proposed methodology, which sets out the measurement design, the
statistical protocol, the evidence rules and the study-to-evidence
mapping; and Chapter 5, the implementation details, covering the
multi-framework harness, the per-step telemetry and raw tensor
persistence, the campaign launcher and its receipt and budget
governance, and the registry and validator components.

\textbf{Part III --- Results} contains four chapters. Chapter 6 reports
the core GRPO studies --- the cross-library and cross-scale scaling
work, the ZVF diagnostic, the group-size and contrast-density sweep, and
the length-bias and held-out generalisation study, corresponding to P1
through P4. Chapter 7 reports the reporting, registry and controller
work, corresponding to P5 through P7, including the minimum reporting
standard, the machine-readable stack registry, and the simulated
behaviour of the adaptive group-size controller. Chapter 8 reports the
applied fraud-detection study, corresponding to the archived P8. Chapter
9 reports the E1--E14 held-out benchmark campaign: the actor, the
governance model, the strictly completed scopes, the original-contract
scores, the incomplete and blocked lanes, the verification results and
the campaign's terminal states.

\textbf{Part IV --- Synthesis} contains Chapter 10, which draws the
cross-study synthesis, states the threats to validity candidly, gives
the conclusions the evidence will bear, and sets out future work and the
reopen conditions under which the blocked lanes could be completed.

Three appendices follow. Appendix A gives the complete run registry.
Appendix B documents reproducibility and provenance, including the
inherited-versus-individual boundary and the environment records needed
to reproduce the reported results. Appendix C gives the notation,
symbols and abbreviations used throughout.



\chapter{Literature Survey}\label{literature-survey}

This chapter surveys the prior work on which the project is built and
the gap it occupies. It revises the survey of the Phase-1 report
(source:
\texttt{platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex}),
retaining that survey's seven-part structure and updating each part with
the material the Semester 4 work added: the P1--P8 paper series and the
E1--E14 held-out evaluation campaign. Two rules of construction carry
over from Phase 1. The first concerns proportion. The contribution of
this project is overwhelmingly a contribution of measurement and
reporting practice rather than of a new training algorithm, so the
literature that establishes measurement practice receives more room here
than the literature that establishes methods. The second concerns
evidence. A figure is quoted only where a repository source actually
states it; where a source states a finding without giving a number, the
finding is described qualitatively and the chapter says so. Where the
project's own documents state the same quantity differently, the
divergence is reported rather than silently reconciled, and Section 2.5
records one such divergence in full.

\section{From RLHF and PPO to Group-Relative
Methods}\label{from-rlhf-and-ppo-to-group-relative-methods}


\fig{fig_lineage}
{Algorithmic lineage from RLHF through PPO and DPO to GRPO and its variants. Each transition removes a component or substitutes a cheaper estimator; GRPO removes the critic and replaces it with a group-relative baseline.}
{fig:lineage}



Policy-gradient post-training of language models begins with the RLHF
pipeline. Christiano et al.~(2017) established the three-stage pattern
of supervised fine-tuning, reward-model training on human pairwise
preferences, and reinforcement learning against that reward model;
Ziegler et al.~(2019) adapted it to language, collecting approximately
60,000 comparisons and introducing the per-token KL penalty against a
frozen reference policy that has been standard ever since. Stiennon et
al.~(2020) scaled the recipe to a 6.7B summarisation model with 64,832
comparisons, demonstrated transfer to an out-of-distribution
summarisation set (CNN/DailyMail), and produced the first empirical
evidence of reward over-optimisation: continued optimisation against a
frozen reward model eventually degrades the true objective. Ouyang et
al.~(2022) then built InstructGPT, whose three-stage pipeline used
roughly 40 contractors to produce about 13,000 supervised fine-tuning
prompts, about 33,000 reward-model prompts, and about 31,000 PPO
prompts. Two details of that work matter for this project. First, the
reward model was 6B rather than 175B because the larger reward model
proved unstable during training --- a reminder that the reward model is
itself a trained component with its own failure modes. Second, the
preference data were collected as rankings of K ∈ \{4, \ldots, 9\}
responses, which yields C(K, 2) pairwise comparisons from a single
annotation batch; this is precisely the within-prompt comparative
structure that group-relative methods would later exploit directly.
InstructGPT's headline results were that a 1.3B InstructGPT was
preferred to the 175B GPT-3 base model, that the 175B InstructGPT was
preferred to GPT-3 in 85\% of evaluations, that hallucination on a
closed-domain task fell from 41\% to 21\%, that toxicity was reduced by
roughly 25\%, and that TruthfulQA performance roughly doubled;
inter-annotator agreement was approximately 73\%. The same source
records four limitations --- annotation cost, reward-model
over-optimisation, the difficulty of aligning to user intent rather than
to annotator preference, and the residual capability costs of alignment
(source: \texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section\_2\_2\_rlhf.tex}).

The optimiser for that pipeline was PPO. PPO's clipping mechanism, with
ε ≈ 0.2, restricts each update's policy ratio, and generalised advantage
estimation with its standard defaults (λ = 0.95, γ = 0.99) provides the
advantage estimate. Recasting language generation as an RL problem makes
the state the prompt plus generated prefix, the action the next token,
the policy the language model, and the reward sparse and terminal. The
practical consequence is a four-model architecture --- policy,
reference, reward, and value --- whose mixed-precision memory footprint
is roughly four times that of supervised fine-tuning alone; PPO-ptx
mitigates capability regression by mixing pretraining gradients into the
update. Zheng et al.'s ``Secrets of RLHF'' study found that policy
constraints, not algorithmic choice, are the single most critical factor
in whether RLHF succeeds: without them, training exhibits reward
hacking, length explosion, and catastrophic pattern collapse (source:
\texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section\_2\_3\_ppo.tex}). That
finding is the intellectual ancestor of the zero-variance analysis in
this project, because it locates the failure mode in the constraint
structure rather than in the objective.

Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), introduced with DeepSeekMath
(Shao et al., 2024), removes the learned value network altogether. For
each prompt it samples a group of G completions, scores them with a
reward function, and standardises each completion's reward against the
group mean and standard deviation; the resulting z-score serves as the
advantage for every token of that completion. In the DeepSeekMath
configuration G = 64, β = 0.04, and ε = 0.2. Eliminating the value model
reduces the number of trained and served models from four to three and
cuts memory requirements by approximately 25\%. DeepSeekMath-RL 7B
reached 51.7\% top-1 accuracy on MATH against a 46.8\% supervised
fine-tuning baseline, 60.9\% with self-consistency over 64 samples,
GSM8K improved from 82.9\% to 88.2\%, and CMATH improved from 84.6\% to
88.8\% despite no Chinese data in the RL training set (source:
\texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section\_2\_5\_grpo.tex}). The
paper's iterative variant retains 10\% of historical data in a replay
buffer to prevent catastrophic forgetting in the co-evolving reward
model. RLOO (Ahmadian et al., 2024) is the closely related leave-one-out
estimator that baselines each sample against the mean of the
\emph{other} samples in the group; it already performs a within-group
baseline, and is therefore directly relevant to the signal-starvation
analysis this project conducts (source:
\texttt{platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex}).

DeepSeek-R1 (Guo et al., 2025) then demonstrated that pure
outcome-reward RL can elicit chain-of-thought behaviour from a base
model without supervised cold-start data. R1-Zero was trained on
DeepSeek-V3-Base, a 671B mixture-of-experts model, using rule-based
rewards only and no neural reward model. AIME 2024 pass@1 rose from
15.6\% to 71.0\% over training --- the revised \emph{Nature} version
reports 77.9\% after extended training --- with consensus decoding over
64 samples (cons@64) reaching 86.7\% and MATH-500 reaching 95.9\%. The
source states the training configuration as 16 sampled outputs per
question, a group size of 512, a learning rate of 3 × 10⁻⁶, a KL
coefficient of 0.001, and approximately 10,400 policy update steps. The
paper documents spontaneous chain-of-thought, self-verification, and the
widely reported ``aha moment'' as emergent rather than taught
behaviours. The full R1 model, trained with a four-stage pipeline that
includes a language-consistency reward, reports 79.8 on AIME, 97.3 on
MATH-500, and 71.5 on GPQA, against 79.2 / 96.4 / 75.7 for o1-1217.
Distillation into six students from 1.5B to 70B produced
Distill-Qwen-1.5B at 28.9\% AIME and 83.9\% MATH-500, and
Distill-Qwen-32B at 72.6\% and 94.3\%, against 47.0\% and 91.6\% for
Qwen2.5-32B-Zero trained from scratch; the 7B and 16B base models failed
to show meaningful reasoning gains from direct RL (source:
\texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section\_2\_6\_deepseek\_r1.tex}).

For this project, the significance of that lineage is narrower than the
headline numbers suggest. GRPO's defining operation is a within-group
standardisation, and a standardisation is undefined when the group's
rewards are all identical. That degenerate case is not an edge case: it
is the modal case early in training on a hard task and late in training
on an easy one. The rest of this chapter and the empirical chapters that
follow are concerned with what that degeneracy does to the training
signal, and with how honestly the field currently reports it.

\section{Direct Preference Optimisation as a
Contrast}\label{direct-preference-optimisation-as-a-contrast}

An orthogonal branch of post-training removes on-policy sampling
entirely. Direct Preference Optimisation (DPO; Rafailov et al., 2023)
exploits the fact that the RLHF objective with a KL constraint admits a
closed-form optimal policy, which permits the reward to be
reparameterised in terms of the policy itself. The result is a simple
classification loss over a fixed dataset of preference pairs, with no
reward model, no value model, and no sampling loop. Reported savings are
40--60\% in both GPU memory and wall-clock time relative to PPO. Four
limitations are documented in the same source: the training data are
static and offline, so there is no online exploration; the loss can
displace likelihood on responses outside the preference pairs; and the
method has weaker guarantees for long-horizon alignment, where credit
must propagate across many steps (source:
\texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section\_2\_4\_dpo.tex}).

The contrast is structurally instructive for this project. DPO cannot
suffer zero-variance collapse, because it never standardises within a
sampled group --- its contrast is supplied by the dataset rather than
constructed at training time. But it equally cannot exploit a verifier
that is available in the loop, which is the property that makes RLVR
attractive on mathematics. GRPO's zero-variance pathology is therefore
the price of its on-policy, verifier-in-the-loop design, and measuring
that price is exactly what makes the pathology worth quantifying. The
DPO family has since diversified into inverse-preference and non-paired
variants --- IPO, KTO, ORPO, SimPO, and SLiC among them --- which the
project's cross-library protocol treats as no-rollout baselines
(sources: \texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section\_2\_4\_dpo.tex};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}).

The boundary between the two families is also less sharp than it first
appears. Wu et al.~prove that GRPO is ``secretly DPO'' --- the
group-level advantage constitutes an implicit contrastive objective ---
and report that 2-GRPO retains 98.1\% of the performance of 16-GRPO at
12.5\% of the rollout budget (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}). That
reduction is the theoretical counterpart of the PCD statistic this
project develops: if a group is exactly as informative as the
correct/incorrect pairs it contains, then the pairwise-contrast density
PCD = ((G − 1)/G) · E{[}p(1 − p){]} is the natural magnitude-aware
summary of a group's usable signal, and the group-size question becomes
a question about how much contrast a given sampling budget buys (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_intro.tex}). The present
project is careful about what it inherits from that argument: the
reduction is cited as prior theory, while the empirical claims resting
on it are scoped to this project's own measurements, reported in Chapter
6.

\section{Fixing GRPO's Biases and
Collapse}\label{fixing-grpos-biases-and-collapse}

A dense and fast-growing literature targets specific GRPO pathologies.
Dr.~GRPO (Tong et al., 2025) removes two normalisation terms from the
standard objective --- the per-response 1/L factor and the
question-difficulty amplification --- restoring the sequence-level
gradient up to a global scale, and is motivated explicitly as a fix for
reward hacking through length (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_intro.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_intro.tex}). DAPO (Yu et
al., 2025, from ByteDance) changes four things in one release:
asymmetric Clip-Higher clipping, dynamic sampling that resamples until
the batch carries signal, token-level loss normalisation, and over-long
filtering; the reported result is 50 points on AIME 2024 with a 32B
model (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}). GSPO
(Qwen team, 2025) moves the importance ratio from the token level to the
sequence level and was credited with stabilising the Qwen3 series,
particularly its mixture-of-experts variants. Production-side variants
include VAPO and Dr.~SAC, the latter arguing that carefully implemented
REINFORCE with a value baseline matches GRPO on several RLHF tasks
(source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}).

A third thread attacks the zero-variance failure mode directly, and this
is the thread the project's own diagnostic sits in. GRESO pre-screens
prompts before sampling so that uninformative prompts are never paid
for. CPPO prunes completions after rollout, reporting up to 7.98×
speedup on GSM8K. NGRPO adds advantage calibration and asymmetric
clipping so that all-incorrect groups still carry gradient. Scaf-GRPO
injects tiered scaffolding hints when learning stagnates, reporting a
44.3\% relative improvement on AIME 2024. MO-GRPO addresses vanilla
GRPO's collapse to a single reward component under multiple objectives
through variance-based reward re-weighting. SSPO operates at sentence
granularity to balance GSPO's stability against token-level GRPO's
expressiveness, improving by 3.56 points on mathematics benchmarks. AERO
/ RL-ZVP takes the opposite approach to filtering: it extracts signal
\emph{from} zero-variance groups through entropy-guided advantage
shaping (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_intro.tex}). GVPO re-weights
by within-group variance (source:
\texttt{platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex}).
The tail continues with λ-GRPO, MC-GRPO (median-centred advantage),
G²RPO, ConSPO, and Goldilocks RL.

One act of bookkeeping in this literature deserves emphasis, because it
is a claim about what may \emph{not} be inferred. AVSPO, query
recycling, ConSPO, and Goldilocks RL are described in the project's own
related-work source as prior or concurrent interventions, not as
evidence for this project's runner, and the paper explicitly claims
neither priority nor an intervention effect over them (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}). The
present thesis inherits that disclaimer without qualification.

Against this background, the project's own contribution to the area is
deliberately meagre in algorithmic ambition and deliberately demanding
in evidential standard. The Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) --- the
fraction of groups whose completions are all correct or all incorrect
--- was measured across libraries, model scales, and training phases in
the companion ZVF paper, and that paper stopped at description: ZVF
tracks collapse, drifts upward, and is mechanically entangled with
accuracy and group size, so it declined to promote ZVF to a predictive
or causal statistic (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_intro.tex}). A minimal
binomial model of group degeneracy was then proposed, in which the
expected ZVF indicator is h\_G(p) = p\^{}G + (1 − p)\^{}G and the usable
signal is S = p(1 − p)(1 − h\_G(p)); three regularities follow in closed
form, each paired with an empirical check drawn from a 368-run Weights
\& Biases audit that counts all logged runs and is therefore a superset
of the benchmark's 70+ core training runs. Because h\_G(p) is symmetric
under p ↔ 1 − p, ZVF assigns the same value to a mastered prompt and to
a hopeless one, producing the U-shape measured across seven models; and
because ZVF is an existence statistic, adding reward jitter of order
10⁻⁴ drives measured ZVF to zero while shifting the measured
within-group contrast by less than 10⁻⁶. The pairwise-contrast density
is proposed as the magnitude-aware replacement, and the toy-scale
validation reports a gradient-norm correlation of corr = +0.71, marked
directional only. The word \emph{controller} in that work names the
implemented policy, not an established improvement: the decisive
static-G = 16 matched-token bakeoff remains unrun (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_intro.tex}).

The one genuinely measured GRPO-variant comparison in the repository is
a Dr.~GRPO versus GRPO control. On Qwen2.5-0.5B at group size 8, 40
steps, and 5 seeds, held-out accuracy was indistinguishable between
arms: GRPO 0.987 ± 0.003 against Dr.~GRPO 0.992 ± 0.003, paired
difference +0.5 percentage points, t = 1.05, df = 4, p = 0.35; mean
completion length was 4.74 against 4.71 tokens. In a second regime ---
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct on GSM8K with a 200-token chain-of-thought cap and
3 seeds --- GRPO moved from 20.2\% to 26.3\% and Dr.~GRPO from 20.5\% to
25.5\%; per-seed McNemar tests were significant in 2 of 3 seeds for GRPO
(p = 0.071, 0.036, 0.016) and 1 of 3 for Dr.~GRPO (p = 0.122, 0.017,
0.122), and the pooled p \textless{} 10⁻³ is expressly rejected as
overstating significance. Neither arm inflated length over 30 steps
(GRPO 195 → 180 tokens; Dr.~GRPO 194 → 187). The same source contains
head-to-head and rank-correlation tables whose values are
\emph{projections}, not measurements: the GRPO baseline row is a
synthetic baseline produced by a dry run of
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/variance\_mitigation\_integration.py},
the file states that it makes no measurement claim for any row of that
table, the correlation coefficients (0.78 / 0.66 / 0.63 / 0.31) carry
the same caveat, and the configuration proxies under
\texttt{platform\_local/unified/} are explicitly not faithful
reimplementations. Those numbers are reported here only to mark them as
unusable as evidence (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/variance\_mitigation\_comparison.tex};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/drgrpo\_vs\_grpo.json};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/drgrpo\_gsm8k\_cot.json}).
The nearest thing to a headline cross-library effect in the project is
also the one that must be read most carefully: holding the visible group
size, learning rate, data, seed, and label fixed while changing stacks
moved final GSM8K reward from 85.6\% to 5.0\%, a 17× swing; the audit
that followed found the managed stack had also pinned a different base
checkpoint, so the source describes this as an under-specification
exhibit rather than a backend-only causal effect (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_intro.tex}). The
complementary telemetry fact is that running ``DAPO'' on an open Colab
trainer whose dynamic sampling demonstrably filters zero-variance groups
produced mean ZVF 0.00, while running ``DAPO'' on a closed managed stack
where only an asymmetric-clip surrogate is user-settable produced mean
ZVF 0.58 --- the same label, opposite telemetry (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_intro.tex}).

\section{Verifiable Rewards, Frameworks and Reporting
Practice}\label{verifiable-rewards-frameworks-and-reporting-practice}

RLVR --- reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards --- was
formalised for open post-training in TÜLU 3 (Lambert et al., 2024),
which is the source this project follows in treating a programmatic
checker rather than a learned preference model as the reward (source:
\texttt{platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex}).
The implementation landscape the project unifies is crowded and
heterogeneous. On the training side it includes TRL, OpenRLHF, veRL
(HybridFlow), NeMo-RL, DeepSpeed-Chat, trlX, Axolotl, and Tinker as a
managed back-end; each makes different default choices about advantage
normalisation, KL estimators, tokenisation loss masking, rollout
batching, and importance-sampling granularity, and the project
identifies those differences as the principal source of the
cross-library gap it quantifies. On the inference side, vLLM and SGLang
supply the rollout backends on which modern frameworks depend, with
FlashAttention and LoRA as load-bearing kernels (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex};
\texttt{platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex}).
Tinker is adopted here through its \texttt{message\_with\_tool}
interface as the neutral substrate for tool-use extensions, and the same
source explicitly cites tool-use and agentic RL settings as concurrent
environments in which this project's ZVF measurements will need to be
re-evaluated rather than assumed to transfer (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}).

The evaluation benchmarks are equally well established and equally
awkward. GSM8K comprises 8,500 problems split 7,473 / 1,319, on which a
fine-tuned GPT-3 verifier reached 35\% at release while current systems
exceed 95\%; GSM8K-Platinum has since documented label noise in the
original set, which is why the source describes the benchmark as
necessary but not sufficient. MATH comprises 12,500 problems across
seven subjects and five difficulty levels, on which the original results
reached 6.9\%; MATH-500 is the 500-problem subset introduced with the
Lightman process-supervision work, on which current systems sit at
roughly 99\%. AIME comprises 15 problems per year with integer answers
in 0--999; reported AIME 2024 accuracies include GPT-5 at 95.7\% and
Grok 4 at 94.3\%. Because these benchmarks are close to saturation, the
source frames chain-of-thought as the action space that makes them
interesting at all, and identifies process reward models as the bridge
from terminal outcome signals to step-level credit (source:
\texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section\_2\_8\_mathematical\_reasoning\_benchmarks.tex}).

Reporting practice is where the project's own contribution is anchored,
and its lineage is explicit. Model Cards (Mitchell et al., 2019)
introduced documentation of intended use, out-of-scope use, and
per-slice evaluation results; Datasheets for Datasets (Gebru et al.,
2021) extended the idea to datasets, covering composition,
preprocessing, annotation process, and discriminatory-impact mitigation;
Data Statements (Bender and Friedman, 2018) added the
speaker-demographic axis; and Data Cards (Pushkarna et al., 2022)
bridged research and industry practice with schema, provenance,
sampling-distribution, and audit-transparency fields. A cross-coupling
audit at iteration 109 maps these four works onto the project's legacy
18-field experimental manifest, showing that they cover 12 distinct
fields, that the remaining six are either RL-specific (per-step ZVF,
group-size schedule) or stack-coverage items with no direct analogue in
the prior literature, and that items 9--12 --- annotation process and
annotator demographics --- have the lowest evidence density in the live
manifests. The same iteration verified all four entries against CrossRef
metadata (4/4 pass on year, five-gram title, and author-family overlap ≥
0.6) and retrofitted two entries that had been present in the
bibliography but never cited (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_related.tex};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_iter109\_minreport\_coupling.tsv};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_iter109\_crossref\_verify.tsv};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_iter109\_bib\_audit.tsv}).
The resulting standard, MIN-REPORT-RL, is an eight-item list aligned at
eight items on 2026-07-11, whose eighth item (held-out pass@k) is an
evaluation-report field rather than run-start stack identity (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_related.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_intro.tex}).

The reproducibility literature to which this standard is a deliberate
echo is older and better established. Henderson et al.~(2018) showed
that deep-RL results depend on codebase, hyperparameters, and even the
choice of random seeds, to the point that the same algorithm from two
repositories behaves as two algorithms. Colas et al.~(2019) and Agarwal
et al.~(2021, \texttt{rliable}) supplied the statistical repair kit ---
rigorous comparison protocols and small-sample-aware aggregate metrics
with interquartile-mean reporting and performance profiles. Jordan et
al.~(2024) argued that most published RL comparisons lack statistical
power. Dodge et al.~(2019) proposed reporting expected validation
performance as a function of compute budget; the project's run manifest
is the RL-for-LLM analogue, replacing \emph{budget} with \emph{stack}.
Pineau et al.~(2020) formalised the NeurIPS reproducibility criteria,
and the project draws the explicit lesson that self-attested checklists
have limits, which motivates an artifact-computed badge rather than a
declared one (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_related.tex};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}).
Statistical treatments of evaluation noise (Miller et al.; Zhang et al.)
quantify the uncertainty floor against which the project's own
within-noise findings should be read, and the evaluation-stack
sensitivity documented by the LM Evaluation Harness effort, by audits of
reasoning evaluations, and by contamination studies of the data side of
the same coin, together establish that RL \emph{training} inherits all
of this --- the verifier is an evaluator in the loop --- plus the
additional vectors unique to on-policy optimisation (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_related.tex}).

Three existing resources come closest to the project's registry and are
the reason it exists in its present form. HELM aggregates evaluation
across a large model and scenario matrix, but its unit is the (model,
scenario) cell, not the training stack. The LM Evaluation Harness
standardises evaluation by \emph{executing} implementations, whereas the
registry here standardises by \emph{describing} stacks it cannot execute
--- many of them closed --- which is precisely why the project required
a null-versus-absent convention and an R5 rung that records
un-auditability rather than converting it into a false value. Papers
with Code catalogued paper--code--leaderboard linkage at scale, stopped
at the repository boundary, and was discontinued in July 2025; the
source treats its fate as a governance cautionary tale. The project's
own claim to novelty is narrow and stated negatively: no existing
resource provides the executed loss form at GRPO-variant granularity,
per-component implementation status, or an unauditability marker
(sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_related\_resources.tex};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_intro.tex}).

\section{Scaling and Layer-Adaptation
Context}\label{scaling-and-layer-adaptation-context}

Compute-optimal pretraining is governed by well-characterised scaling
laws (Kaplan et al.; Hoffmann et al., Chinchilla), but RL post-training
is much less well characterised, and the sources the project builds on
separate cleanly into those that predict and those that describe. Hilton
et al.~(2023) derived compute-efficiency frontiers for RL in games and
robotics. For RLHF, Gao et al.~(2023) characterised the
over-optimisation curve of reward models --- the point at which
additional optimisation against a frozen reward model buys a decreasing
true objective --- and Rafailov et al.~(2024) provided scaling laws for
DPO and PPO that show a strong dependence on reward-model quality.
Nimmaturi et al.~(2025) derived an empirical scaling law specifically
for GRPO, identifying three training phases (slow start, rapid
improvement, plateau) and reporting that most of the benefit is
exhausted by roughly 80\% of a single epoch. Tan et al.~(2025) treat
group size as a budget knob in an empirical scaling study of GRPO
post-training. Snell et al.~treat inference-time compute as a scaling
axis in its own right, and the project's own related-work source cites
these as the works against which its measurements should be read
(source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}).

The project's scaling study covers 70+ runs across seven RL libraries
and five model families. Its own documents do not agree on the upper end
of the model-range: the P1 paper's introduction states the range as
0.6B--1T, while the P2, P3, and P4 introductions state
0.6B--\textasciitilde671B, and the related-work source states the
cross-family data as spanning 0.6B to 235B across five families and four
frameworks (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_intro.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p2\_intro.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p3\_intro.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_intro.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}). This
divergence is a reporting defect in the portfolio's own documents and is
recorded here rather than harmonised, because harmonising it would mean
asserting a range that no single source states. The principal empirical
result of the scaling work is a null: across that range, no reliable
scaling law is recovered from reward curves (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_synthesis\_scaling.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_conclusion.tex}). The
companion group-size study adds a second bounded negative: a single-seed
sweep does not establish an optimum, trainability is non-monotone in
group size, and the measured signal-to-noise ratio grows at only about
52\% of the √G ideal, with G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\} measured and G ∈ \{4, 8,
16, 32, 64\} explicitly reconstructed rather than run.

Layer adaptation supplies the second half of this section's context, and
connects the project's white-box investigation to the
parameter-efficient fine-tuning literature. LoRA (Hu et al., 2022)
freezes the pretrained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices
into the attention projections, achieving a 128× reduction in trainable
parameters at d = 4096 with rank r = 16, which is on the order of 0.1\%
of the model's parameters. QLoRA (Dettmers et al., 2023) extends this by
quantising the frozen base to NF4 with double quantisation at 0.37 bits
per parameter --- approximately 3 GB for a 65B model, permitting 65B
fine-tuning on a single 48 GB GPU. The motivating arithmetic is that
Adam at mixed precision costs roughly 16N bytes, exceeding 100 GB for a
7B model. Prefix tuning, prompt tuning, and adapter layers are the
earlier members of the same family, and LoRA's advantages for RL
specifically are that the frozen base doubles as the reference policy
and that the trainable parameter count is small enough for modest
hardware (source:
\texttt{capstone-literature-survey/section2\_7\_peft.tex}). The
project's layer-adaptation pillar asks a narrower and orthogonal
question: where inside the network GRPO-style adaptation concentrates,
and whether the layers that will matter can be identified early enough
to freeze the rest. The reported finding is moderate concentration of
per-layer adaptation, with the important negative that step-1
predictability does \emph{not} survive scaling. This connects directly
to Liu et al.~(2025), who found that RL fine-tuning updates only 5--30\%
of model parameters across seven algorithms and ten model families,
suggesting that RL activates a sparse subnetwork (sources:
\texttt{platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}). The
length-bias pillar completes the scaling picture with its own bounded
null: under a 200-token GSM8K generation cap, GRPO and Dr.~GRPO produce
held-out gains that are indistinguishable at the project's seed budget,
and neither arm shows length inflation; the source is careful to state
that the 200-token cap is part of the estimand rather than a nuisance
detail, so the null bounds the capped, short-horizon regime and cannot
adjudicate uncapped reasoning or long agent trajectories (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_intro.tex}).

\section{Process-, Tree- and Stability-Aware
Variants}\label{process--tree--and-stability-aware-variants}

Beyond the group-baseline fixes, a parallel line of work changes
\emph{what} signal drives the update. Process reward models supply
denser, step-level feedback instead of a single terminal reward, which
reduces the chance that a whole group collapses to identical terminal
rewards. Let's Verify Step by Step (Lightman et al., 2023) showed that
step-level PRMs substantially outperform outcome reward models on GSM8K
and MATH; Math-Shepherd derives process rewards from automatically
generated process annotations, avoiding costly human labels; PRM800K and
PRMBench supply step-level evaluation data; OmegaPRM uses Monte-Carlo
tree search to supervise process rewards without human-in-the-loop;
ReasonEval introduces reasoning-specific reward models; and Implicit PRM
learns process rewards implicitly from outcome labels, with VersaPRM
extending process rewards to multi-domain tasks. Tree-of-thought search,
self-consistency, and critic-style post-hoc verifiers all either benefit
from or substitute for process supervision (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}). The
same source documents the counter-current that matters most here:
VerifyBench and ``A Sober Look'' warn that reward hacking and evaluation
artefacts can inflate perceived gains on reasoning benchmarks, which is
the project's stated motivation for insisting on binary verifiable
rewards in its own mathematics work.

Stability-aware variants constrain the update itself. GSPO's
sequence-level importance ratio, discussed above, is the clearest
instance, credited with stabilising the Qwen3 mixture-of-experts series.
SSPO's sentence-granularity objective is explicitly framed as balancing
GSPO's stability against token-level GRPO's expressiveness.
Entropy-guided advantage shaping in AERO / RL-ZVP regulates the update
when the group carries no reward contrast, and GRESO's pre-screening is
a sampling-side stability control. On the reasoning-RL side, GRPO-Lead,
LCPO, and LIMR tune reasoning depth through length-aware rewards,
Search-R1 trains reasoning agents to interleave search with
chain-of-thought, and TTRL performs test-time RL against self-generated
verifiers (source:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/related\_work\_v2.tex}).

The Phase-1 survey's judgement on this body of work is retained without
amendment, because the Semester 4 evidence has not changed it. These
methods propose \emph{levers}; this project first builds the
\emph{instrumentation} needed to tell whether any such lever converts
wasted rollouts into held-out accuracy --- ZVF, group utilisation,
pairwise-contrast density, and the collapse decomposition. The nulls
reported in the results chapters are exactly the kind of evidence that
instrumentation is designed to produce, and they constitute a standing
caution against adopting a variant on the strength of a single-seed
improvement. The project applies that caution to its own proposals: the
adaptive group-size controller is described as an implemented policy
rather than an established improvement, and its decisive matched-token
comparison remains unrun (sources:
\texttt{platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex};
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_intro.tex}).

of what is claimed and, more importantly, what is not. Table 2.1
reproduces the Phase-1 positioning and updates it with the Semester 4
evidence.
