historical E11 receipt, adds 2,507 decoded nested API-Bank strings to
reach 26,240 source documents, and records 43,977,748 bytes downloaded
against a 500 MiB cap, with the checker not having enumerated the
roughly 2.58 billion possible document pairs (source:
outputs/public\_portfolio\_2026-09-05/decontamination/README.md).

Two features of the campaign's execution are methodological rather than
incidental. The first is that a lane stops when its receipts are
incomplete, and the record says why. A single inventory pass on
2026-09-21 checked every blocked lane live and found concrete, named
failures: E1 lacked W\&B authentication, a sealed reservation and a live
actor endpoint; E2 awaited an owner IAM binding that only the project
owner can create; E5 lacked both a bound authorization receipt and any
real native runtime adapter in the repository; E13's request remained a
draft with launch authorisation false; E6 and E9 were refused by cloud
quota in both regions (source:
outputs/UNBLOCK\_CARRYOUT\_2026-09-21.md). Recording a lane as
blocked-with-reason, rather than reporting a shortened run, is the same
principle as the terminal-state taxonomy applied to execution. The
second is that the campaign's largest lane is reported as a negative
result. E1 on SWE-bench Pro produced 2/731 resolutions, or 0.274\%
pass@1, with 731 terminal generations, 713 native evaluations, and 14
generation failures and 4 artifact losses retained in the denominator
rather than excluded (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). A separate authorized
rerun of the base model on the E4 finance suite completed 100 of 100
trials with a mean reward of 0.0, and the audit of all one hundred
trajectories attributed the result to a mechanism rather than to
reasoning: agents terminate after two to fourteen steps with a median of
about six and no deliverables, so the verifier short-circuits to zero,
and 27 or more trajectories end in role-token degeneration; the source's
own reading is that this zero measures tool-dialogue collapse in the
base model and not finance reasoning (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). Reporting those
outcomes as findings about the actor, with the mechanism identified, is
the behaviour the whole apparatus is designed to produce.

The campaign also carries its integrity accounting in the same register.
Eleven of eleven deterministic checks passed on 2026-09-19, covering
exact divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash equality and
lost-source absence probes; the recorded spend was \$5.6301 against the
\$50 additional cap counted at that date, with later budget-mode records
moving to an unlimited mode whose per-run resource and timeout bounds
remain in force (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). A judgment layer was
added for classification, faithfulness and value-ordering questions and
then partly invalidated by an external service outage: both
blocker-classification batteries returned degenerate distributions after
the incident, their receipts are retained as incident evidence, and the
per-lane blocker column therefore rests on document evidence and not on
model judgment (source: outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md).
A design rule was banked from the healthy window and is followed in this
thesis: arithmetic and string lookups stay in code, and model judgment
is reserved for classification, faithfulness and value ordering, because
a parity probe was answered incorrectly even while the service was
healthy (source: outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md).

What the campaign does not claim follows directly from the design. It
does not claim improvement over any baseline anywhere, because no lane
carries a test establishing it: the campaign's role in this thesis is to
fix the evaluation protocol, the receipt discipline and the disposition
taxonomy that any future improvement claim would have to satisfy. It
does not claim that the fourteen suites constitute a portfolio result.
It does not pool original-contract with replacement-scope figures, and
it does not average across suites. Its one trained actor means it says
nothing about model comparisons. And where the record is incomplete ---
a lost execution source, an unanswered external access request, a run
whose budget exceeded the authorised envelope --- the incompleteness is
written into the ledger rather than resolved by inference.



\chapter{Implementation Details}\label{implementation-details}


\fig{fig_receipt_pipeline}
{The evidence chain from sealed request through execution and native grading to a deterministic ledger check. A break anywhere in the chain means no number is reported for that lane.}
{fig:receipts}



This chapter describes what was built. Its organising claim is that the
engineering contribution of the project is a \textbf{dispatch and
evidence layer} rather than a training algorithm: the algorithmic
content of GRPO is inherited from the literature, but the work of making
six reinforcement-learning frameworks and six compute backends
addressable through one Tinker-style interface --- and of making every
number that comes out of them traceable to a sealed, hash-pinned
artefact --- is where the implementation effort sits. The chapter
proceeds from the abstraction (the adapter architecture), through the
concrete training loop, to the two engineering episodes that most shaped
the codebase (the parallel logging defect and the Colab white-box path),
and finally to the evaluation harness and the recovery
re-implementations undertaken after an execution-source loss in the
final phase of the project.

\section{Two Rosters, One Dispatch
Surface}\label{two-rosters-one-dispatch-surface}

A reader approaching the project's comparison material will encounter
two different framework rosters, and conflating them is the single most
likely misreading of the implementation. The paper's methods note states
the distinction explicitly: the cross-launcher LLM-RL scaffold
referenced in the framework-gap discussion is a seven-library roster ---
Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, veRL, OpenRLHF, Atropos, and HF reference launchers
--- that targets LLM post-training launchers specifically, while the
cross-RL-library evidence reported elsewhere uses a different roster.
Critically for this chapter, the same note records that ``only Tinker
and TRL produced completed runs in this release; veRL and OpenRLHF
entries in the \texttt{framework\_comparison.json} artefact are dry-run
placeholders'' (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/\_shared\_methods.tex). The veRL and
OpenRLHF entries in that artefact carry peak and last-ten-average reward
values, but they are the output of the harness resolving a launch plan,
not the output of a training run, and they must never be pooled with
real results or read as framework comparison evidence.

Atropos occupies a different position again. Rather than being a cell in
the dispatch matrix, it is integrated through a dedicated package under
\texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos/}, a \texttt{tinker\_atropos} module
containing \texttt{config.py}, \texttt{api.py}, \texttt{trainer.py},
\texttt{types.py}, \texttt{dataset.py}, \texttt{stats\_utils.py}, and an
\texttt{environments/} directory with per-task environment servers
(source: platform\_tinker/atropos/). Its integration model is the
Atropos managed-server pattern: a three-terminal workflow in which
\texttt{run-api} starts the Atropos API,
\texttt{launch\_training.py\ -\/-config\ configs/default.yaml} starts
Tinker training against a \texttt{TINKER\_API\_KEY}, and an environment
server --- a built-in one or any external Atropos environment pointed at
the same config file --- serves rollouts. The README's quickstart
example runs a fifty-step training run on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct against
a GSM8K environment, and the environment contract is that the
\texttt{env} and \texttt{openai} sections are consumed by the
environment server while the \texttt{tinker} section is consumed by the
trainer and ignored by the environment (source:
platform\_tinker/atropos/README.md). This separation is what allows an
arbitrary existing Atropos environment to be reused against the Tinker
backend without modification to the environment.

\section{The Adapter Architecture: What versus
Where}\label{the-adapter-architecture-what-versus-where}


\fig{fig_architecture}
{Six reinforcement-learning backends behind one Tinker-style API. A single task, reward and decoding configuration drives any backend, so differences in outcome are attributable to the stack.}
{fig:arch}



The dispatch surface is built on one orthogonal decomposition, stated in
the backends module docstring: ``A \texttt{Backend} knows \textbf{where}
to run one experiment cell (local GPU, Modal, Colab, vast.ai, GCP, HF
Spaces); the framework dimension (trl/tinker/verl/openrlhf/skyrl) is the
\textbf{what}. \texttt{plan()} resolves a cell into a concrete
\texttt{LaunchPlan} without spending compute, so \texttt{-\/-dry-run}
and the matrix test work uniformly'' (source:
platform\_local/unified/backends/base.py). The \texttt{LaunchPlan}
dataclass carries \texttt{backend}, \texttt{framework},
\texttt{command}, \texttt{driver\_file}, \texttt{output}, \texttt{env},
and \texttt{notes}, with a \texttt{format()} method for printing; the
default \texttt{\_execute} runs
\texttt{subprocess.run(plan.command,\ shell=True,\ check=True)}. Because
plan resolution is separated from execution, the entire matrix can be
validated without a GPU, an API key, or a dollar of spend --- a property
that the dry-run placeholders discussed above depend on, and which is
also the reason those placeholders must be labelled as such.

The matrix itself ships as data.
\texttt{platform\_local/unified/matrix.json} declares schema
\texttt{tinkerrl-framework-backend-matrix-v1} with thirty cells --- five
frameworks (trl, tinker, verl, openrlhf, skyrl) crossed with six
backends (local, modal, colab, vast, gcp, hfspaces), five cells per row
and per column --- and a \texttt{canonical\_experiment} block that pins
the single configuration every cell is meant to instantiate:
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B} on GSM8K with GRPO, thirty training steps,
\texttt{num\_generations} 8, per-device batch size 2, gradient
accumulation 8, learning rate \texttt{1e-06}, LoRA rank 16 and alpha 32
over all linear modules, \texttt{beta} 0.0, \texttt{epsilon} 0.2,
maximum completion length 1024, and seed 211 (source:
platform\_local/unified/matrix.json). Each cell is a concrete plan; a
representative one reads
\texttt{\{"framework":"trl","backend":"local","command":"python\ -m\ platform\_local.unified\ -\/-backend\ local\ -\/-framework\ trl\ -\/-model\ Qwen/Qwen3-8B","driver\_file":"platform\_local/trl\_integrations/trainer.py","output":"./checkpoints/","env":{[}{]},"notes":"in-process\ framework\ dispatch"\}}.

The launcher implements the dispatch. \texttt{UnifiedLauncher} holds
three registries: \texttt{FRAMEWORKS} mapping \texttt{skyrl},
\texttt{tinker}, \texttt{verl}, \texttt{openrlhf}, and \texttt{trl} to
human-readable labels; \texttt{BACKENDS} mapping \texttt{local},
\texttt{modal}, \texttt{colab}, \texttt{vast}, \texttt{gcp}, and
\texttt{hfspaces} to labels; and \texttt{ALGORITHMS} covering
\texttt{grpo}, \texttt{ppo}, \texttt{reinforce}, \texttt{dapo}, and
\texttt{dpo} (source: platform\_local/unified/launcher.py). Dispatch is
outer-on-backend and inner-on-framework, which is why the per-framework
handlers can each be written against the framework's own idioms.
\texttt{\_run\_tinker} shells to
\texttt{python\ -m\ platform\_tinker.tinkerrl.grpo\_cli\ -\/-preset\ \textless{}spec.task\textgreater{}};
\texttt{\_run\_openrlhf} delegates to
\texttt{platform\_modal/openrlhf/trainer.py:run\_openrlhf\_training(\{\},\ output\_dir="./checkpoints/openrlhf")},
which drives
\texttt{openrlhf.cli.train\_ppo\_ray\ -\/-advantage\_estimator\ group\_norm};
\texttt{\_run\_trl} generates a resumable GRPO training script through
\texttt{trl\_integrations.trainer.generate\_trl\_train\_script} into a
temporary directory and executes it; \texttt{\_run\_verl} imports
\texttt{verl.config} and \texttt{verl.trainer} directly; and
\texttt{\_run\_skyrl} requires a \texttt{SKYRL\_CHECKOUT} environment
variable and emits
\texttt{uv\ run\ -\/-extra\ gpu\ -\/-extra\ tinker\ -m\ skyrl.tinker.api}
(source: platform\_local/unified/launcher.py). For the \texttt{local}
and \texttt{colab} backends the launcher runs
\texttt{dispatch\_framework()} in-process, a path the code comments
identify as the one that breaks the Colab entry's self-recursion --- an
implementation detail worth recording because it indicates that the
backend and framework dimensions are not perfectly orthogonal in
practice, and the launcher carries framework-specific knowledge
precisely at the points where they intersect.

Fairness across frameworks is addressed by configuration auditing rather
than by assertion. The
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/framework\_config\_dumps/}
supplement ships one exact hyperparameter dump per framework for the
canonical Qwen3-8B + GSM8K matched-configuration run, and the
accompanying README recomputes the field partition from those dumps: of
44 tracked leaf fields, 25 are identical across all four frameworks
(LoRA rank, alpha, target modules and dropout; GRPO group size, rollouts
per group and max new tokens; sampling temperature, top-p, top-k and
do-sample; optimizer name, learning rate, weight decay, betas and
epsilon; reward type; and the task, seed and source-git-sha metadata),
while 11 are Tinker-managed --- serialised as
\texttt{null\ \ \#\ managed\_by\_tinker} in the Tinker dump --- covering
\texttt{kl.beta}, \texttt{kl.surrogate}, importance-sampling
granularity, reference-model offload and recompute, tokens per optimizer
step, reward broadcast precision, gradient checkpointing, micro-batch
size, gradient accumulation, and tensor-parallel size (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/framework\_config\_dumps/README.md). The
same README records two disclaimers that matter more than the identical
count: \texttt{model} and \texttt{model\_sha256} are \emph{not}
identical, because the Tinker run uses \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base} while
TRL, veRL and OpenRLHF use the instruction-tuned \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B},
and \texttt{model\_sha256} is a placeholder rather than a verified
checksum. Remaining fields are framework-specific and documented rather
than harmonised --- KL beta is 0.04 for TRL and veRL against 0.02 for
OpenRLHF, importance-sampling granularity is token-level for TRL and
veRL against sequence-level for OpenRLHF, and reference-model placement
differs across all four (\texttt{on\_device}, \texttt{managed},
\texttt{separate\_process}, and \texttt{sharded\_ray}). The appendix's
original ``byte-identical'' framing is retracted on the strength of this
partition; the honest statement is that a majority of tracked fields are
matched, a known Base-versus-Instruct confound survives, and eleven
fields are not under user control at the Tinker API level at all.

\section{The Tinker GRPO Loop}\label{the-tinker-grpo-loop}

The reference implementation of the training loop lives in
\texttt{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py} and is exposed through
\texttt{grpo\_cli.py}. The loop follows the standard four-stage shape
recorded in the Phase-1 report under ``The Tinker GRPO Loop'': sample a
group of \texttt{G} completions per prompt, compute group-relative
advantages, form the loss, and take an optimiser step (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).
Advantages are normalised within the group to mean zero and unit
standard deviation, and the loss is the clipped surrogate with a KL
penalty to a frozen reference policy,

\texttt{L(θ)\ =\ -E{[}min(ρ\ A,\ clip(ρ,\ 1-ε,\ 1+ε)\ A){]}\ +\ β\ KL(π\_θ\ ‖\ π\_ref)}

where \texttt{ρ} is the importance ratio and \texttt{ε} the clipping
range. The implementation uses the Tinker SDK's \texttt{ServiceClient}
with \texttt{create\_lora\_training\_client},
\texttt{forward\_backward\_custom} with
\texttt{loss\_type\_input="logprobs"} so that per-token
log-probabilities flow into a custom loss, \texttt{optim\_step} with
\texttt{AdamParams}, and \texttt{sample()} for rollouts; resumption uses
\texttt{create\_training\_client\_from\_state\_with\_optimizer} (source:
platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo\_cli.py).

The single most consequential property of this loop for the project's
research questions is the degenerate case. When the group's reward
standard deviation \texttt{σ\_g} is zero, every advantage \texttt{A\_i}
is zero, the surrogate term vanishes, and only the KL term survives ---
so the step is a pure pull toward the reference policy with no learning
signal at all. This is the mechanism behind the zero-variance-fraction
diagnostic that runs through the project; it is recorded in the Phase-1
report as the observation that motivated the collapse analysis, and it
is the reason \texttt{zero\_loss} and \texttt{zero\_reward} are
first-class outputs of the training CLI rather than derived quantities.
Zero reward is counted as an average reward equal to zero; zero loss is
counted as a step loss below \texttt{1e-6} (source:
platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo\_cli.py).

The CLI exposes eight presets --- \texttt{tooluse\_synth},
\texttt{tooluse\_baseline}, \texttt{tooluse\_heldout},
\texttt{tooluse\_xlam}, \texttt{gsm8k}, \texttt{math100},
\texttt{pavlov\_xlam}, and \texttt{pavlov\_portfolio} --- each resolving
a dataset factory and a reward function through a
\texttt{DATASET\_FACTORIES} registry and a \texttt{REWARD\_MAP}
(\texttt{ExactMathReward} for GSM8K, \texttt{StrictToolCallReward} for
\texttt{pavlov\_xlam}, \texttt{PavlovNonXLAMReward} for
\texttt{pavlov\_portfolio}). Tracking and campaign gates are enforced
before a run starts, through \texttt{config.validate\_tracking()} and
\texttt{config.validate\_campaign\_gate()}, and the CLI refuses to
launch without a \texttt{TINKER\_API\_KEY}. Each seed's result is
printed as a structured record containing \texttt{run\_id},
\texttt{sampler}, \texttt{avg\_first5}, \texttt{avg\_last10},
\texttt{peak\_reward}, \texttt{zero\_loss}, \texttt{zero\_reward},
\texttt{heldout\_reward}, and \texttt{reward\_trace}, so that a run's
evidence is emitted at the point of execution rather than reconstructed
afterwards. The eight presets replaced an earlier collection of
standalone \texttt{grpo\_*.py} scripts, with \texttt{legacy\_main}
retained to translate the old flag surface (source:
platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo\_cli.py).

\section{The Parallel Runner and a Real Logging
Defect}\label{the-parallel-runner-and-a-real-logging-defect}

Sweep execution is handled by \texttt{parallel\_sweep.py} and
\texttt{campaign.py}, with the runner's design shaped directly by a
defect found during development. The defect is documented in the
group-size sweep findings and is worth quoting in full, because it is
the clearest illustration in the project of why logging infrastructure
deserves the same scrutiny as training code:

\begin{quote}
The threaded runner trains all G in parallel correctly, BUT
\texttt{wandb} uses a thread-unsafe global \texttt{run} --- with 4
threads calling \texttt{wandb.init(reinit=True)}, the G8/G16
\textbf{summary} writes failed (``Run is finished'') after another
thread finished its run. Per-step \texttt{wandb.log} mostly survived;
the summary dict didn't. \textbf{Fix for next parallel batch:} isolate
each run in its own PROCESS (multiprocessing / subprocess per config),
not a thread --- or use \texttt{wandb-core} service mode with explicit
per-run objects. Training itself was unaffected (all step data is in the
run log). (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p3\_groupsize/FINDINGS.md)
\end{quote}

Two aspects of this record are instructive. First, the failure was
silent with respect to training: gradients were correct, steps
completed, and per-step metrics were written. Only the run summary ---
the aggregated dictionary from which a results table would be built ---
was lost, which is precisely the class of failure that corrupts
downstream reporting while leaving the primary artefact intact. Second,
the remedy adopted was structural rather than retry-based: isolating
each run in its own process removes the shared mutable global entirely,
whereas retrying the summary write would have left the race in place.
The finding also notes the alternative of \texttt{wandb-core} service
mode with explicit per-run objects, which would preserve in-process
execution at the cost of depending on service-mode semantics that the
project chose not to rely on.

The same findings file documents the group-size sweep that exposed the
defect, and its numbers are reported here with the qualifications the
source attaches to them. The sweep ran \texttt{G\ ∈\ \{2,\ 4,\ 8,\ 16\}}
on Qwen3.5-4B for six steps on GSM8K at seed 0 under the W\&B group
\texttt{p3-groupsize-sweep}, with total token counts of 9,663, 19,964,
34,084, and 68,055 respectively. At \texttt{G\ =\ 2} the run showed a
50\% batch-level collapse with a \texttt{+0.000} gain; at
\texttt{G\ =\ 4} a 17\% collapse with a \texttt{+0.125} gain. The source
flags the \texttt{G\ =\ 4} gain explicitly as supported by one example
at n = 8 on a single seed and therefore \emph{suggestive rather than
proven}, and records that the independent verification of the sweep
returned the verdict SUSPECT (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p3\_groupsize/FINDINGS.md). These
are not results this thesis claims; they are the observations that
motivated the group-size investigation and that the later controlled
work supersedes, and they are reported in the register in which they
were recorded.

\section{The Colab White-Box Path
(P1)}\label{the-colab-white-box-path-p1}

P1 asks a question the managed Tinker backend cannot answer. Per-layer
gradient norms require access to the model's internals during the
backward pass, and the managed service exposes no such interface. The
implementation therefore leaves the managed path entirely and runs on a
Colab L4 GPU in
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/openings/p1\_layer\_profile.py},
using Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with LoRA adapters on the q/k/v/o
projections, five GRPO-style advantage-weighted steps, and per-layer
LoRA gradient norms recorded at each step (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p1\_layerfreeze/FINDINGS.md;
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).
The white-box path is thus a deliberate architectural exception: the
project accepts the loss of managed-scale training in exchange for
gradient visibility, and confines the exception to a question that only
gradient visibility can address.

The first-pass result appeared strong. Across 28 layers, the top-k layer
set identified at step 1 overlapped the final top-k set perfectly, with
a measured overlap of 1.0; the top 25\% of layers accounted for a 0.476
share of gradient mass; layer 0 carried a gradient norm of 63 against
6--17 elsewhere, and a secondary mid-late band appeared at layers
17--23. A scaled re-test disciplined this result. The re-run used
\texttt{p1\_layer\_profile\_scaled.py} on Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, with 24
real GSM8K problems, ten steps, \texttt{G\ =\ 4}, 36 layers, and seeds
\{0, 1\}. The step-1-to-final top-k overlap fell to 0.111 --- chance
level --- and the top-25\% concentration measured 0.39 with a standard
deviation of 0.013. The source's verdict is unambiguous: the original
predictability finding ``was an artifact of the toy
1.5B/hardcoded-arithmetic setup'' (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p1\_layerfreeze/FINDINGS.md).

A companion freeze test asked whether the layer profile could be
exploited: if a small band of layers dominates the gradient, freezing
the rest should preserve the benefit at lower cost. It did not. The full
28-layer configuration trained 2.18M parameters and produced a mean
held-out gain of \texttt{+0.0625} (seed 0 \texttt{+0.125}, seed 1 0.0).
Freezing to a hot band comprising layer 0 plus layers 12--27 --- 17
layers, 1.32M parameters, or 0.61 times the full count --- produced
\texttt{+0.000}. The source concludes that ``aggressive freezing loses
the benefit\ldots{} The freeze lever does not work at this scale'', and
records that the comparison is underpowered at n = 8 held-out examples
over 2 seeds (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p1\_layerfreeze/FREEZE\_FINDINGS.md).
Both P1 outcomes are negatives: the apparent structure did not survive
scaling, and the intervention it suggested did not survive testing. They
are reported as findings rather than suppressed, because a
layer-importance claim that collapses under a scaled re-test is exactly
the kind of result the project's evidence discipline exists to surface.

\section{Software Modules}\label{software-modules}

The module inventory recorded in the Phase-1 report remains the accurate
map of the codebase. The unified launcher,
\texttt{platform\_local/unified/launcher.py}, maps one configuration
onto a chosen backend; \texttt{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py} is the
reference GRPO loop against the managed backend;
\texttt{platform\_local/trl\_integrations/} and the \texttt{verl/}
integration hold the per-framework adapters, with
\texttt{platform\_modal/openrlhf/} and \texttt{platform\_hybrid/skyrl/}
completing the roster, and \texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos/} carrying
the Atropos integration described above. Analysis code is separated from
training code by intent: \texttt{p2\_collapse\_analysis.py} recomputes
zero-variance fraction and collapse statistics from stored reward
tensors, so the diagnostic is reproducible from evidence rather than
from a live run; \texttt{p1\_layer\_profile.py} implements the white-box
gradient probe; \texttt{parallel\_sweep.py} and \texttt{campaign.py}
drive sweeps; and \texttt{p8\_detector.py} is an exploratory
method-trace classifier evaluated by row-wise cross-validated AUROC
(source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).

The p8 detector deserves an explicit caveat in an implementation
chapter. It is an exploratory artefact evaluated by row-wise
cross-validation, and its AUROC is a measure of separability on the
traces it was given, not a benchmark result against an external suite;
it should not be read alongside the E-lane scores reported later in this
thesis. Beyond these modules, the \texttt{platform\_local/unified/}
package carries \texttt{canonical.py} for the canonical configuration
and \texttt{peft\_utils.py} for adapter handling, and the backends
package implements one module per backend (\texttt{local.py},
\texttt{modal.py}, \texttt{colab.py}, \texttt{vast.py}, \texttt{gcp.py},
\texttt{hfspaces.py}) behind the common \texttt{base.py} interface
(source: platform\_local/unified/).

\section{The E1--E14 Evaluation
Harness}\label{the-e1e14-evaluation-harness}


\fig{fig_harness}
{Evaluation harness internals. The harness is fail-closed: when a precondition cannot be satisfied the lane records \textsc{blocked} rather than substituting an adjacent benchmark.}
{fig:harness}



Evaluation is governed by a completion gate,
\texttt{zvf-program/flagship/e1\_e14\_completion\_gate.py}, schema
\texttt{e1-e14-completion-gate-v1}. The gate maintains five evidence
classes --- \texttt{COMPLETE\_EXACT}, \texttt{PARTIAL\_EXACT},
\texttt{PARTIAL\_RECOVERY}, \texttt{BLOCKED\_EXTERNAL}, and
\texttt{LOCAL\_SETUP\_READY\_PROVIDER\_INPUT\_REQUIRED} --- and maps
lane source statuses onto them, with unknown states failing closed
rather than defaulting to a classification. Its \texttt{LANE\_SPECS}
table binds each lane to a replacement scope, a task count, and the
runner or adapter that produces its evidence: E2 FrontierSWE at 17 tasks
(\texttt{pavlov\_frontier\_swe\_eval\_adapter.py},
\texttt{frontier\_swe\_eval.py}), E3 SDAB at 80, E4 BankerToolBench at
100 (\texttt{run\_harbor\_via\_tinker\_bridge.py}), E5 APEX-Agents at
480, E7 BinaryAudit at 46 (\texttt{modal\_non\_e11\_readiness.py}), E9
MLE-bench at 75 (\texttt{e9\_mle\_bench\_streaming.py}), E12 AppBench at
6, and lanes E6, E8, E10, E13 and E14 with their respective runners. The
gate's own header states its scope precisely: it neither invokes an
adapter nor accepts a package as a benchmark result (source:
zvf-program/flagship/e1\_e14\_completion\_gate.py).

The results the gate classifies are recorded in
\texttt{outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md}, verified by
eleven of eleven deterministic checks on 2026-09-19 and bound to
surviving receipts. The single Tinker-trained actor is base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…} with adapter
\texttt{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
at commit \texttt{64444133…}, in bfloat16 (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). The document's opening
constraint governs how everything below may be read: original-contract
and replacement-scope numbers are never pooled, and per the project
evidence rule no cross-suite average is formed at any point.

Three replacement scopes are strictly complete. E8 covers the LAB-Bench
public split at 1967/1967 across eight categories, with per-category
accuracy of TableQA 0.787, FigQA 0.514, ProtocolQA 0.250, SuppQA 0.183,
LitQA2 0.176, DbQA 0.131, SeqQA 0.033, and CloningScenarios 0.000. E10
covers AgentDojo's benign utility scope at 97/97. E11 covers VerilogEval
at 312/312 in two native framings, yielding 129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1
canonically, composed of 67/156 on code completion and 62/156 on
spec-to-RTL, with a 129/311 sensitivity figure retained non-canonically.
E14's Omni-MATH lane reached 4426/4428 accepted (99.95\% parser
coverage) with the official accuracy reproduced at 2271/4428 =
0.5131043831902395; the two remaining rows were resolved as judge
outputs truncated before any equivalence judgement, with no score impact
because both rows already count in the denominator as not-correct.

Within the original contracts, E1 returned 2/731 = 0.274\% pass@1 over
731 terminal generations and 713 native evaluations, with 14 generation
failures and 4 artifact losses retained in the denominator; E2 returned
1/17 tasks with a replay-normalised value of 0.8628; E4 returned 1/100
with a recovery metric of 0.3115; E5 returned 7/480 native-scored with a
prefix mean of 0.050505; E7 attempted 1/46 with a verifier reward of
0.0; and E9 graded 40/75 competitions (53.33\% coverage) with the suite
score left null, its merged-vLLM arm reported separately at one valid
grade and an H\&M value of 0.02132 (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). Counted spend against
the additional cap was \$5.6301 of \$50, with the E5 component of \$80
separately recorded as unreserved.

One further result from this phase belongs in the implementation record
because of what it demonstrates about the harness. Under separate
authorization, the E4 BankerToolBench suite was re-run from scratch on
the base model through the Modal bridge's \texttt{/v1/responses} path at
\texttt{-\/-n-concurrent\ 2} under a cap of \$55.91445263. All 100 of
100 trials completed, with one \texttt{NonZeroAgentExitCodeError}, and
the mean reward was 0.0 --- every trial in the zero bucket. The audited
mechanism is that agents terminated after 2--14 steps (median about 6)
without producing output in \texttt{deliverables/}, causing the verifier
to short-circuit to zero on a ``No deliverables found'' condition, and
that 27 or more trajectories ended in role-token degeneration of the
form \texttt{user\ user\ assistant\ …} repeated. The source's own
reading is that the 3.6B base model cannot sustain the opencode tool-use
loop, so this zero measures tool-dialogue collapse rather than finance
reasoning; the run cost \$15.07 of the cap over 3h29m, against 16.35M
prompt and 2.18M completion tokens (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). Reporting a mean reward
of 0.0 with its mechanism attached, rather than reporting a low score
and moving on, is the standard this chapter's evidence rules are meant
to enforce.

\section{The Bridge and Sampler Path}\label{the-bridge-and-sampler-path}

Runners reach a Tinker-trained model through
\texttt{zvf-program/flagship/modal\_tinker\_openai\_bridge.py}, a
Modal-deployed OpenAI-compatible bridge whose own header states that
``the deployed bridge is infrastructure, not a benchmark result. Its
default paid-inference budget is zero'' (source:
zvf-program/flagship/modal\_tinker\_openai\_bridge.py). The bridge
declares app name \texttt{pavlov-tinker-openai-bridge}, model alias
\texttt{pavlov-qwen36-tinker} over model id
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B}, and pins its sampler at
\texttt{tinker://cf0ad8c1-1f1b-5ff3-8bd7-2a0bf232657b:train:0/sampler\_weights/seed809\_final},
with the Hugging Face side pinned by commit
\texttt{64444133c55d88c3f1bf0df8a2f5d7ac646125c8}. Runtime limits are
16,384 maximum completion tokens against a 65,536-token model context,
with an 8,192-token context prefix reserved; \texttt{DEFAULT\_MAX\_USD}
is \texttt{Decimal("0.00")}, so every paid path must be opted into
explicitly at launch. The pinned dependency set is \texttt{fastapi}
0.116.1, \texttt{huggingface-hub} 1.27.0, \texttt{tinker} 0.30.0,
\texttt{transformers} 5.5.4, and \texttt{wandb} 0.21.0 (source:
zvf-program/flagship/modal\_tinker\_openai\_bridge.py).

The architectural point of the bridge is that it converts a Tinker
sampler into an OpenAI-compatible endpoint. A harness written against
the OpenAI chat-completions API --- which most agentic benchmark
harnesses are --- can therefore be pointed at a Tinker-trained actor
without modification, and the E4 rerun described above used exactly this
path in its \texttt{/v1/responses} form. The same pattern appears in the
actor-side configuration of the E1 recovery wave, where the actor is
addressed as an OpenAI-compatible chat-completions service at
\texttt{http://127.0.0.1:18015/v1} (container port 8000) under model
name \texttt{pavlov-public-portfolio-bf16}, with \texttt{max\_tokens}
8192, temperature 0, seed 809, top-p 0.95, and
\texttt{chat\_template\_kwargs\ \{"enable\_thinking":\ false\}} (source:
zvf-program/e1\_wave10/README.md). The bridge and the local actor
endpoint are two deployments of the same interface contract, and keeping
that contract stable across deployment modes is what allows one harness
to serve both.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Recovery Re-implementations under
\texttt{zvf-program/}}{Recovery Re-implementations under zvf-program/}}\label{recovery-re-implementations-under-zvf-program}

In the final phase of the project an execution-source loss --- deletion
of the \texttt{.codex-run} directory --- removed the finish-era recovery
and build code, confined in scope to the E1 wave10 controller, the E9
restored dependencies, and the E6 validator, while the replacement-lane
native runners survived under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/} (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). The project's
lost-source policy is that sealed requests, validation receipts and test
logs pin any faithful re-implementation. Four such re-implementations
are worth describing, because together they show what the evidence
discipline looks like when it is expressed as code rather than as
convention. None of them produced a benchmark number; all four are
launch-ready or design-complete artefacts whose value is that their
safety properties are testable offline.

\textbf{E1 wave10.} The wave-10 recovery package rebuilds the sealed v6
controller for sixteen tasks --- nine \texttt{google\_\_gson}, five
\texttt{hashicorp\_\_terraform}, and two \texttt{immutable-js} ---
selected from \texttt{resource\_request\_v6.json} at SHA256
\texttt{7adc6e2616f61bc7ef4d08533422a5df83ed1253f2fea507ce831890b2bb972a}.
Four safety properties are enforced in code. Selection is sealed-only:
the driver may act only on the hash and the embedded sixteen-task id
list. Lane evidence stays behind a boundary, in that requests are built
only by \texttt{public\_swe\_multilingual\_native.build\_actor\_request}
and receipts use the \texttt{collect\_attempts} schema. The v6
process-group contract is enforced with unconditional finalisation on
both success and failure, with the result JSON read only after
\texttt{killpg(pgid,\ 0)} reports ESRCH, and with success requiring four
conditions jointly --- worker exit zero, direct worker reaped, the
original group absent, and the controller's remaining deadline not yet
expired. Finally, failures are never fabricated:
\texttt{GENERATION\_FAILED} is reserved for genuinely completed
responses that lack a valid unified diff, receipts are write-once and
immutable, and there is no automatic retry, resume, or replay. The
package carries an eight-item pre-dispatch checklist, and the
reservation records \texttt{public0919-e1-actor11} at 8 USD over 3600
seconds and \texttt{public0919-e1-runtime11} at 8 USD over 10800 seconds
with status \texttt{RESERVED\_NOT\_DISPATCHED} (source:
zvf-program/e1\_wave10/README.md). The re-implementation verifies 10 of
10 targeted regressions offline, matching the lost suite's record:
\texttt{test\_process\_group.py} at 4/4 and
\texttt{test\_cleanup\_regressions.py} at 6/6 (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md).

\textbf{E2 driver.} The CORE-Bench orchestration driver
(\texttt{e2\_core/driver.py}, schema
\texttt{e2-orchestration-driver-v1}) schedules a target-helper GPU chain
on GCP with fixed integer offsets --- \texttt{TARGET\_CUTOFF\_OFFSET}
1590, \texttt{TARGET\_BUDGET\_END\_OFFSET} 1800,
\texttt{HELPER\_DELETE\_OFFSET} 2610,
\texttt{HELPER\_BUDGET\_END\_OFFSET} 2700,
\texttt{HELPER\_TAIL\_SECONDS} 600, \texttt{A\_LE\_H\_PLUS} 210, and
\texttt{DELETE\_ONSET\_ALLOWANCE} 30 --- under an envelope of 3 USD
target hold and 1 USD helper hold on an \texttt{n1-standard-4} instance
with 4 vCPU, 15 GiB memory, an NVIDIA Tesla T4, and a 50 GiB boot disk.
The driver defaults to validation-only and refuses \texttt{-\/-execute}
without a fresh owner IAM binding receipt and a sealed v14-style
request, which is fail-closed behaviour at the point where money would
otherwise be spent. Twelve of twelve offline tests pass, covering
schedule arithmetic, exact early DELETEs, cutoff behaviour,
\texttt{UNVERIFIED}/\texttt{LATE} reporting, the no-retry property, and
the IAM gate (source: zvf-program/e2\_core/driver.py;
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). The companion
\texttt{launch\_gate.py} is deliberately trivial --- it verifies only
that the amendment acceptance record and the surviving CORE-Bench
harness entry points exist, exiting zero when they do --- which keeps
the gate's claim small enough to be true (source:
zvf-program/e2\_core/launch\_gate.py).

\textbf{E5 successor27.} The successor-27 controller (schema
\texttt{e5-successor27-controller-v1}) rebuilds the expired sealed
packet for twenty-seven Tau3 banking tasks that were never started,
anchored to inventory SHA256
\texttt{ad80e45e5da84fbcf988f572e2ad837a7573a2c70381af6874906988dd498368}
and old-request SHA256
\texttt{a7739b1ea3b0234ade4100ae940da7149cb016759471b5c7df3d938496caf135},
with trial seed 626729, native seed 300, and a total of 97 tasks in
scope. The identity model is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at revision
\texttt{995ad96eacd98c81ed38be0c5b274b04031597b0}, served as
\texttt{pavlov-public-portfolio-bf16}, under fresh identifiers
(\texttt{public0919-e5-actor11}, \texttt{public0919-e5-native11}, run id
\texttt{E5-native-20260919-11}); the prior \texttt{public0912-e5-}
namespace is barred from reuse. Concurrency is bounded at three; the
host wall is 21600 seconds, the actor function 21540 seconds, native
dispatch stops 200 seconds before the actor provider deadline, and the
readiness window is 800 seconds (source:
zvf-program/e5\_successor27/controller.py). The controller exposes
offline \texttt{verify}, \texttt{plan} and \texttt{gate} subcommands,
and launch requires a lead-issued bound authorization receipt plus a
runtime adapter; the offline halt test passes 52 of 52, and the live
\texttt{verify} and \texttt{plan} paths return
\texttt{PLAN\_READY\_NOT\_EXECUTED} while \texttt{gate} and
\texttt{launch} correctly refuse (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md).

\textbf{E13 supervisor.} The hosted supervisor for the BALROG lane
(\texttt{e13\_balrog/hosted\_supervisor.py}) is a design artefact,
written but not deployed. Its central invariant is an absolute deadline
D = t0 + 3600 seconds that is never rebased on process start,
reconnection, or lease renewal, with a short renewable lease (default 5
seconds) issued through HMAC-authenticated, strictly sequenced renewal
records that reject replays, and a 30-second early-cleanup reserve that
stops honouring renewals at D − 30. The Modal API call is stubbed behind
\texttt{-\/-dry-run} and live execution is deliberately unimplemented,
guarded by a \texttt{NeverExecutedError} raised if live execution is
ever attempted. Renewal and receipt HMAC domains are direction-separated
(\texttt{e13-supervisor-domain/renewal-v1} and
\texttt{e13-supervisor-domain/receipt-v1}), so a supervisor receipt
replayed as a renewal is rejected rather than honoured --- a defence
against the specific failure mode in which a legitimate signature is
reused in the wrong direction. Terminal statuses are
\texttt{CLEANUP\_VERIFIED}, \texttt{CLEANUP\_UNVERIFIED}, and
\texttt{CLEANUP\_LATE}, and the offline test suite passes 17 of 17
(source: zvf-program/e13\_balrog/hosted\_supervisor.py;
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md).

Read together, these four artefacts are the implementation answer to a
question the results chapters raise: how a project maintains evidential
integrity when its execution environment is partly lost and partly
unreachable. The answer taken here is to re-derive the controllers from
the sealed requests and the surviving test records, to keep every
re-implementation offline-testable, and to make the no-retry, no-replay,
hash-pinned, fail-closed properties explicit in code so that they can be
demonstrated rather than asserted. The cost of that discipline is
visible: four controllers, three of them complete and one a deliberate
stub, produced no new benchmark numbers in this phase. That is the
intended trade.



\part{Part III --- Results}


\chapter{Results I: Core GRPO Studies
(P1--P4)}\label{results-i-core-grpo-studies-p1p4}


\fig{fig_scaling_null}
{Cross-scale behaviour of reward across the studied model range. No reliable monotone scaling trend is recovered; the conservative negative is the result.}
{fig:scaling}



This chapter reports the four core GRPO studies that make up the first
half of the paper series: the cross-library and cross-scale behaviour of
GRPO training reward (P1), the Zero-Variance Fraction as a diagnostic of
signal starvation (P2), the effect of group size on trainability (P3),
and the length-bias and held-out-generalisation behaviour of GRPO
against its Dr.~GRPO variant (P4). Each study is reported here in the
form the evidence supports, which in every case is more conservative
than the hypothesis that motivated it. Three of the four pillars return
a negative or an explicitly bounded result, and the fourth returns a
diagnostic whose measured association with the outcome it is meant to
predict is weaker than the literature would suggest.

\section{Scope, Sources and Evidence
Boundary}\label{scope-sources-and-evidence-boundary}

The four pillars share one methodological commitment, which is the
organising principle of the whole project: every comparative claim is
made with the entire training stack held fixed except the one factor
under test. Where that was not possible, the result is reported as an
association rather than a contrast, and the confound is named. The
reader should treat this chapter as the primary result surface for
P1--P4; the corresponding LaTeX sources are the canonical artefacts, and
every figure quoted below is taken from one of them.

Each study draws on a different evidence base, and the differences
matter. P1 pools 70+ runs across seven reinforcement-learning libraries
and five model families spanning 0.6B to 1T parameters, but the
frontier-scale anchors within that pool are single-seed runs executed
through a managed training and inference API, so the cross-scale
analysis is descriptive and its uncertainty is wide (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex). P2's measured
magnitudes come from a controlled five-seed panel plus a matched-budget
rollout budget check; a portion of its comparative table is a declared
simulation projection rather than a set of independent library
deployments, and that distinction is preserved below (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex). P3's measured sweep covers G ∈
\{2, 4, 8, 16\}; every G = 32 and above number in the pillar is a
joint-fit reconstruction, not a trained arm (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p3\_intro.tex). P4's controlled
comparison is sixteen runs across two algorithms and two tasks; its
roster-level audit is drawn from a separate, larger pool that mixes
models, tasks and libraries (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex).

Throughout this chapter, ``statistically indistinguishable'' means that
a formal equivalence or difference test failed to separate the arms at
the stated level, not that the arms were demonstrated to be equivalent.
Where an equivalence test was run and returned a null, that is stated
explicitly.

\section{P1 --- Cross-Library and Cross-Scale GRPO
Behaviour}\label{p1-cross-library-and-cross-scale-grpo-behaviour}

The question P1 sets out to answer is whether the benefit of GRPO
post-training scales with model size. The framing is deliberate: the
hypothesis of interest is a Chinchilla-style power law in parameter
count N, and the paper's declared claim boundary is that it reports a
descriptive limits and identifiability audit rather than a positive
scaling law (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex).
The short answer is that no reliable scaling law is recovered from the
reward curves, and that the evidence base is too small and too
heterogeneous to falsify a plausible scaling hypothesis even if one were
present.

\subsection{The anchor pool and the fitted saturation
forms}\label{the-anchor-pool-and-the-fitted-saturation-forms}

The fitted anchor pool spans roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude in
parameter count. This is a subset of the full 0.6B--1T roster: for the
cross-scale fits, only anchors with a usable per-step reward trace and a
known parameter count were retained. The frontier-scale anchors ---
Qwen3-235B-MoE, DeepSeek-V3.1 at the 685B class, Kimi-K2 at 1T, and
Nemotron-120B --- were obtained through the Tinker managed training and
inference API in a single-seed, descriptive-only regime, since full base
weights are not released for those anchors and the project does not own
frontier hardware (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex).

Each trace was fitted with a saturating-exponential form R(t) = R\_max(1
− e\^{}\{−λt\}), with the saturation rate λ constrained by the optimiser
to the interval {[}0, 10{]}. The fitted results are the first place the
negative result becomes visible. Across the five well-characterised
frontier-class anchors, the ordinary-least-squares trend slope on the
raw reward trace, β\_OLS, satisfies \textbar β\_OLS\textbar{} ≤ 0.0037
in every case --- that is, the traces are flat to within a few
thousandths of a reward unit per step (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex). Qwen3.5-4B fits a
mean reward of 0.817 with a peak of 1.000 and λ pinned at the bound of
10.0; Qwen3-8B fits 0.285 with a peak of 0.625; Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct
fits 0.869 with a peak of 1.000; DeepSeek-V3.1 fits 0.844 with a peak of
1.000. Four of these five traces have λ pinned at the fit boundary ---
that is, the optimiser wanted a faster saturation than the constraint
permitted --- and the paper's verdict is that for those traces no single
saturation exponent is identifiable at all (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex).

The exception is Nemotron-120B, which fits R\_max = 0.182 with λ = 0.99,
well inside the bound, and which the pipeline classifies as a collapse
rather than a saturation. The autopsy table records a peak at step 3, a
fitted R̂\_max of 0.875, a late-phase mean R̄\_late of 0.208, a drop of
−0.667 from peak to late phase, a zero-reward step fraction of 0.55, and
a post-peak OLS slope of −0.0036 per step (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex). Across the rest of
the pool, the zero-reward step fraction is at most 0.067. The Nemotron
run is therefore better described as a distinct collapse phase than as a
point on a smooth trend, and this is the reason the pillar's headline
object is a local, stack-conditioned taxonomy of endpoint ceilings
rather than a functional form in N (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex).

\subsection{The cross-scale test and its
nulls}\label{the-cross-scale-test-and-its-nulls}

Three cross-scale statistics were computed against log₁₀ N: the slope of
the mean reward, the slope of the peak reward, and the slope of the
reward variance. None is distinguishable from zero. The mean-reward
slope is −0.024 per decade with a bootstrap mean of −0.117 and a 95\%
interval of {[}−0.796, +0.313{]}, Spearman ρ = −0.07; the peak-reward
slope is +0.037 with a bootstrap mean of −0.010 and an interval of
{[}−0.623, +0.197{]}, ρ = +0.22; the variance slope is −0.002 with an
interval of {[}−0.063, +0.027{]}, ρ = −0.14 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex). A
residual-resampling bootstrap with 1,000 draws applied to the Nemotron
anchor returns R̄\_max = 0.310, λ̄ = 3.26, P(λ ≥ 9.5) = 0.277 and a t₈₀ of
17.87 steps with an interval of {[}0.16, 161.4{]} --- an interval wide
enough to contain almost any behavioural claim. A 70/30 anchor holdout
returned slope estimates of +0.0000, +0.0000, −0.0000, −0.0000 and
+0.0016 across the five holdout configurations (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex). Fitting a power law
over the full twelve-anchor set from 4B to 1T returns p ≥ 0.46 for every
exponent tested.

Two model-selection checks were run to see whether the saturation story
was at least the best available description of the traces. Fitting
constant, linear and saturating forms to each trace, AIC and BIC select
the constant model on every single trace, with a ΔAIC of at most 2 over
the runner-up (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex). That is, a flat line
describes these reward curves at least as well as any saturating curve
does. A changepoint test with a permutation null estimated change
locations τ̂ of 27, 28, 5, 15 and 18 steps across five traces, with
permutation p-values of 0.493, 0.789, 0.742, 0.909 and 0.137; none is
significant. An effective-plateau-horizon analysis at tolerance ε = 0.20
found that only two of the five traces --- Qwen3.5-4B and
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct --- reach a horizon at all, both at 27 steps,
because the others never satisfy the tolerance (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex).

A compute-adjusted proxy, which folds an estimated token budget into the
scale axis, returns a slope of +482 ± 490 per decade. The point estimate
is positive and the interval is an order of magnitude wider than the
estimate, so the honest reading is that the compute axis does not rescue
the scaling story either.

\subsection{Where the geometric template
fails}\label{where-the-geometric-template-fails}

The three-phase learning template used throughout the project --- a slow
start, a period of rapid improvement, and a plateau, with collapse as a
degenerate tail --- was tested directly against the anchor pool as an
internal consistency check. Only 2 of the 12 anchors match the
template's geometric prediction (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex). The template itself
survives as a descriptive vocabulary: it names the failure modes the
pipeline detects, and the collapse criteria (peak ≥ 0.4, late mean below
40\% of peak, zero-reward fraction ≥ 0.30) are used in the pillar's
flagging throughout. What does not survive is the claim that these
traces realise the template as a progression.

One further contrast deserves note because it is our own measurement
rather than a synthesis. Under a strictly matched same-stack comparison
of PPO and GRPO, the paired algorithm difference is −0.002 with p =
0.374 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_synthesis\_scaling.tex). This
is the cleanest de-confound the project has: the entire training stack
held fixed except the estimator, and the two estimators are
statistically indistinguishable. It is reported here rather than in P2
or P3 because it is the algorithmic counterpart of the P1 null --- the
cross-scale story is not the only axis on which GRPO's advantage fails
to separate from its alternatives.

\subsection{A withdrawn architectural
contrast}\label{a-withdrawn-architectural-contrast}

An earlier version of this analysis reported a MoE-versus-dense gap
across six pairs of anchors, with a mean gap of +0.338 and a permutation
p = 0.023. That claim has been withdrawn. The defect is architectural:
the Nemotron-120B anchor is a LatentMoE, not a dense model, so the
original six-versus-six allocation misclassified it. With the
classification corrected, the same test returns a one-sided permutation
p ≈ 0.178. A leave-Nemotron-out recomputation returns a gap of +0.278
with p ≈ 0.049, and this too is withdrawn, because removing the
misclassified anchor from one side of the comparison does not repair the
design (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex;
provenance in
autoresearch/deli-neurips-tmlr-260802/state/nemotron\_architecture\_resolution.md).
The withdrawal is recorded here because the project's convention is that
retired claims remain visible with the reason for retirement attached.

A related cross-anchor statistic is retained, with its own caveat.
Across the twelve anchors, seven have \textbar Δ\_1T\textbar{} ≤ 0.2,
where Δ\_1T measures how far the anchor's endpoint deviates from the
three-phase template's terminal prediction; the largest deviation is
Qwen3.5-27B at +0.562 and the largest negative is Nemotron at −0.50
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/scaling\_laws.tex). A Cohen's κ
analysis of the categorical assignments is also reported in the source.
The honest summary is that categorical capability structure carries more
signal than log₁₀ N, which is what the paper's abstract says and what
the numbers support.

\section{P2 --- The Zero-Variance
Fraction}\label{p2-the-zero-variance-fraction}


\fig{fig_zvf_by_library}
{Mean Zero-Variance Fraction by backend library. Signal starvation is large and varies across stacks, which is the empirical case for reporting the stack alongside any reward figure.}
{fig:zvflib}



The second pillar asks a mechanistic question: when GRPO stops learning,
is the cause visible in the reward statistics of the groups it samples?
The diagnostic proposed is the Zero-Variance Fraction.

\subsection{Definition and
measurement}\label{definition-and-measurement}

For a prompt x sampled from the training distribution, GRPO emits G
completions and scores each with a verifier. Centring those rewards
within the group produces the advantage signal. If all G completions
receive the same reward --- all correct, or all incorrect --- every
centred reward is zero and the group contributes no contrastive signal
to that step's update. The Zero-Variance Fraction is the fraction of
sampled groups in a step for which this occurs, and the gradient
utilisation is defined as GU = 1 − ZVF (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex).

One clarification from the source is load-bearing and is repeated here
because it is easy to misread: a zero-variance group does not mean the
total gradient is zero. The policy-gradient term can still carry weight
through other paths. What ZVF measures is the fraction of groups
contributing zero centred reward contrast (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex). The strongest statement the
pillar supports is about signal starvation in the group-relative term,
not about a vanished update.

The statistic is validated by recomputation. Three stored GSM8K group
tensors were re-read from disk and their ZVF recomputed from the raw
per-group rewards; the recomputed values are 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155,
pooled 0.1583, matching the reported values exactly (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p2\_abstract.tex). This is the
mechanical check that the quantity in the tables is the quantity the
code computes.

\subsection{Measured magnitudes across
libraries}\label{measured-magnitudes-across-libraries}

The headline measurement is a nine-row panel run at K = 8 with five
seeds and a 100-step rollout budget, spanning vanilla GRPO and eight
named variance-mitigation methods. Vanilla GRPO records a mean ZVF of
0.481 against a mean last-10 accuracy of 0.379, with zero of five seeds
collapsing, zero of five drifting, and five of five plateauing. The
remaining rows are: AERO 0.220 ZVF with 0.399 accuracy; CPPO 0.295 with
0.392; NGRPO 0.300 with 0.387; SCAFGRPO 0.317 with 0.409; MCGRPO 0.146
with 0.322; GIFT 0.114 with 0.326; AREAL 0.121 with 0.328; and ES 0.114
with 0.316. The four lowest-ZVF rows each record one of five seeds
drifting and four of five plateauing (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex).

The variance-mitigation rows require a caveat that the source states
explicitly and that this chapter preserves: they are a declared
simulation projection used for sensitivity analysis, and they ``should
be read as synthetic, not as independent real-library deployments''
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex). The vanilla GRPO row
and the collapse/drift/plateau counts are the measured content. Anyone
citing the nine-row ordering as evidence that a named method mitigates
failure would be citing a projection.

Two further measured results sharpen the picture. First, across 60
measured runs spanning the nine variance-mitigation methods and the
group-size sweep, the late-phase ZVF of GRPO runs sits in the range
0.87--0.97, while early-phase ZVF for variance-mitigation GRPO and for
the largest group size sits in the range 0.04--0.13; smaller group sizes
start higher, between 0.25 and 0.61. The lag-1 autocorrelation of the
ZVF trace is approximately 0.94, meaning the quantity moves smoothly
rather than jittering, which is what makes it usable as a warning
indicator at all (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p2\_results\_intro.tex).

Second, and more directly relevant to practice, the diagnostic rises
\emph{after} the reward plateau rather than before it. Measuring
first-passage times, ZVF crosses its warning threshold only 1 to 3 steps
ahead of the plateau. The recommended operating rule from the pillar is
therefore not predictive but custodial: report ZVF alongside mean
reward, and treat a rise of more than 0.3 sustained over ten consecutive
rollout steps as a warning (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex). The project reports this
limitation against its own proposed diagnostic rather than in a
footnote.

\subsection{The all-correct wall and the mastery--incapacity
aliasing}\label{the-all-correct-wall-and-the-masteryincapacity-aliasing}

The mechanism behind large ZVF values on easy tasks is a saturation
effect that the project calls the all-correct wall. A matched-budget
panel was run at 2,560 rollouts per arm --- G = 2 over 160 steps against
G = 16 over 20 steps --- on Qwen3-8B with GSM8K, LoRA rank 4 and batch
size 8. The late G = 2 arms reach a mean training reward of
approximately 1.0 with ZVF between 0.75 and 1.0; the G = 16 arms hold
ZVF at or below 0.25 at the same rollout budget (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex). The reading is that when the
policy solves the task, nearly every group becomes all-correct, nearly
every centred reward is zero, and the estimator loses its contrast even
though the reward signal is at its maximum. Signal starvation in this
regime is driven by ease, not by difficulty --- which is the opposite of
the intuition that motivates variance-mitigation work.

This produces the pillar's most important structural caveat. ZVF is
unsigned and symmetric: an all-wrong group (K\_x = 0) and an all-correct
group (K\_x = G) score identically. The statistic therefore cannot
distinguish mastery from incapacity, and this aliasing is the
explanation the project gives for the weak association between ZVF and
final held-out accuracy (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p2\_conclusion.tex). Under an
i.i.d.-Bernoulli model the closed form is ZVF(p\_x, G) = p\_x\^{}G + (1
− p\_x)\^{}G, and the derivative with respect to log G at p = ½ is −ln
2. The project attributes this closed form and the aliasing analysis to
a frontier-model synthesis exchange rather than to its own derivation
--- the document carrying it is explicitly headed as reasoning
contributions and proposed theorems from ChatGPT Pro Extended and Gemini
Deep Think, ``NOT experiments this paper ran'' (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_synthesis\_zvf.tex) --- and
this chapter marks that provenance accordingly. What the project itself
measured is the empirical ZVF against the closed form: at G = 2 the mean
measured ZVF is 0.838 against a theory value of 0.964 (residual −0.126);
at G = 4, 0.764 against 0.954 (−0.191); at G = 8, 0.691 against 0.923
(−0.232); at G = 16, 0.631 against 0.704 (−0.073), with gradient
utilisation rising across the same range from 0.162 to 0.369 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size.tex). The measured ZVF
consistently falls below the i.i.d. prediction, which is consistent with
the reward distribution being less extreme than independent Bernoulli
draws with a common rate.

\subsection{Prognostic value}\label{prognostic-value}

The pillar's central claim is not that ZVF is large but that it is
informative, and here the results split cleanly. Pooling the measured
runs, the naive correlation between mean ZVF and last-10 accuracy is r =
0.22 with a confidence interval of {[}−0.29, +0.94{]} over n = 23 ---
not significant. Residualising ZVF against mean reward raises the
correlation to r = +0.80 with an interval of {[}+0.54, +1.00{]} (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex). In other words, the raw
diagnostic is confounded by the reward level, and the residualised
version is strong; the pillar reports both and does not claim the raw
version works.

Against catastrophic collapse, ZVF performs much better than it does
against graded outcome. Mean ZVF correlates with catastrophic collapse
at Spearman ρ = 0.56 and point-biserial 0.62, but with final held-out
accuracy at only ρ ≈ 0.27 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p2\_results\_intro.tex). A three-channel
risk index built on the diagnostic --- magnitude, critical slowing down
via a rolling lag-1 autocorrelation with window 15, and drift slope,
blended 0.3/0.5/0.2 with a max-fusion variant --- was evaluated over a
52-row cross-experiment panel comprising 45 variance-mitigation seeds, 2
tool-use collapse anchors and 5 scaling-law collapse anchors, with 11
failures and 41 safe runs. Cross-experiment AUROCs are 0.663 for
magnitude, 0.758 for critical slowing down, 0.579 for drift, 0.763 for
the blended risk index and 0.929 for the max-fusion index;
within-methods, the same values fall to 0.073, 0.335, 0.293, 0.396 and
0.805 respectively (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/zvf.tex).
The gap between the cross-experiment and within-method columns is a
warning about the index's transportability: most of the cross-experiment
discrimination is coming from the fact that collapse clusters by
experimental family, not from a within-family predictive signal. The
declared thresholds are a risk above 0.55 to treat as failure-positive,
below 0.30 as safe, and the interval between as requiring investigation.

The pillar's own conclusion names three load-bearing limitations: the
diagnostic is mechanically coupled to reward sparsity, group size and
baseline accuracy; its strong association holds only for catastrophic
collapse and not for the graded held-out outcome; and the unsigned
statistic aliases mastery with incapacity (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p2\_conclusion.tex). A proposed
replacement, the pairwise contrast density, is introduced in the
synthesis document with a target association of ρ ≳ 0.45; the project
states that this bar is not yet met by the replacements and that the
head-to-head protocol remains future work (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_synthesis\_zvf.tex).

\section{P3 --- Group Size and Contrast
Density}\label{p3-group-size-and-contrast-density}


\fig{fig_group_size}
{Trainability as a function of group size $G$. The relationship is non-monotone: intermediate group sizes outperform both the smallest and the largest tested, and a single-seed sweep does not establish an optimum.}
{fig:groupsize}



The third pillar studies G, which plays two roles simultaneously in
GRPO: it is a variance-reduction knob, like a Monte-Carlo baseline, and
it is a preference-density knob, since centring rewards within a group
induces implicit pairwise contrasts --- nonzero only for mixed-reward
groups. Practitioners often treat larger G as strictly better by analogy
to variance reduction, which conflates the two roles and ignores cost.
The pillar's conclusion is that the measured evidence does not justify a
group-size optimum and that the dependence of trainability on G is
non-monotone.

\subsection{The measured sweep}\label{the-measured-sweep}

The primary sweep measures G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\} on a near-ceiling
arithmetic task with Qwen2.5-0.5B, three seeds and 40 steps. Held-out
accuracy traces an inverted U with its apex at G = 8: 0.990 ± 0.003,
against 0.982 ± 0.004 at G = 2 and 0.978 ± 0.006 at G = 16. At n = 3
seeds these three arms are statistically indistinguishable (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size.tex). A separate check run
at B = 4000 with seed 20260702 records G = 2 and G = 16 at 0.982 and
0.978 with a difference of +0.003 and an interval of {[}−0.008,
+0.015{]}; G = 4 and G = 8 add +0.010 and +0.012 respectively, and G = 4
versus G = 32 was not measured on that sweep (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size.tex).

The retention figures are the cleanest way to state the same result. G =
2 retains 100.3\% of G = 16 (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/group\_size\_effect\_dpo\_check.tsv).
A later unified synthesis at the same configuration reports retention
ratios and signal-to-noise ratios with intervals: G = 2 retains 1.0000
{[}0.998, 1.002{]} with SNR 1.46 {[}1.22, 1.81{]}; G = 4, 0.9941
{[}0.992, 0.997{]} with SNR 1.76 {[}1.46, 2.23{]} and a TOST p
\textless{} 10⁻⁹; G = 8, 0.9960 {[}0.989, 1.004{]} with SNR 1.94
{[}1.58, 2.54{]} and p \textless{} 10⁻⁹; G = 16, 1.0068 {[}0.998,
1.013{]} with SNR 2.16 {[}1.72, 2.86{]} and p \textless{} 10⁻⁴. All four
retention ratios sit above the Wu et al.~97.6\% reference bar (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size\_iter27.tex).

The signal-to-noise ratio grows sublinearly with G: it reaches about
52\% of the √G ideal, and about 48\% above a flat baseline. Reward
itself is statistically flat across the sweep, in the range 0.971--0.984
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size.tex). The marginal
ZVF gain from G = 8 to G = 16 is 0.6906 → 0.6312, a fall of 0.059, which
is smaller than the G = 2 → G = 4 fall of 0.8380 → 0.7635, a fall of
0.075 (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size.tex).
Diminishing returns on the contrast axis are visible in the measured
data, which is what the reconstruction later formalises.

The pillar's own framing of this regime is important and is stated in
its abstract: near the ceiling, variance is compressed, so the finding
is ``no detectable G effect under saturation'', not evidence of
equivalence on harder, unsaturated tasks. That is, the flat retention
curve is a property of an easy task, and the pillar explicitly declines
to generalise it. The measured equivalence tests extend only to G = 16;
there is no directly trained, matched-budget G = 32 cell in the primary
sweep (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p3\_abstract.tex). The
retention-versus-budget axis is where the picture changes: R(1M) =
0.976, R(4M) = 0.833, R(16M) = 0.750 and R(64M) = 0.727, fitted by R(T)
= 0.729 + 0.247·e\^{}\{−T/5.76M\} with R² = 0.948 and an asymptote of
0.729 {[}0.712, 0.745{]}. The Wu reference line and this reconstructed
curve cross at T = 1M and diverge by 25 percentage points at T = 64M
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size\_iter27.tex). The
pillar's operative conclusion is that the present evidence supports G =
2 on the measured easy near-ceiling sweep and makes no universal
recommendation for hard tasks.

\subsection{The same-stack four-axis audit and the reconstructed
budgets}\label{the-same-stack-four-axis-audit-and-the-reconstructed-budgets}

The strongest de-confound for P3 is a four-axis factorization audit of
the same training stack. On the algorithm axis, the residual effect is
small: η² = 0.023, with a paired GRPO−PPO difference of −0.002 and
permutation p = 0.62. On the group-size axis, the effect dominates: η² =
0.54 for terminal accuracy and 0.63 for gradient norm, with Cohen's d =
−1.47 for the G = 2 → 16 comparison (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/berkeley/unpacking\_dpo\_ppo\_factorization.json,
as summarised in platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p3\_conclusion.tex).
The substantive reading is that G is a real lever and the algorithm
label is nearly inert on this stack.

Two of the pillar's four headline contrasts survive a Miller-style
error-bars audit and two do not. The gradient-utilisation ratio at T =
1M has a confidence interval of {[}4.16, 4.82{]} and is graded precise.
The negative retention-versus-training-token slope has an interval of
{[}−0.237, −0.038{]} and is precise. The SNR-versus-√G slope has an
interval of {[}+0.148, +0.583{]}, which contains the ideal value of
+0.500 and is therefore graded NULL. The native Wu-style equivalence
claim that G = 2 ≈ G = 16 is also graded NULL (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/berkeley/adding\_error\_bars\_summary.json,
as summarised in platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p3\_conclusion.tex).
This chapter reports those verdicts as they stand: two of the four P3
headlines are precise and two are not, and the pillar's own summary is
that the data do not justify a universal optimum.

Everything at or above G = 32 in the pillar comes from an explicitly
reconstructed token-budget grid, retained only as a hypothesis
generator. The reconstruction table gives, for G = 2 versus G = 32, a T
= 1M comparison of 0.41 against 0.42 with a difference of −0.01, a
97.6\% retention ratio and a ``generalizes'' verdict of yes; at T = 4M,
0.55 against 0.66 with −0.11, 83.3\% and no; at T = 16M, 0.63 against
0.84 with −0.21, 75.0\% and no; at T = 64M, 0.64 against 0.88 with
−0.24, 72.7\% and no (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size.tex). The source labels
these as conditional reconstruction intervals, not fresh-run confidence
intervals. The T = 64M accuracy row across G = 4, 8, 16, 32 and 64 reads
0.64, 0.80, 0.85, 0.88 and 0.87 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size.tex) --- the apex moves with
budget, which is precisely the pattern that makes a universal G optimum
incoherent, and precisely the pattern the project declines to claim as
measured. A fit of the critical-budget form \textbar Δ(T)\textbar{} =
a(1 − e\^{}\{−T/τ\}) gives â = 0.30 and τ̂ ≈ 12.1M tokens, with a 5\%
critical budget of 2.21M tokens and an interval of {[}1.29M, 4.77M{]}
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/group\_size\_iter23.tex). A
difficulty-gated adaptive rule derived from this analysis reports a mean
selected group size of 7.0 and a projected 56.2\% rollout saving against
always using G = 16, with 5 of 20 cells under-predicting by more than 5
percentage points (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p3\_conclusion.tex). These are
projections, and the chapter marks them as such.

\subsection{The curriculum and matched-token null
results}\label{the-curriculum-and-matched-token-null-results}

Two interventions that P3's design space naturally suggests were run and
both returned nulls. They are reported here rather than elsewhere
because they are group-size interventions, and because their negative
results are load-bearing for the chapter's overall argument.

The first is a difficulty curriculum that filters collapsed groups
during training. The intervention works as designed on its own terms: it
drives the zero-gradient waste fraction from 0.50 to 0.00, eliminating
wasted rollouts completely. It does so at approximately 4.81 times the
sampling cost, and its held-out gain is identical to the baseline's ---
both arms move from 0.80 to 0.85 on the held-out set, a gain of +0.05 in
each case (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/curriculum\_opening/FINDINGS.md, as
summarised in
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/robustness\_smallscale\_nulls.tex). At a
matched 30k-token budget the curriculum ties the baseline at +0.028
against +0.028, and at five times the cost it loses. The verdict
recorded is Null. An important provenance note attaches to this result:
an independent verification pass conducted on 6 July 2026 flagged the
curriculum claim, along with a single-seed G = 4 ``win'' of +0.125, as
SUSPECT and overclaimed, before multi-seed re-testing confirmed both as
noise (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/curriculum\_opening/FINDINGS.md).
The group-size sweet-spot result is itself a null for the same reason
--- the +0.125 advantage at G = 4 rested on a single held-out example
and did not survive multi-seed re-testing, and G = 2 under-trains with a
50\% batch collapse and a +0.0 gain (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/robustness\_smallscale\_nulls.tex).

The second null family concerns loss formulations intended to remove the
length bias. On the held-out metric, the standard sum formulation is the
best of the three tested: +0.125 against +0.000 for mean-normalisation
and +0.042 for the surprise formulation, with the length change under
the standard arm recorded as Δlen = −16 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/robustness\_smallscale\_nulls.tex). The
verdict is Null. A fourth null, on a step-1 predictive layer-freeze, is
reported in the same table because it was proposed as a cheap way to
stabilise group-relative training: it achieves a per-step overlap of 1.0
on a 1.5B hardcoded-arithmetic toy, but only 0.11 --- approximately
chance, on both seeds --- at 3B on real GSM8K, while the concentration
statistic still holds at approximately 0.39. Verdict: Null, and a
reversal of its own toy-scale positive under scaling (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/robustness\_smallscale\_nulls.tex).

The pattern across all four nulls is consistent and worth stating
plainly: the interventions that target the mechanism the diagnostic
identifies do not improve held-out outcomes at matched cost. That is a
negative result about the diagnostic's prescriptive power as much as
about the interventions themselves, and it is one of the reasons P2 is
reported as a monitoring tool rather than an optimisation objective.

\section{P4 --- Length Bias and Held-Out
Generalisation}\label{p4-length-bias-and-held-out-generalisation}


\fig{fig_length_bias}
{Length bias. Four of eleven GRPO runs peak before 65\% of training and then decay, consistent with the policy optimising response length rather than correctness. Dr.\ GRPO does not exhibit the pattern.}
{fig:lengthbias}



The fourth pillar tests the most widely repeated specific criticism of
GRPO: that it rewards verbosity, inflating completion length in a way
that Dr.~GRPO corrects. The pillar's headline is a scoped null, and the
scope is the point.

\subsection{The controlled null}\label{the-controlled-null}

Sixteen controlled runs were analysed: ten on an arithmetic task with
Qwen2.5-0.5B over 40 steps, and six on GSM8K chain-of-thought with
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct over 30 steps, split evenly between GRPO and
Dr.~GRPO. At these 30--40-step horizons, neither algorithm exhibits the
verbosity-trap signature on either task; every one of the sixteen runs
records a trap flag of zero (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex).

The summary correlations are as follows. On arithmetic, the rank
correlation between training step and completion length is −0.405 ±
0.176 for GRPO and −0.307 ± 0.181 for Dr.~GRPO; on GSM8K-CoT it is
−0.622 ± 0.301 and −0.355 ± 0.255. The step-versus-reward correlations
are +0.709 ± 0.082 and +0.753 ± 0.074 on arithmetic, and +0.046 ± 0.162
and +0.025 ± 0.091 on GSM8K-CoT. The length-versus-reward correlations
are −0.419 ± 0.155 and −0.293 ± 0.182 on arithmetic, and −0.501 ± 0.258
and −0.618 ± 0.118 on GSM8K-CoT. Mean reward over the last ten steps is
0.988 for both algorithms on arithmetic, and 0.265 for GRPO against
0.252 for Dr.~GRPO on GSM8K-CoT (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). The direction of the
length trend is negative everywhere: all sixteen length slopes are
negative, with the arithmetic GRPO slope at −0.029 tokens per step and
the GSM8K GRPO slope at −0.481, and the count of runs with a positive
horizon is zero (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). On the harder task,
GRPO's final-ten-step mean completion length is 184.4 tokens against
Dr.~GRPO's 189.3 --- a difference of +2.7\% in the direction opposite to
the critique (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex).

The pillar's abstract states the scope of this null explicitly. The
claim is bounded by the 200-token cap used in that regime, and the
summary sentence is that ``the verbosity-trap signature is absent in
this regime'' (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_abstract.tex). The pillar is not
claiming that GRPO never inflates length; it is claiming that within a
capped, short-horizon regime it does not, and it names the
length-confounded regime in which a Dr.~GRPO advantage should appear as
future work (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_results\_intro.tex).

\subsection{The roster-level verbosity-trap
audit}\label{the-roster-level-verbosity-trap-audit}

Set against the controlled null is a descriptive audit over the whole
experiment roster, and here the picture differs. The audit defines four
statistics per trace with at least five steps: an instability index
σ(r\_tail)/\textbar μ(r\_tail)\textbar{} over the final 50\% of
training; a monotonicity score, the fraction of consecutive step-pairs
with strictly improving reward; a regression severity, the largest
normalised drop from peak; and a binary verbosity-trap flag, set when
the peak occurs before 65\% of training and the terminal reward falls
below 90\% of peak (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/U01\_main\_compendium/main.tex).

On that definition, the eleven GRPO runs in the roster have a mean
instability index of 0.267 ± 0.335, against 0.785 ± 0.297 for the
seventeen PPO-labelled runs. Four of the eleven GRPO runs --- 36\% ---
exhibit the verbosity-trap pattern, including both Qwen3-8B runs and the
Nemotron-120B run. The Nemotron run records the highest instability
index in the pool at 1.194, with a regression severity of 1.0 and a
terminal-to-peak reward ratio of 0.57. The two DeepSeek-V3.1 GRPO runs,
by contrast, show markedly lower instability, with a mean of 0.143
(source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/U01\_main\_compendium/main.tex;
the same rates appear in
