platform\_tinker/reports/combined/combined\_capstone.tex, where the trap
rates are given as GRPO 36\% and PPO 71\%).

Two caveats on that comparison must travel with it. First, the PPO pool
is dominated by classic-RL library runs (SB3, CleanRL, Tianshou) that
failed to train at all, with last-10 rewards below 5\%; a near-zero
reward trajectory mechanically produces a high instability index.
Restricted to LLM-native PPO, the PPO Llama instability is 0.105, lower
than GRPO Llama's 0.228, and the source therefore explicitly declines to
claim that GRPO is more or less stable than PPO at the algorithmic level
(source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/U01\_main\_compendium/main.tex;
platform\_tinker/reports/combined/combined\_capstone.tex). Second, the
audit's reward metric is the in-training reward, not a held-out one, and
the audit pool is single-seed per configuration: each Tinker
configuration was run once and is reported descriptively. The project's
only matched base-versus-RL held-out control is the five-seed Qwen3-8B
comparison discussed below.

The task specification for this chapter attributes to P4 the claim that
Dr.~GRPO is the only algorithm without the verbosity-trap pattern. That
specific claim could not be located in any source read for this chapter.
What the sources state is that 36\% of the audit's GRPO runs carry the
flag; the audit does not report a per-algorithm flag rate for Dr.~GRPO,
and the controlled sixteen-run comparison reports zero flags for both
algorithms. The finding recorded here is therefore the sourced one: the
trap pattern is present in a minority of roster GRPO runs and absent in
the controlled comparison for both algorithms, and the project makes no
claim that Dr.~GRPO is uniquely free of it. The narrowest related
statement the sources do support is a length-drift one --- that Dr.~GRPO
is the only algorithm whose length keeps drifting upward after the
initial compression phase (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias\_iter48.tex) --- which is a
different statistic and is not the verbosity-trap flag.

\subsection{Elevated analyses}\label{elevated-analyses}

Because the controlled 30--40-step null is a null, the pillar runs a
sequence of progressively more sensitive analyses to establish whether
the trap is present but invisible at that horizon. Four of these deserve
report.

A sliding-window trap-onset analysis with window W = 10 relaxes the flag
from ``peak before 65\% of training'' to ``local peak followed by decay
within a window'', and finds traps in 2 of 10 arithmetic runs (20\%), 4
of 6 GSM8K runs (67\%) and 6 of 16 runs overall (37.5\%) --- every one
of which is a local re-expansion rather than a terminal divergence
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). This is the
analysis that most nearly matches the roster-level trap rate, and the
pillar treats it as the honest upper bound on trap prevalence in the
controlled data.

A paired bootstrap on the Dr.~GRPO minus GRPO difference in rank
correlations gives, on arithmetic, Δρ(step, len) = +0.098 with an
interval of {[}+0.073, +0.119{]} --- excluding zero, so Dr.~GRPO
reliably compresses length relative to GRPO on the easy task; Δρ(step,
reward) = +0.043 with an interval of {[}−0.024, +0.112{]}, which is not
significant; and Δρ(len, reward) = +0.126 with an interval of {[}+0.045,
+0.201{]}, again excluding zero. On GSM8K the same three quantities are
+0.267 {[}+0.164, +0.419{]}, −0.020 {[}−0.069, +0.065{]} and −0.117
{[}−0.265, +0.015{]} (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). The reading is that
Dr.~GRPO's length attenuation is real but task-dependent, and that on
the harder task it deepens rather than weakens the length--reward
coupling.

A decile-binned coupling analysis on GSM8K shows the mechanism directly:
completions in the first length decile, spanning 153--180 tokens, earn a
mean reward of 0.351, while completions in the eighth decile, spanning
194--195 tokens, earn 0.233. Longer completions are rewarded less, which
is the opposite of the verbosity-trap prediction (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). A 100-step
cross-validation on the arithmetic metrics file returns ρ(step, len) =
+0.41 and ρ(step, reward) = +0.27, but the absolute length range across
that run is 0.08 tokens against a cap of 5 tokens --- a positive
correlation over a range too small to be a length effect (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex).

The length--ZVF coupling analysis at iteration 12 is the most
mechanistically interesting of the elevated results, because its sign
flips between tasks. On arithmetic, ρ(len, zvf) is −0.449 {[}−0.584,
−0.324{]} for GRPO and −0.291 {[}−0.428, −0.139{]} for Dr.~GRPO; on
GSM8K it is +0.286 {[}+0.231, +0.348{]} and +0.187 {[}+0.148, +0.225{]}.
The Dr.~GRPO minus GRPO difference is +0.158 {[}+0.075, +0.239{]} on
arithmetic and −0.099 {[}−0.132, −0.041{]} on GSM8K (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). Conditioning ZVF on
length bins makes the arithmetic structure concrete: the shortest bin
has ZVF 0.92 and the longest 0.62, a drop of −0.30 {[}−0.34, −0.26{]},
whereas on GSM8K the corresponding values are 0.30 and 0.41, a rise of
+0.11 {[}+0.10, +0.13{]} (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). On the easy task,
shorter completions are more often all-same-reward; on the hard task,
longer ones are.

The mechanism analysis at iteration 20 fits a regression of length on
reward and finds that Dr.~GRPO reduces the slope magnitude on arithmetic
--- −3.65 ± 0.08 against −3.22 ± 0.15, a difference of −0.43 {[}−0.58,
−0.28{]} with p = 0.036; the token-surprisal component moves by −1.11
{[}−1.59, −0.63{]} with p = 0.047; the rank correlation moves by −0.10
{[}−0.18, −0.03{]} with p = 0.087; and the lag-1 autocorrelation is
essentially unchanged, +0.01 with p = 0.92. On GSM8K the same
differences reverse sign, with an OLS difference of +4.41 whose interval
{[}−4.58, 19.84{]} is far too wide to interpret, p = 0.57 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). A mediation analysis
attributes 42\% of the arithmetic effect to ZVF under GRPO and 55\%
under Dr.~GRPO, against 28\% and 12\% on GSM8K.

The drift analysis at iteration 24 closes the sequence. Windowed ρ drift
on arithmetic is +0.326 for GRPO with p = 0.035, decomposing into an
early −0.330 and a late −0.003, against +0.187 for Dr.~GRPO with p =
0.399; on GSM8K the corresponding values are +0.019 with p = 0.905 and
−0.084 with p = 0.769 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). The sign-flip
analysis of late windows gives fractions of 0.527 (p = 0.468), 0.382 (p
= 0.562), 0.048 (p = 0.011) and 0.000 (p \textless{} 0.001) across the
four algorithm-task cells. The one fully significant cell is the GSM8K
Dr.~GRPO arm, in which not a single late window flips sign.

\subsection{Held-out generalisation and the scale
extension}\label{held-out-generalisation-and-the-scale-extension}

The pillar's held-out results are the part of the chapter closest to a
conventional capability claim, and they are reported with corresponding
care. The only matched base-versus-RL held-out control in the project is
a five-seed comparison on Qwen3-8B-Instruct, which moves from 82.0\% to
83.3\% with p = 0.26 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/U01\_main\_compendium/main.tex).
Stated as a mean improvement over the pre-RL checkpoint, the gain is
+0.013 with a paired p = 0.256 at n = 5 --- small and not statistically
significant, and a matched pre-RL control for the Qwen3-8B-Base
checkpoint is not available (sources:
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_results\_intro.tex},
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_intro.tex}). The honest
summary is that on this near-ceiling configuration, GRPO does not
produce a held-out improvement that can be separated from noise at the
available seed budget, and that GRPO and Dr.~GRPO produce held-out gains
that are indistinguishable from each other at that budget (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_abstract.tex).

The lower-accuracy configuration behaves differently and more
conventionally. On Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with GSM8K-CoT, both algorithms
do produce significant pre-to-post held-out gains: +6.2 percentage
points with McNemar exact p = 1.1 × 10⁻⁴, and +5.0 percentage points
with p = 7.6 × 10⁻⁴ (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_results\_intro.tex). This is the
project's clearest evidence that the pipeline trains something real; it
is reported alongside the near-ceiling null precisely because the
contrast between the two configurations is what the pillar's
bounded-null interpretation rests on. Across the same comparison, the
pooled mean completion length drifts from 194.4 to 183.5 tokens, that is
downward (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_abstract.tex).

A scale extension tests whether the null survives when the cap is
lifted. Qwen3-8B with LoRA rank 4 was run on GSM8K for 30 steps at G = 8
and batch size 4, three seeds per algorithm, with the completion cap
raised fivefold to 1,024 tokens. All six arms compress: GRPO moves 1004
→ 905, 981 → 944 and 996 → 900 tokens, and Dr.~GRPO moves 999 → 931, 972
→ 902 and 1000 → 878 --- declines of between 3.8\% and 12.2\% in every
arm. Late ZVF is 0.45, 0.70 and 0.72 for Dr.~GRPO against 0.47, 0.47 and
0.55 for GRPO, and step-0 completions are approximately 98\% of the cap
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/length\_bias.tex). The step-0
saturation is the caveat that limits this extension: with completions
already at 98\% of the ceiling at the first step, the run starts
truncated, and length compression under a binding cap is not the same
phenomenon as length control in an uncapped regime. The pillar reports
the extension as consistent with the null, not as an independent
confirmation of it.

Finally, the P4-facing headline in the Miller-style error-bars audit is
the iteration-136 arithmetic late-training-efficiency contrast: paired,
n = 5, Cohen's d = +2.68, one-sided parametric p = 0.031 and two-sided
permutation p = 0.063. It is graded DECISIVE (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/berkeley/adding\_error\_bars\_summary.json;
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_conclusion.tex). The audit's three
NULL verdicts fall on Pillar-1 and Pillar-3 headlines rather than on
this one. The pillar also records its own limitations plainly: a
single-benchmark held-out evaluation, a single-seed top-10-checkpoint
frontier comparison, weak length identifiability, and marginal paired
tests (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_conclusion.tex).

\section{Cross-Pillar Observations and
Limitations}\label{cross-pillar-observations-and-limitations}

Three observations span the four pillars and are worth stating together,
because the chapter's overall contribution is better captured by their
pattern than by any single number.

First, the project's strongest results are its de-confounds, and its
de-confounds mostly return nulls. The matched same-stack PPO-versus-GRPO
contrast returns a paired difference of −0.002 with p = 0.374; the
four-axis factorization audit attributes η² = 0.023 to the algorithm
axis and η² = 0.54 to the group-size axis; the controlled length-bias
comparison returns zero trap flags across sixteen runs. In each case the
entire stack was held fixed except the one factor under test, and in
each case the expected algorithmic advantage failed to appear. The
single exception is the group-size axis, which is a hyperparameter
rather than an algorithm choice. A reader looking for the project's
central empirical claim should take this one: on the stacks measured
here, the algorithm label matters much less than the sampling
configuration.

Second, the diagnostics and interventions are less prescriptive than
their motivating literature suggests, and the project says so. ZVF
measures signal starvation reliably but predicts catastrophic collapse
well (ρ = 0.56) and graded held-out accuracy poorly (ρ ≈ 0.27), because
it is unsigned and therefore cannot distinguish mastery from incapacity.
The four interventions built on it --- the difficulty curriculum, the
group-size sweet spot, the length-bias loss formulations and the step-1
layer freeze --- all return nulls at matched cost, and two of them (the
curriculum and the G = 4 win) were flagged as SUSPECT by an independent
verification pass before multi-seed re-testing reclassified them as
noise. Reporting an intervention as a null after an independent reviewer
flagged it as overclaimed is, in this project's convention, part of the
result.

Third, the single-seed regime is the dominant shared limitation. The
cross-scale anchors are single-seed and descriptive. The roster-level
verbosity-trap audit is single-seed per configuration, and its
in-training reward metric is not a held-out one. Only the P3 sweep, the
P4 controlled comparison, the five-seed Qwen3-8B held-out control and
the P4 scale extension operate at n ≥ 3, and only the Qwen3-8B control
is a matched base-versus-RL held-out comparison. Every headline in this
chapter is therefore stated at the strength that its own evidence base
supports, which is in several cases weaker than the strength at which it
would need to be stated to generalise beyond the named stack. The
reconstructions (G = 32 and above in P3, the T = 64M grid, the
difficulty-gated saving projection) are held at hypothesis-generator
status for the same reason: they are joint-fit extrapolations, not
trained arms, and the project declines to let them carry a
recommendation.

Taken together, the four pillars describe a training method whose
measured behaviour is dominated by signal availability and sampling
configuration rather than by algorithmic identity, and whose most widely
repeated specific failure mode --- length inflation --- is absent in the
regimes where it was tested directly and present in only a minority of
the roster runs where it was measured descriptively. That is a narrower
and more cautious set of conclusions than the paper series set out to
establish, and the chapter reports it as such.



\chapter{Results II: Reporting, Registry and Controller
(P5--P7)}\label{results-ii-reporting-registry-and-controller-p5p7}

The three papers reported in this chapter form a chain rather than a set
of independent results. P5 makes a claim about the epistemic status of
reinforcement-learning results for language models and proposes a
checkable reporting standard to match it. P6 turns the standard into a
machine-readable artifact with a validator, a badge and a diff tool. P7
takes the diagnostic that the standard requires authors to publish ---
the zero-variance fraction (ZVF) --- and asks whether it is
mechanistically predictable and ready to control rollout allocation.
Each paper is explicit about where its claim stops, and this chapter
reports their evidence in the same register.

\section{A Minimum Reporting Standard for Stack-Conditioned Results
(P5)}\label{a-minimum-reporting-standard-for-stack-conditioned-results-p5}

P5's position is that results in this field are
\emph{stack-conditioned}, and that reporting the framework or algorithm
label without the stack is therefore insufficient to support the claims
that are routinely made from it (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_intro.tex). The evidence for that
position is not a single demonstration but a family of levers, each
showing that varying one component of the stack, with the label held
fixed, moves or flips the reported outcome or its telemetry.

The cleanest lever is a managed 12-cell head-to-head: four
group-relative labels (GRPO, DAPO, Dr.~GRPO, GSPO) run on one stack ---
Tinker-managed, Qwen3.5-4B, GSM8K, three seeds per arm. Mean last-10
training reward spans 0.710 to 0.744, a band of 0.034, with per-arm
values of 0.744 (DAPO), 0.742 (Dr.~GRPO), 0.723 (GRPO) and 0.710 (GSPO);
mean ZVF over the same arms is 0.578, 0.500, 0.567 and 0.511 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-h2h}). The labels move telemetry by more than they move
outcome, and neither movement is large.

The largest lever in the corpus is an outcome flip of 5.0\% to 85.6\%
final training reward under a fixed label when the backend is changed.
That figure must be read with its provenance caveat attached, because
the same inspection that surfaced it also found that the nominal backend
comparison bundled a different base checkpoint. The number is therefore
evidence that item 3 of the standard must record backend \emph{and}
checkpoint identity; it is not a causal estimate of the backend alone
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex, §Item 3). A
second same-label lever points the other way: the label ``DAPO''
produced mean ZVF 0.00 on an open trainer running true dynamic sampling
and 0.58 on a closed stack running an asymmetric-clip surrogate (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_abstract.tex).

These exhibits are summarised by a variance decomposition rather than an
anecdote. On the 12-cell corpus the algorithm term contributes η² =
0.0454 on ZVF, with a bootstrap CI of {[}0.0014, 0.0585{]}, and η² =
0.0075 {[}0.0014, 0.0577{]} on mean reward; the spread of last-10 mean
reward across arms is 0.0359 {[}0.0164, 0.0984{]}. The headline CIs are
decisive on the algorithm axis and tiny on the outcome axis, and the
same decomposition attributes η² = 1.0000 to the group-size axis --- 22×
the algorithm term on ZVF and 133× on mean reward (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Exhibit 5 and Table
\texttt{tab:p5-headline-cis}). The 98-cell mega-campaign reproduces the
shape at larger scale: stack axes account for 72.9\% to 92.7\% of the
variance in the five logged metrics depending on the metric, with
stack-to-seed ratios from 503× to 96,128× (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-mega-eta2}).

The standard that P5 proposes has eight items, and each item earned its
place by exhibiting a documented lever of this kind (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-minreport}). Item 1 is the loss form, reported as a tuple
of ratio level, clip low and high, token mask, advantage normalisation
and dynamic-sampling status --- the same label with and without dynamic
sampling produced opposite ZVF telemetry (0.00 against 0.58). Item 2 is
the reference policy and KL handling, which distinguishes family members
that are otherwise conflated and whose omission is unrecoverable after
the fact. Item 3 is the sampler, backend and precision, including
base-checkpoint identity, motivated by the 17× exhibit above. Item 4 is
the per-step ZVF and gradient-utilisation trajectory, because a single
mean aliases opposite regimes: across a 368-run audit drawn from a
790-run corpus, mean ZVF is 0.97 and 0.95 for two low-reward models
(Llama-3.2-1B and Llama-3.2-3B), falls to 0.25 and 0.29 at mid-range
rewards, and rises again to 0.87 and 0.96 for two near-saturated models
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-ushape}). Item 5 is the group-size schedule, since group
size is a signal-geometry knob rather than a throughput knob; measured
mean ZVF for Qwen3-32B falls from 0.33 at G=4 to 0.18 at G=8 and 0.08 at
G=16, and for Llama-3.1-8B from 0.76 to 0.65 to 0.47, while the two
boundary cases compress the effect as the theory predicts (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-gsize}). Item 6 requires a held-out split disjoint from
the reward environment. Item 7 requires a contamination check and a
parser-robustness probe: adding micro-jitter ε \textasciitilde{}
U(0,10⁻⁴) to rewards collapsed batch ZVF from 0.158 to 0.000 while
pairwise contrast density moved by 3×10⁻⁷, which establishes that a
diagnostic sensitive to verifier rounding is reporting a property of the
stack rather than of the policy (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex, §Item 7).

Item 8 is deliberately different in kind: held-out pass@k curves at k ∈
\{1, 8, 32\} belong to the evaluation report rather than to the run
manifest. The lever here is a matched base-versus-post-RL panel --- four
adapters at G ∈ \{2, 4, 16, 32\}, one stack, the same problems --- on
which pass@1 deltas scatter from −0.5 to +1.9 points (under which
reading the G=2 arm would be called a regression) while the pass@32
frontier improves by +1.5 to +2.5 points in all four arms. Neither
reading reaches significance at a single training seed, which is itself
the argument: without pass@k curves and stated noise bands the same
panel supports any narrative the reader prefers (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex, §Item 8).

P5 also ships the machinery that makes the standard enforceable rather
than advisory. An auditor scores a manifest against the eight items and
emits a 0--100 weighted badge; on an n=103 audit the weighted total was
55.0 with a badge span of {[}35.8, 56.7{]} and standard deviation 3.1,
awarding zero gold, zero silver, 99 bronze and 4 wood (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Exhibit 8). Coverage,
however, is where the honest negative lives. On 98 manifests, every one
of the seven manifest fields is present as a key 100\% of the time, yet
item 2 is \emph{validated} in 0 of 98 cases (0.0\%), and all twelve of
its sub-fields are present at 0 in each case; nine of the sub-fields
across items 1 and 2 --- advantage normalisation, clip high, clip low,
dynamic sampling, KL coefficient, KL estimator, ratio level, reference
snapshot and token mask --- sit at 0.0\% with zero information content
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Exhibits 6
and 18). Twenty-three sub-fields examined across the population show no
field at a full null rate, but twenty of the twenty-three carry at most
two distinct values, and the twenty entries collapse to ten distinct
23-bit fingerprints (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_registry\_health.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p6-minreport-subfield}, reported for the same corpus).

Two further analyses bound what the standard can be expected to buy. The
first is a discriminative-entropy audit: four of the seven manifest
fields are vacuous on this corpus --- loss form, reference policy and
KL, sampler/backend/precision, and decontamination notes each carry too
little task-discriminative information to distinguish runs --- while the
per-step ZVF path and the group-size schedule carry the information
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-discriminative-entropy}). A field-sufficiency regression
agrees: the full stack predicts ZVF at R² = 0.832 {[}0.772, 0.879{]}
while a label-only model predicts R² = 0.000, and for mean reward the
full stack reaches R² = 0.993 {[}0.990, 0.996{]} against the same
label-only 0.000 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-field-suff}). The second is a manifest self-sufficiency
test, and it splits sharply by metric. The manifest carries essentially
all of the ZVF signal (R² = 0.776 {[}0.730, 0.846{]} against 0.773
{[}0.735, 0.834{]} for the cells, ΔR² = −0.003 {[}−0.045, +0.034{]}) but
only a minority of the reward signal (R² = 0.280 {[}0.219, 0.443{]}
against 0.743 {[}0.688, 0.820{]}, ΔR² = +0.463 {[}+0.309, +0.564{]});
all 98 cells are therefore labelled \texttt{honest\_but\_vacuous} on the
three-axis triangulation (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_evidence.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p5-manifest-self-suff}).

The most useful negative in P5 is the placebo-replacement bound. The
four-of-seven vacuity pattern admits two readings: the schema chose the
wrong items, or any stack-descriptor schema would be vacuous on a
single-stack corpus. Projecting six candidate replacement items onto the
live corpus refutes the first reading. Four of the six --- KL
coefficient, clip range low, advantage normalisation and mini-batch size
--- project to exactly one unique value on the 98-cell corpus with a
bootstrap CI excluding any positive information gain, because the corpus
never varies them; temperature schedule would add 0.995 bits and lift
the total from 11.41 to 12.41 bits, and a model-family label is
redundant with an existing column. The variance partition confirms it:
stack axes explain η² = 0.947 of ZVF variance across 25 unique buckets,
with 5.3\% seed residual and an algorithm term of zero by construction
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex,
§Placebo-Replacement Bound). The schema is not the binding constraint;
the corpus is. On the live corpus the seven-field fingerprint carries
11.4 bits in total, four fields carry exactly zero, and Hamming distance
over the fingerprint predicts the absolute difference in ZVF (Spearman ρ
= +0.529, p ≤ 10⁻³), PCD (ρ = +0.541, p ≤ 10⁻³) and mean reward (ρ =
+0.251, p = 0.003), while the badge itself is constant across cells and
cannot discriminate seed-pair dispersion (ρ = +0.000, n = 48 pairs)
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_stack.tex, §Live-Corpus
Coupling).

\section{The GRPO-Registry (P6)}\label{the-grpo-registry-p6}

P6's premise is that the deltas defining GRPO variants --- DAPO's
asymmetric clip, dynamic sampling and token-level loss; Dr.~GRPO's
dropped length and standard-deviation normalisation; GSPO's
sequence-level importance ratio --- live in paper appendices and
framework defaults rather than in any machine-readable form (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_abstract.tex). The registry is the
response, and it ships as three artifacts at \texttt{registry/}:
\texttt{schema.json}, \texttt{entries/*.json} and \texttt{query.py}
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/paper\_P6\_registry.tex).

The schema defines two record types under a single \texttt{oneOf}, both
carrying \texttt{schema\_version} (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_schema.tex). The stack record's core
is \texttt{min\_report}, one sub-object per minimum-report item, with
base-checkpoint identity required inside the \texttt{sampler\_backend}
block precisely because the audit that motivated item 3 found a managed
backend silently pinning a different base checkpoint. Two design
decisions carry weight. The first is the distinction between an
unreported field and a field reported as absent: a null means the author
said nothing, whereas an explicit \texttt{false}, \texttt{0.0} or
\texttt{"none"} means the author made a checkable claim --- ``a stack
that says `we use no KL term' is making a checkable claim; a stack that
says nothing is not.'' The second is that item 8, the held-out pass@k
requirement, is attached under outcomes as an evaluation-report field
rather than folded into the run manifest, preserving the eight-item
standard's split between what describes a run and what describes a
result. Per-component delta status is recorded on the stack side in
\texttt{variant\_deltas\_applied}, with each entry's status drawn from
\{implemented, surrogate, absent, unknown\}. The shipped entry for the
managed DAPO run makes the point concretely: \texttt{clip\_higher} is
recorded as \texttt{surrogate} with the note that the asymmetric clip
was set through the user-facing config while the underlying managed loss
form is unverifiable, \texttt{dynamic\_sampling} is recorded as
\texttt{absent}, and \texttt{token\_level\_loss} is \texttt{unknown}
because it is managed by the runtime. An optional \texttt{ci\_method}
object (method, bootstrap count, seed, confidence level, source) is
populated on the seven derived entries with \texttt{n\_boot\ =\ 2000}
and a percentile 95\% interval, and left null on the remaining
twenty-four.

Three variant-delta records were verified against their source papers:
DAPO with five components (clip higher at ε\_low = 0.2 and ε\_high =
0.28, dynamic sampling, token-level loss, overlong reward shaping, KL
removed); Dr.~GRPO with two removals (the 1/\textbar o\_i\textbar{}
response-length normalisation in the loss and the division by the
within-group reward standard deviation in the advantage); and GSPO with
a length-normalised sequence-likelihood importance ratio and
sequence-level clipping.

The seed population is twenty stack records in five families, all of
which pass validation (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_population.tex). Family A holds four
framework config dumps (Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, seed 42) scoring badges 74, 60,
60 and 62; family B holds the four open Colab trainer arms at toy scale,
scoring 96, 96, 96 and 96; family C is the managed head-to-head at 57,
62, 62 and 60; family D is a same-stack four-method run at 67 each; and
family E is a five-seed risk-index batch at 43 each. The finding worth
stating plainly is why the ordering looks the way it does. The config
dumps were assembled for a reviewer-requested fairness audit and score
only 57 to 74: they nail items 2, 3 and 5, expose item 1 only partially,
and cover items 6 and 7 not at all. The open Colab arms score 96 not
because the runs are better but because an open backward pass, coupled
with explicit declarations that no KL term and no decontamination check
were used, makes every field reportable. Reproducibility supplements of
the kind commonly shipped with RL papers do not automatically satisfy
MIN-REPORT.

The health audit is the registry's own evidence that it works. Every
shipped record parses: 31 of 31 pass, comprising 20 stack records and 11
variant-delta records, with zero broken delta pointers (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_registry\_health.tex). The delta-mix
table is the sharpest single result in P6: the label ``DAPO'' is claimed
by ten entries, of which four implement a delta, one records a
surrogate, three record it as absent and two record it as unknown ---
the same label covering four distinct implementations (Table
\texttt{tab:p6-delta-mix}). Per-item coverage on the twenty stack
records is 100\% for the held-out split (40/40), 95\% for
sampler/backend (76/80), 90\% for telemetry (54/60), 68\% for the
group-size schedule (41/60), 48\% for reference policy and KL (29/60),
32\% for the loss form (38/120) and 20\% for decontamination (8/40)
(Table \texttt{tab:p6-coverage}). The per-openness breakdown inverts the
conventional expectation: the open subset records sampler/backend at
91.7\% and telemetry at 83.3\% with reference KL at 58.3\%, while the
managed subset records sampler/backend and telemetry at 100\% but
reference KL at 33.3\% and the loss form at only 22.9\%.

The registry was stress-tested rather than asserted. A batch of 3,230
adversarial perturbations --- 13 categories across 31 entries and 10
mutations each, with B = 4000 --- moved the overall robustness statistic
from 0.864 {[}0.756, 0.878{]} before the corresponding schema bumps to
1.000 {[}1.000, 1.000{]} after them, closing three silent-failure
categories: a missing identifier pattern check (0.645 to 1.000),
MIN-REPORT leaves declared in \texttt{properties} but not in
\texttt{required} (0.000 to 1.000, fixed by promoting key presence to a
schema invariant), and free-form delta references (0.000 to 1.000, fixed
by replacing \texttt{delta\_id} with an enum generated from the entry
filenames) (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_registry\_health.tex, §Stress Test).
A separate consistency check cross-references the variant-delta records
against the MIN-REPORT blocks: of 31 triples from 13 entries, 7 are
MATCH, 0 are MISMATCH, 0 are MISSING\_REPORT, 5 are SURROGATE\_OBS and
19 are NOT\_APPLICABLE, giving an implementation-honesty match rate of
7/7 = 1.000 with a 95\% bootstrap CI of {[}0.65, 1.00{]}.

The empirical payoff of the schema is the same-stack measurement.
Re-executing four GRPO-family methods (GRPO, AERO, GIFT, AREAL) on a
single identical stack --- managed sampler, G = 8, seed 0, 40 steps, 16
prompts per step --- with the full per-prompt reward tensor logged at
every step, produces reward deltas of magnitude ≤ 2 percentage points.
Two of the three are statistically significant \emph{decreases} rather
than improvements (AERO at −0.014 {[}−0.023, −0.005{]} and AREAL at
−0.020 {[}−0.032, −0.008{]}), so the magnitude thesis survives while the
stronger claim that all differences are within noise does not. The
largest same-stack effects are not on reward at all: AERO and AREAL
lengthen completions by approximately 31 tokens and tighten the length
coefficient of variation, and GIFT's loss shift of −1.67×10⁴ is a
baseline constant (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_measured\_evidence.tex, Table
\texttt{tab:p6-measured-deltas}). A later registry extension measures
the anti-herding residual δ\_div = ZVF\^{}iid − ZVF\^{}obs on the same
tensors, reporting a stack-mean of 0.049 with a range of {[}0.039,
0.053{]}; only GIFT shows a borderline-significant reduction against
GRPO (p = 0.066, CI {[}−0.021, +0.001{]}), which resolves a synthesis
range of {[}0.13, 0.23{]} downwards --- the anti-herding diversity bonus
is real but smaller than the qualitative synthesis had guessed (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_registry\_health.tex, §Contrastive
Yield and Anti-Herding).

The reference CLI, \texttt{registry/query.py}, implements four verbs
using only the standard library: \texttt{list};
\texttt{query\ -\/-item\ reference\_kl} returning FULL, PARTIAL or
UNREPORTED; \texttt{badge}; and \texttt{stackdiff}, which maps a pair of
entries onto the R0--R5 ladder and reports the highest rung triggered
--- R0 identical manifests, R1 nuisance-only, R2 runtime differences, R3
loss-form or KL-handling differences, R4 differing variant-delta sets,
R5 unauditable because a differing item is null on at least one side
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_query\_auditor.tex). The
badge formula weights the seven items equally and is outcome-blind:
badge(s) = ⌊100 · (1/7) Σ c\_i(s)⌋. \texttt{stackdiff} prints a warning
at R4 and above that outcome differences must not be attributed to the
shared algorithm label, and the paper is careful to describe the ladder
as ``a risk scale, not an effect-size scale.'' Two boundaries deserve
restating. The registry reports stated provenance and conflicts; it does
not reconcile unresolved exact-ID or model conflicts, nor does it
validate nominal algorithm comparisons (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_abstract.tex). And the badge is
coarse relative to the corpus --- twenty entries collapse to ten
distinct fingerprints, and twenty of twenty-three sub-fields carry at
most two values.

\section{From Diagnostic to Controller
(P7)}\label{from-diagnostic-to-controller-p7}

P7 asks a narrower question than P5 and P6. Given that the zero-variance
fraction is worth reporting, is it mechanistically predictable, and is
it ready to control rollout allocation? The paper's own framing is that
this does not overturn the descriptive restraint of the earlier work ---
it \emph{tests} ZVF's predictive value through falsifiable predictions
rather than assuming it --- and it flags at the outset that the weakest
link in the chain, the causal connection from within-group contrast to
gradient magnitude, rests on toy-scale directional evidence only
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_abstract.tex).

The theory is compact. Fix a prompt with latent per-rollout success
probability p and a group of G conditionally independent Bernoulli
rewards. Write h\_G(p) = p\^{}G + (1−p)\^{}G for the probability that
the group is degenerate, and model the usable signal per prompt as
S(p,G) = p(1−p)(1 − h\_G(p)). The first factor is the contrast a
non-degenerate group can carry; the second is the probability that any
signal survives centring. Both factors vanish at the endpoints and peak
at p = ½, so the reading is that GRPO learns fastest exactly where the
policy is most uncertain and starves at both ends (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_theory.tex). Three consequences
follow, each paired with the measurement that could have falsified it.
T1 states that the expected ZVF indicator is exactly h\_G(p). T2 states
that h\_G is strictly decreasing in G, with exponential decay rate −ln
max(p, 1−p). T3 states that with unnormalised group-centred advantages
the mean absolute advantage in a group is exactly 2p̂(1−p̂).

The three checks behave as the theory predicts, with the deviations
reported rather than smoothed over. For T1, binning the per-group reward
tensors of the GSM8K runs by empirical success rate gives a ZVF
indicator averaging 1.000 at p̂ ∈ \{0, 1\} and 0.000 at every interior
bin; at run level, measured ZVF sits \emph{above} h\_G(p̄) at every G,
which the paper attributes to the Jensen gap --- the binomial model
evaluates h\_G at the mean p while the true prompt population mixes
mastered and hopeless prompts. For T2, two independent grids agree. The
368-run audit shows monotone decline for every model with an interior
success rate (Qwen3-32B 0.33 → 0.18 → 0.08; Llama-3.1-8B 0.76 → 0.65 →
0.47), with the two boundary rows behaving as the theory flags them:
Llama-3.2-1B sits near p ≈ 0 where h\_G ≈ 1 regardless of G (0.98 →
0.97), and Qwen3-4B-Instruct sits near saturation where the decay rate
is tiny and seed noise dominates, producing the single non-monotone cell
(0.89 → 0.84 → 0.91). The repository-internal sweep at G ∈ \{2, 4, 8,
16\} shows the same decline, with mean ZVF falling from 0.838 to 0.631
while held-out accuracy stays essentially flat near 0.98 throughout ---
raising G buys signal, not, on this near-saturated task, outcome
(source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_theory.tex, Tables
\texttt{tab:p7-modelG} and \texttt{tab:p7-gsweep}). T3 cannot be checked
on the closed stack at all, because it does not expose per-group
gradients; the open-backward-pass measurement, corr(‖∇‖, p̂(1−p̂)) = +0.71
on a 0.5B model with synthetic arithmetic, is reported strictly as
directional evidence.

Because ZVF aliases mastery with incapacity, the paper promotes the
pairwise contrast density PCD = ((G−1)/G)·E{[}p(1−p){]} as the signal to
control on. The falsification that motivates the promotion is the
micro-jitter experiment already cited in §7.1: ZVF collapses from 0.158
to 0.000 while PCD is unmoved. The controller's design follows from the
theory: a \texttt{zvf-triage} callback watches per-step ZVF with PCD as
an aliasing guard, and an adaptive-G controller escalates group size on
ZVF spikes, changing only the sampler budget so that it composes with
any group-relative objective.

The four-arm open-trainer audit is the first place where the
intervention is measured against alternatives on the same data, budget
and seeds (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_controller.tex,
Table \texttt{tab:p7-e3}). Against a 120-rollout baseline, GRPO gains
+0.500 held-out at mean ZVF 0.25; Dr.~GRPO gains +0.575 at 0.27 on the
same 120 rollouts; DAPO-style dynamic sampling gains +0.550 while
driving ZVF to 0.00 at 174 rollouts, a +45\% cost; and adaptive-G gains
+0.575 while lowering mean ZVF only modestly to 0.23 at 186 rollouts.
Three readings are stated. Telemetry behaves as the mechanisms dictate.
Dynamic sampling buys perfect telemetry at a 45\% rollout premium and
does not exceed the cheaper fixed recipe. Adaptive-G spends comparable
overhead and ties the best held-out result, concentrating its spend on
flagged steps --- and the tie is small-n and single-task, so the honest
description is ``competitive with, not superior to, the best fixed
recipe.''

The controller simulation then asks a counterfactual question on the
real reward tensors: for each of the 2,560 prompt-step decisions in the
four-method run, when would each controller have fired, what group size
would it have chosen, and how much contrast would it have restored? The
answer is a negative result of some force. Across all four methods and
all five thresholds of the point-estimate rule, the controllers save
\emph{zero} prompts. Of GRPO's 640 prompt-step pairs, 461 are truly
degenerate --- 427 all-correct and 34 all-wrong --- with p̂ at exactly 0
or 1, and the counts are near-identical for the other methods (AERO 461,
GIFT 493, AREAL 452). At p̂ ∈ \{0, 1\} no group size recovers contrast,
because ZVF(G) = 1 for every G. The empirical ZVF of roughly 0.72 is
high because the prompts are easy, not because they are noisy at the
decision boundary. Bootstrapping the observed binary rewards to size G =
16 confirms it directly: 0 of 1,867 boundary prompts would have been
restored by escalation. The operating range where the trigger fires
selectively is τ ∈ {[}0.70, 0.80{]}, 12 to 19 fires per 40 steps at a
cost ratio of 1.30 to 1.48, and the different thresholds differ only in
how wastefully they fire (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_controller.tex, Tables
\texttt{tab:p7-cf-sweep} and \texttt{tab:p7-cf-granularity}).

In the same trace the escalation asymmetry is stark: 1,723 of 1,867
escalation events (92.3\%) occur on all-correct groups and only 144 on
all-wrong groups (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_abstract.tex). A symmetric ZVF
trigger mostly spends on solved prompts. The paper is explicit that
deleting those fires is a counterfactual proposal only, because
performance preservation has not been measured.

Two refinements sharpen the picture and then complicate it. The Bayesian
controller replaces the point estimate with a Beta(1,1) posterior and a
mid-range probability m(k, G) = Pr{[}0.05 ≤ p ≤ 0.95 \textbar{} k, G{]},
escalating when m exceeds a posterior threshold. At τ\_post = 0.60 it
fires on the same 466.75 degenerate prompt-steps as the point-estimate
rule at τ = 0.50 --- with a 95\% bootstrap CI of {[}454.25, 485.00{]}
--- but at strictly lower cost, 8,854 rollouts against 10,240, a 14\%
saving; and all 466.75 of those fires are on saturated prompts, so it
too saves zero prompts. Above τ\_post = 0.65 it never fires, because the
maximum m over degenerate observations is 0.630 (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_controller.tex, §Bayesian
Refinement).

The calibrated eight-controller comparison then produces a genuine
metric reversal with bootstrap CIs attached. On the cost axis the Pareto
winner is Dualformer-Auto, an automatic difficulty-gated de-escalation
rule, at a mean cost ratio of 0.657 {[}0.599, 0.738{]} against 1.000 for
fixed G = 8 --- a 34\% rollout saving, achieved because the prompts it
shrinks to G = 2 would have been degenerate at G = 8 anyway. On a
different axis, contrast restored per 1,000 extra rollouts, the ordering
reverses: \texttt{zvf-triage} at τ = 0.50 restores 90.10 units per 1,000
extra rollouts {[}89.40, 90.77{]}, against 86.68 for τ = 0.70, 84.00 for
Dualformer-Auto and 80.00 for the Bayesian rule, and all four pairwise
differences have CIs excluding zero. The Bayesian controller ---
Pareto-dominant on the earlier axis --- is dominated here, because its
principled prior fires on the hardest-to-restore degenerate prompts. The
regime decomposition explains the mechanism rather than leaving it as a
ranking: \texttt{zvf-triage} at τ = 0.50 fires on 100\% of steps in
every regime on this panel, so it is operationally equivalent to
``escalate every step,'' and its apparent advantage is mechanical
exposure rather than a smarter rule. Its restoration decomposes 64.76\%
degenerate, 13.69\% boundary and 21.56\% mid-range; the non-degenerate
35.24\% share is what the Bayesian rule sacrifices (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_controller.tex,
§Posterior-Predictive Contrast Restoration and §Statistically Validated
Pareto Frontier).

Two further analyses set the ceiling on what any adaptive rule can
achieve. A per-prompt hindsight-optimal calculation scans G' down from a
base of 8 and finds a bimodal optimum: G* = 8 for 72.9\% of the 2,560
prompt-steps, which are fully saturated and admit no rescue by any group
size, and G* = 2 for the remaining 27.1\%, which are already
non-degenerate at G = 8 and are cheapest to shrink. Mean G* is 6.376,
giving a cost ratio of 0.797 {[}0.776, 0.818{]} --- a 20.3\% saving that
is pure economy, since mean contrast restored per prompt is 0.000 and
mean contrast lost to shrinking is 0.130 ZVF units. That ceiling is
tighter than Dualformer-Auto's observed 0.66 to 0.68 on the four-bin
rule, and the paper states plainly that any future learned rule must
beat it. A finite-pool correction then shows that even this ceiling is
loose: because a finite pool never collides more than independent
sampling, the exact hypergeometric contrast-preservation probability
exceeds the binomial one everywhere, by +0.0494 {[}+0.0481, +0.0506{]}
on the 693 relevant prompt-steps, which means the binomial model
over-predicts starvation and the ``zero contrast restored'' accounting
understates true preserved contrast by roughly 34.2 ZVF units (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_controller.tex, §Exact Finite-Pool
Correction).

Cross-base replication is where the retreat from ``controller'' to
``audit'' is earned. On a second panel of five independent seeds, the
trigger fires 4.20 times per seed {[}3.00, 5.40{]} at τ = 0.70, with the
per-step rate falling from 0.48 to 0.28 as the panel's overall ZVF
drops; what replicates is the sign and the operating-point logic, not a
fixed firing magnitude. The Bayesian branch is entirely silent on this
panel, firing 0.0 times per seed, because every step's implied mid-range
probability lies in {[}0.95, 0.999{]} --- a real negative finding that
scopes the Bayesian controller to distributions containing boundary
steps. And the aggregate firing rate is stable while the \emph{location}
is not: at τ = 0.70 with a de-escalation threshold of 0.95, the
cross-seed Jaccard overlap of the fire sets is 0.133, only 13.3\% of
steps see all five seeds choose the same group size, and the mean
cross-seed Kendall-τ of per-step ZVF trajectories is −0.060. The firing
rate is seed-stable; the firing location is not, and the training-step
index is a meaningless axis for the ZVF signal across seeds. The
replayed unification confirms the direction: on this panel
Dualformer-Auto is the only controller that saves compute (+14\%
{[}0.10, 0.18{]}) while \texttt{zvf-triage} spends more than baseline
(−28\% {[}−0.36, −0.20{]}), with disjoint CIs.

Five design rules close the paper, and they read as constraints
discovered rather than principles asserted (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_design\_rules.tex). Gate on PCD and
alarm on ZVF: escalate only when ZVF is high \emph{and} PCD indicates
interior difficulty, because high ZVF with near-zero PCD and high reward
is mastery --- where h\_G(p) ≈ 1 − Gε near the boundary and added
rollouts buy order ε --- while high ZVF with near-zero reward is
incapacity, where the correct intervention is the data, not the sampler.
Escalate multiplicatively in small steps, since at p = ½ each additional
rollout halves the degenerate fraction; the audited 4 → 6 → 8 schedule
at multipliers of 1.5 and 1.33 captured most of the achievable reduction
at a total cost of +55\% rollouts, whereas G = 16 costs four times the
rollouts of G = 4 for a ZVF drop from 0.76 to 0.63 and no held-out gain
on a near-saturated task. Trigger on spikes rather than levels, because
ZVF is temporally sticky with a lag-1 autocorrelation of approximately
0.94 and drifts upward over training, so a level threshold fires either
never or permanently. Never compare controllers across stacks; ship the
seven-field manifest with item 5 carrying the escalation log. And treat
ZVF = 0 as a purchase rather than a prize --- dynamic sampling shows
perfect telemetry is always available at a 45\% rollout premium, and on
this task it was not the best use of the marginal rollout.

The chapter's honest summary of P7 is the paper's own. No result
establishes prospective superiority over static G = 16, Dr.~GRPO, or
dynamic sampling; adaptive group size is explicitly \emph{not} promoted
to a recommended policy; and all intervention evidence is single-task,
small-n, or retrospective. The paper's contribution is a retrospective
controller audit together with a pre-registered prospective test plan,
and the bakeoff that would close the question --- a matched-budget,
multi-seed held-out comparison against the strong static baseline --- is
future work rather than a claim. In the same spirit, the paper flags its
own August 2026 evidence boundary: unsigned ZVF alone cannot select
between retiring, distilling, retrying, resampling or warm-starting a
prompt, so any operator policy remains a prospective evaluation contract
rather than a demonstrated benefit.



\chapter{Results III: Applied Study
(P8)}\label{results-iii-applied-study-p8}

This chapter reports the applied side study of the programme: a
head-to-head comparison between a tuned gradient-boosted tree and a
fine-tuned large language model on credit-card fraud detection. The
study is deliberately cross-domain. It steps outside the GRPO
post-training work that occupies the rest of this thesis and asks
whether the programme's governing discipline --- measure capability, do
not trust the label --- survives contact with a setting that already has
a strong non-LLM baseline. Where the benchmark chapters ask whether
reinforcement learning changed a model in a measurable way, this chapter
asks a blunter question: when a lender is told that an LLM can detect
fraud, what is the LLM actually doing that the existing tree cannot?
Applied here, the label under test is ``AI'' itself.

The short answer is that the tree keeps the real-time scoring seat, that
the head-to-head which produced this conclusion is in any case the least
interesting fact in the study, and that the LLM's real contribution lies
in four task categories the tree cannot enter at all. Those categories
are compliance narration, document and image fraud via vision-language
models, cold-start triage of novel typologies, and agentic investigation
of the alert queue. The chapter reports the measured comparison first,
then the operational arguments that survive even at accuracy parity,
then the measured evidence beyond the headline, then the capability-gap
taxonomy and the hybrid architecture that follow from it. It closes with
the limitations, stated plainly, because several of them bound what any
number in this chapter may be used to claim.

A scope note is owed at the outset. The study is parked outside the ZVF
thesis and supplies no evidence for GRPO signal starvation, group-size
control, PPO, or SAO; it contributes an example of the programme's
measurement discipline and nothing to the RL claims (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_intro.tex).
Its conclusions stand on the fraud artifacts and the cited domain
literature alone.

\section{Dataset, splits, and the two
arms}\label{dataset-splits-and-the-two-arms}

The benchmark is a custom synthetic dataset generated with
scikit-learn's \texttt{make\_classification} (seed 42): 50,000
transactions, 20 anonymised numeric features V1--V20 of which 10 are
informative and 2 redundant, two clusters per class, class separation
0.8, and a 1\% label-noise setting (\texttt{flip\_y=0.01}). The
generator's 1\% target positive rate realises as an actual fraud rate of
1.44\% over the emitted rows. Four aggregates are appended to the twenty
raw features --- per-row mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum
--- giving a 24-feature design matrix (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_setup.tex).
The released dataset at the repository root carries exactly this schema:
\texttt{V1} through \texttt{V20}, a \texttt{Class} label, and the four
aggregate columns, across 50,000 rows (source: fraud\_data.csv).

The V-prefix convention mirrors the PCA-transformed public card-fraud
datasets, but the data here is a stylised stand-in for one institution's
post-processing feature view, and the study says so. It reproduces the
class imbalance and the anonymised feature shape of single-institution
card-fraud data and none of its temporal drift, verification latency,
seasonality, or adversarial adaptation (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_limitations.tex).

The split is 80/20 stratified with seed 42, giving 40,000 training rows
and 10,000 held-out test rows containing 144 positives. The primary
metric is held-out ROC-AUC; F1, precision, and recall at the default
threshold are logged alongside.

The scorer arm is XGBoost with 200 estimators, maximum depth 6, learning
rate 0.05, subsample and column-subsample of 0.8, and
\texttt{scale\_pos\_weight=7} to counter the roughly 99:1 imbalance. No
hyperparameter search was run beyond this single sensible configuration
(source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_setup.tex).

The challenger arm is a Qwen3.5-4B model fine-tuned on row-to-text
serialisations in the TabLLM style: each transaction is rendered as a
feature-name/value string and the model is trained to emit a
fraud-or-legitimate answer. Because the natural-rate test split contains
too few positives for a stable LLM AUC, evaluation uses a 500-row
positive-enriched held-out subset at 20\% fraud, disjoint from the
training data (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/quick\_20260704/qp8-fraud-sft\_manifest.json).
The fine-tune uses token-level cross-entropy with weight zero on prompt
tokens and weight one on answer tokens, with no reference policy and no
KL term --- this is pure supervised fine-tuning, not RL. The run
completes in 63 steps at batch size 32 and takes 309.1 seconds to reach
its final evaluation (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/quick\_20260704/qp8-fraud-sft.tsv).

\section{The head-to-head result}\label{the-head-to-head-result}

On the tabular scoring task the released artifacts place the two arms as
follows.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\caption{Head-to-head result on the synthetic card-fraud set, by arm and
evaluation split.}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
Arm & Eval split & \emph{n} & Accuracy & ROC-AUC \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
Arm & Eval split & \emph{n} & Accuracy & ROC-AUC \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
XGBoost (scorer) & full test & 10,000 & n/a & 0.7955 \\
Qwen3.5-4B SFT & positive-enriched test & 500 & 0.7920 & 0.4827 \\
\end{longtable}

(source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/quick\_20260704/qp8\_fraud.tsv)

The LLM's accuracy is not evidence of useful ranking. At this class
balance an AUC of 0.4827 means the SFT scores do not order positives
ahead of negatives: the model is at chance-level discrimination and its
apparently reasonable accuracy comes from the majority class. This is
consistent with the broader tabular literature, where tree ensembles
remain the reference class on medium-sized tabular problems and LLM
row-serialisation approaches are most attractive in few-shot regimes,
not at 40,000 labelled examples (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_scorer.tex).

Three provenance points must be stated with the table rather than buried
beneath it. First, the two AUCs sit on different held-out splits and are
not a like-for-like ranking comparison; the load-bearing point is only
that the LLM scorer ranks at chance, not that it is worse than the tree
by this exact margin (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_abstract.tex).
Second, the precision, recall, and F1 figures belong to the released
script's own output rather than to the quick artifact's 0.7955 run. That
file logs AUC 0.7942 with precision 0.723, recall 0.236, and F1 0.356 at
the default threshold, alongside a training time of 0.49 seconds and an
inference time of 0.0062 seconds per 10,000 rows (source:
xgboost\_results.json). The paper's own setup section notes that this
run uses a slightly different feature and library configuration from the
quick artifact and that the two released artifacts agree to two decimals
(source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_setup.tex).
The precision-recall asymmetry --- precision 0.723 against recall 0.236
--- is the class-imbalance signature at an untuned default threshold:
the tree alerts on a small set of transactions it is mostly right about,
and misses most fraud. It is a statement about the operating point, not
about the model's ranking ability, which the 0.7955 AUC measures.

Third, an older internal record from 21 June 2026 listed XGBoost at AUC
0.975 and the LLM arm at 0.948. That environment could not be
reconstructed, so those numbers survive only as historical records and
are not headline evidence; the paper's architectural conclusions are
insulated from them by design, since the seat assignment rests on
arguments that apply at accuracy parity (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_limitations.tex).

\section{Why the seat does not move even at
parity}\label{why-the-seat-does-not-move-even-at-parity}

The artifact-backed ranking is only the first of five reasons the tree
keeps the real-time scoring seat. Even at AUC parity the LLM would not
be moved into the synchronous path, for four operational reasons
(source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_scorer.tex).

Latency is the first. Card authorisation is a hard-real-time loop whose
score must arrive within a network-and-rules budget measured in tens of
milliseconds. The committed run of the released script measures roughly
6 ms per 10,000 rows (source: xgboost\_results.json) --- a rounding
error against that budget. An LLM forward pass over a serialised row,
let alone an autoregressive generation, is orders of magnitude slower
and jitter-prone, and the batching that would amortise its cost is
exactly what a per-authorisation synchronous path cannot use.

Cost is the second. The released cost table prices the XGBoost scorer at
\$1.00 per 10,000 rows and the Qwen3.5-4B SFT path at \$0.0035 per row
on 120 input and 5 output tokens. The synchronous LLM path is about 35
times more expensive than the tree-only path per transaction, and
several orders of magnitude slower on latency --- approximately 300 ms
against approximately 6 ms (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_evidence.tex).
The tree's marginal cost per transaction is effectively zero at
card-network volumes; the LLM's is a token bill that scales linearly
with traffic.

Calibration is the third, and it is the reason the argument does not
reduce to cost. Downstream of the score sit threshold rules, alert
budgets, and expected-loss computations that consume the score as a
probability. Boosted trees produce dense scores that calibrate well
under standard post-hoc maps; verbalised LLM confidences inherit the
miscalibration pathologies of modern neural networks, compounded by the
quantisation of confidence into tokens. A scorer whose 0.9 does not mean
ninety per cent silently corrupts every expected-loss decision behind it
(source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_scorer.tex).

Prompt-injection exposure is the fourth, and it is qualitative but
decisive. A tree scores numbers; there is no channel through which a
transaction can address the model. An LLM that reads serialised
transaction fields --- merchant names, memo strings, cardholder-supplied
text --- creates an attack surface in which adversarial instructions are
embedded in attacker-controlled fields, the indirect prompt-injection
pattern documented in the security literature. A fraudster who learns
that the scorer reads free text will put text in front of it. Placing an
instruction-following model in a synchronous decision loop over
attacker-authored inputs is a security regression independent of
accuracy, and it is the single strongest argument for keeping the scorer
seat non-linguistic (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_scorer.tex).

The conclusion of this section is deliberately narrow: on fixed-schema
tabular scoring with ample labels, the current artifacts score the LLM
below the tree, and it would not get the seat even had it tied.

\section{What the measured evidence
adds}\label{what-the-measured-evidence-adds}

Beyond the headline pair, the released dataset supports a set of checks
that sharpen the negative result and bound its interpretation.

The first is an empirical surrogate for the LLM-as-sensor idea. A tree
fitted on only the four aggregate columns --- a stand-in for what an LLM
sensor returns --- is worse than the 24-feature tree on every measured
axis at 95\% confidence under a paired bootstrap: AUC lower by 0.0245
with interval {[}0.0174, 0.0320{]}, accuracy lower by 0.0135 with
interval {[}0.0109, 0.0160{]}, and Brier score worse by 0.0109 with
interval {[}0.0099, 0.0120{]} (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/p8\_headline\_cis.tsv). Adding
the four engineered aggregates to the twenty raw features does not move
AUC or accuracy outside paired-bootstrap noise: the AUC difference is
0.0002 with interval {[}−0.0002, 0.0007{]}, which contains zero (source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/p8\_headline\_cis.tsv). The
sensor is complementary information, not a substitute for the raw
features.

The second is a leakage caveat that must travel with any tree number
quoted from the wider evidence section. Several tables in that section
report AUC between 0.998 and 0.999 for tree variants on the 10,000-row
split with 144 positives. Those figures are not absolute operating
numbers. The calibration and robustness pipeline that produces them fits
on the full \texttt{fraud\_data.csv} and scores \texttt{test\_data.csv},
whose rows are a subset of \texttt{fraud\_data.csv}; training therefore
overlaps evaluation, which inflates the absolute AUC towards 1.0 and
drives top-2\% caught-recall to 1.000. The leakage-free release split
reproduces the headline 0.7955 with top-2\% caught-recall of
approximately 0.40, and that is the tree's reproducible absolute
performance. The 0.998--0.999 tables are within-protocol relative
comparisons only (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_evidence.tex).
Quoting 0.9988 as the tree's accuracy would be a straightforward
misreading of the evidence.

The third is a noise budget for any deployed sensor. In the oracle
frame, where the sensor emits the exact aggregates, the sensor-augmented
tree breaks even at a missed-fraud loss of about \$5.29. At the
certified sensor-noise budget of σ ≤ 0.02, the break-even rises to
\$26.44, and at σ = 0.05 it reaches \$36.40. The oracle break-even is a
five-fold under-estimate of the realistic break-even a production LLM
sensor would face, and the paper states this as the sharpest
reviewer-facing claim of the noisy-sensor frame (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_noisy\_sensor.tex).
Notably, the qualitative cost signature is unchanged by noise: the
sensor-augmented tree remains detectably costlier at low fraud loss, and
confidence intervals straddle zero elsewhere.

The fourth is cost per fraud caught at a canonical cost cell. At an
investigation cost of \$0.50 per alert and a missed-fraud loss of \$100,
raw-features-only costs \$2.68 per fraud caught, the sensor-augmented
tree \$3.28, and the sensor-only surrogate \$14.92 --- a 5.57× penalty
whose direction is consistent across all 25 cells of the phase diagram.
In the (σ, L) plane the sensor never pays for itself in any of the 25
cells (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_evidence.tex).

The fifth is threshold transfer. A cost-optimal threshold fitted on
training data is a fingerprint of the training prior, not a portable
decision rule: under class-prior shift the transfer gap is detectable
for all three tree variants at the ρ = 500 cell, at +6.55, +6.67, and
+2.19 cents per decision respectively (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_evidence.tex).
Any deployment that carries a fitted threshold across a base-rate change
inherits this penalty, LLM or not.

\section{Where the LLM does contribute: four capability
gaps}\label{where-the-llm-does-contribute-four-capability-gaps}

Framing the study as ``LLM versus XGBoost'' was a category error, and
the error is instructive. The comparison was conducted on the tree's
home ground --- fixed-schema tabular scoring with ample labels --- and
the LLM lost there. The useful question is the inverse: on which tasks
does the tree not compete at all? The study identifies four, each of
which is a task category the tree cannot enter, not an accuracy
improvement on a task the tree already performs (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_taxonomy.tex).

The first is document and image fraud: forged receipts, tampered
identity documents, synthetic cheques, and manipulated screenshots.
These inputs lie outside any tree's feature space by construction. The
claim is not that a vision-language model is accurate enough to
auto-decide, but that it is the only model family in the pipeline that
can look at the evidence at all; in the proposed architecture its output
is a feature vector and a written observation, not a verdict.

The second is compliance narration, and the study argues it is the
paper's most durable claim because it survives any future accuracy
result. A tree emits a float; it cannot enter this category at any
accuracy. Suspicious Activity Report narratives are governed by 31
C.F.R. § 1020.320, which sets a \$5,000 threshold and a 30-day filing
window, and by FinCEN's narrative guidance requiring who, what, when,
where, and why alongside the method of operation. Adverse-action
statements under ECOA and Regulation B, 12 C.F.R. § 1002.9, must state
the principal reasons for the action specifically, and CFPB Circular
2022-03 states that creditors may not rely on black-box complexity as a
defence (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_compliance.tex).
Two design consequences follow: the scorer must remain
attribution-extractable, which makes keeping XGBoost in the scorer seat
a compliance posture and not only a performance one; and the scribe must
narrate the scorer's actual attributions rather than invent them.

The third is cold-start triage of novel typologies, before labels exist.
This is a few-shot regime, and it is precisely where row-serialisation
LLM approaches are most attractive. The claim is not that the LLM is a
better classifier but that it is a stopgap classifier that exists during
the label vacuum (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_taxonomy.tex).

The fourth is agentic investigation of the alert queue, at roughly 85
times lower cost than a human analyst by the study's internal estimate
--- a figure reported to one significant figure, built on June-2026
token prices and assumed investigation times, and not validated against
any production deployment. It should be read as ``one to two orders of
magnitude,'' not as a measured constant. Crucially, this role is
post-score: the agent works the queue after the tree has ranked it
(source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_taxonomy.tex).

\section{The hybrid architecture}\label{the-hybrid-architecture}

These findings distil into a three-seat architecture. The LLM is the
sensor: it reads unstructured evidence and emits typed features ---
document-consistency scores, extracted field values, tamper-likelihood
indicators, mismatch flags --- across a fixed numeric schema, with no
tools, no memory across cases, and no authority. XGBoost remains the
scorer: nothing in the real-time loop parses natural language, which
preserves both the tree's calibration and the containment of the
injection surface to per-case feature corruption. The LLM is also the
scribe: it drafts Suspicious Activity Report narratives and
adverse-action statements conditioned on the tree's own feature
attributions, so that the stated reasons are the model's actual
principal reasons, with provenance links on every draft and a human
signature on every filing. An agentic triage stage sits between scoring
and writing, working the alert queue asynchronously under a budget cap,
so that token spend lands only on the small fraction of traffic that
alerts. Analyst dispositions flow back as confirmed labels, closing the
loop with the scorer (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_architecture.tex).

\section{Limitations}\label{limitations}

Six limitations bound what this chapter may be used to claim, and they
are not incidental (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_limitations.tex).

The benchmark is a custom synthetic dataset, not production
transactions. It reproduces class imbalance and anonymised feature shape
and none of the temporal drift, verification latency, seasonality, or
adversarial adaptation of real card-fraud data. The artifact-backed AUCs
of 0.7955 and 0.48268 are results on a stylised testbed and must not be
quoted as production expectations. No number in the study should be read
as an expected production AUC, and the margin between the arms is
dataset- and environment-specific.

The historical numbers are records, not reproductions, as discussed
above. There is no cross-institution validation: everything reported
here is one configuration of one synthetic generator standing in for one
institution's feature view, and the head-to-head has not been replicated
on any second data distribution, public benchmark, or real institution.
The cost estimates are internal; the 85× agentic-triage figure, like the
latency and per-transaction cost contrasts, is an operational
characterisation rather than a benchmarked service-level measurement.
The challenger is a single LLM family under a single serialisation
format and a single fine-tuning budget; stronger or differently trained
models could narrow the accuracy gap, though the seat assignment does
not hinge on the gap because the latency, cost, calibration, and
injection arguments apply at parity. Finally, the capability gaps are
argued rather than all measured: of the four taxonomy entries, only the
scorer head-to-head carries a number of the study's own. The sensor,
scribe, and cold-start seats are grounded in external literature and
primary regulatory text, the hybrid architecture is a design
contribution whose integrated performance is future work, and nothing in
the compliance discussion is legal advice.

\section{Relation to the thesis}\label{relation-to-the-thesis}

The fraud study belongs in this report as a boundary test of the
programme's central discipline rather than as a result about RL. It
contributes one worked example of the programme's habit of measuring a
claimed capability instead of accepting its label: the label happened to
be ``AI in fraud detection,'' the measurement happened to return a
negative, and the negative is reported as found. Its scientific claims
stand or fall on the fraud artifacts and the cited domain literature
alone, and it supplies no evidence for the GRPO, PPO, or SAO results in
the surrounding chapters (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_intro.tex).



\chapter{The E1--E14 Held-Out Evaluation
Campaign}\label{the-e1e14-held-out-evaluation-campaign}


\fig{fig_lane_status}
{Terminal status of all fourteen E1--E14 lanes. Each cell names the suite and its state, so status is legible without relying on colour alone.}
{fig:lanes}



\section{Design of the campaign}\label{design-of-the-campaign}

The E1--E14 campaign evaluates a single frozen actor against fourteen
held-out agentic and reasoning benchmark suites. Its distinguishing
commitment is that the actor is fixed across every lane: one checkpoint,
one sampling configuration, no per-lane fine-tuning, no prompt tuning,
and no evaluator-specific adaptation. Any difference observed between
lanes therefore bears on what the tasks demand rather than on how the
model was prepared for them.

Each lane is graded by its own suite's native evaluator at a pinned
revision. No lane substitutes a re-implemented metric for the upstream
grader, because a substituted metric would no longer be evidence about
the benchmark it claims to measure. Where a lane's original benchmark
could not be obtained, the replacement scope is named explicitly and its
results are reported separately from the original contract rather than
pooled with it.

The evaluated actor is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} (base commit
\texttt{995ad96e}) with the Tinker-trained adapter
\texttt{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1}
(commit \texttt{64444133}), served in bfloat16. Prompt text, sampling
parameters and preserved original outputs are unchanged from the run
that produced them.

\section{Evidence discipline}\label{evidence-discipline}

Every reported figure in the campaign is bound to a surviving receipt in
the project repository, and the structural claims were re-verified by
deterministic code checks on 19 September 2026 (11 of 11 passing;
source:
\texttt{outputs/verification/LEDGER\_CODE\_CHECK\_2026-09-19.json}).

Three quantities are tracked separately throughout, because a single
table would otherwise merge them: what was \textbf{prepared} (a suite
inventoried, a harness built, a manifest validated), what was
\textbf{executed} (tasks actually run against the actor), and what was
\textbf{scored} (tasks carrying a native, verifiable verdict). A lane
with high preparation and low scoring is a different situation from a
lane that was never attempted, and the campaign's records distinguish
them.

The campaign is fail-closed. When a precondition cannot be satisfied ---
a credential withheld, a cloud quota denied, a private bundle not
released --- the lane records that state rather than substituting an
adjacent benchmark and reporting its score under the original name. Two
reporting rules follow from this and are held throughout:
original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are never pooled, and no
cross-suite aggregate or average is computed.

\section{Scope of the fourteen lanes}\label{scope-of-the-fourteen-lanes}

Each lane is defined by one original benchmark contract and, where
applicable, one named replacement scope.

The suites span software engineering (E1, E2), tool-using agents (E4,
E5), web navigation (E6), binary and security analysis (E7), scientific
literature reasoning (E8), machine-learning engineering (E9), agent
safety and utility (E10), hardware design in Verilog (E11), application
deployment (E12), sequential decision-making in games (E13), and
competition mathematics (E14). The breadth is deliberate: it tests
whether a single actor's behaviour generalises across task types whose
native evaluators share no implementation.

\section{Reporting of results}\label{reporting-of-results}

The campaign is complete in the sense that matters for an evidence
artefact: all fourteen lanes have reached a recorded terminal state, and
none remains unresolved. Each lane's terminal state, alongside its
original contract and the replacement scope used where one was required,
is tabulated in §C.5.

Per-lane results, with each figure quoted together with its own
denominator and its evaluation coverage, are reported directly at the
review session.

The evaluated actor and its training artefacts are released publicly at
\texttt{huggingface.co/arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1-1f1b-5ff-9f777c4018b6},
and the per-run checkpoint series is published under the same account.

\section{What the campaign design
establishes}\label{what-the-campaign-design-establishes}

Independent of any individual score, the campaign's design establishes
three properties that are worth stating separately from its results.

First, the evaluation is \textbf{reproducible from the repository
without re-running the models}. Because every figure traces to a stored
receipt and the structural claims are checked by deterministic code, a
reviewer can verify the arithmetic and the provenance of the campaign's
numbers without access to the compute that produced them.

Second, the evaluation is \textbf{attributable to one actor}. Fixing the
checkpoint across all fourteen lanes means that a difference between
lanes cannot be explained by per-lane preparation, which is the failure
mode that makes cross-benchmark comparisons unreliable in practice.

Third, the evaluation is \textbf{honest about its own coverage}. By
tracking preparation, execution and scoring separately --- and by
refusing to substitute a different benchmark under an original
benchmark's name --- the campaign distinguishes what it measured from
what it did not. That distinction is what makes the lanes it did
complete interpretable.
