\part{Part IV --- Synthesis}


\chapter{Cross-Study Synthesis, Threats to Validity, Conclusions and
Future
Work}\label{cross-study-synthesis-threats-to-validity-conclusions-and-future-work}


\fig{fig_threats}
{Threats to validity grouped as internal, construct and external, each with the mitigation applied in this work.}
{fig:threats}



\section{Cross-Study Synthesis}\label{cross-study-synthesis}

This report has presented two bodies of work built with different
instruments. The first is the P1--P8 corpus, which probes GRPO-style
post-training across libraries, model scales, group sizes and datasets
and ends in a reporting standard, a machine-readable registry and a
controller audit. The second is the E1--E14 campaign, a governance-bound
evaluation of one Tinker-trained actor against fourteen held-out agentic
and reasoning suites. They are the same methodological commitment
applied twice --- once to \emph{what does GRPO do}, once to \emph{what
does a GRPO-trained actor score}. That commitment is attribution: every
comparative claim holds the entire training stack fixed except the one
factor under test, and where that was unachievable the confound is
reported alongside the number rather than absorbed.

The corpus's own synthesis, recorded at the close of Phase 1, is that on
this compute scale the \emph{measurement} layer is useful while most
\emph{algorithmic} levers are noise-limited. The dominant measured
phenomenon is advantage-signal starvation, with the Zero-Variance
Fraction running at approximately 0.72--0.77, and it is easy-driven: the
collapsed groups are mostly prompts the model has already solved. That
rules out the most tempting remedy, because re-baselining the advantages
merely converts the signal into a difficulty proxy with no learning
content, and the remedy that remains --- a curriculum that removes the
wasted rollouts --- returned an honest null. The narrow systems
observation survived better than any algorithmic one: per-step telemetry
separated the available method traces more cleanly than reward-only
features, though the classifier's integrity generalisation was never
tested (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).

The Semester 4 papers sharpen those threads rather than reversing them.
P2's diagnostics are recomputable from stored raw tensors rather than
read back from logged summaries: three stored GSM8K group tensors
reproduce ZVF at 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155, pooling to 0.1583, and the
correlation between ZVF and final held-out outcome is ρ ≈ 0.27 --- real
but weak, so ZVF is offered as a diagnostic and not a predictor. P4's
matched comparison of GRPO against Dr.~GRPO finds the two held-out gains
indistinguishable, with pooled mean completion length drifting from
194.4 to 183.5 tokens under a 200-token cap that bounds what the
comparison can say. P4's companion independent error-bar audit grades
the single P4-facing headline --- audit H7, the iter-136 arithmetic
late-training-efficiency contrast, paired n = 5, Cohen's d = +2.68,
one-sided p\_param = 0.031, two-sided p\_perm = 0.063 --- as DECISIVE
under its n \textless{} 10 paired-bootstrap rule, while its three NULL
verdicts (H3, H4, H5) fall on Pillar-1 and Pillar-3 headlines rather
than on this paper's claims. Across all seven audited headlines the
aggregate is four DECISIVE, three NULL and none merely suggestive. The
asymmetry is the useful part: the headline of ours that survived
adversarial audit is a late-training efficiency contrast, not a
capability claim (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_conclusion.tex;
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/berkeley/adding\_error\_bars\_summary.json).

P3 and P7 both treat group size as a lever and are best read as a pair,
because they disagree in emphasis. P3 finds that under saturation the
retention ratio between G = 2 and G = 16 is approximately 1.00, that the
effective signal-to-noise ratio reaches only about 52\% of the √G ideal,
and that a 505-task conditional utility audit selects G = 4 over G = 5
by 0.00177 with a 95\% bootstrap interval of {[}0.00082, 0.00270{]} ---
positive, small, and explicitly cohort- and objective-conditional. P7
models usable signal per prompt as S = p(1−p)(1 − h\_G(p)) with h\_G(p)
= p\^{}G + (1−p)\^{}G, derives three falsifiable consequences, and
checks them: a 368-run Weights \& Biases audit across seven models
reproduces the predicted U-shape in accuracy, with ZVF 0.95--0.97 at
both reward extremes and 0.25--0.29 at mid-range rewards of 0.35--0.50;
a model × G grid confirms the monotone effect at interior accuracy,
Qwen3-32B moving 0.33 → 0.18 → 0.08 across G = 4/8/16; and a toy-scale
open-backward-pass experiment finds corr(‖∇‖, p(1−p)) = +0.71 on a 0.5B
model, which the paper labels directional only. Where P3's sweep could
not find an optimum, P7 supplies a mechanism under which a shallow one
should exist. That is a reconciliation, not a resolution: the mechanism
is checked in telemetry, and its causal link to the gradient remains
toy-scale (source: platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_abstract.tex).

The same paper is clear about the control it does not have. A
micro-jitter falsification collapses batch ZVF from 0.158 to 0.000 while
leaving the pairwise-contrast density unchanged, which is why PCD rather
than ZVF is promoted as the control signal. In a four-arm open-trainer
audit, adaptive group size matches the best fixed-recipe held-out gain,
+0.575, at 186 rollouts, while DAPO-style dynamic sampling drives ZVF to
0.00 at +45\% rollout cost --- an accuracy-versus-compute trade, not a
controller win. In the larger 2,560-observation controller trace, 1,723
of 1,867 escalation events, 92.3\%, occur on all-correct groups and only
144 on all-wrong, so a symmetric ZVF trigger spends most of its budget
on prompts already solved; deleting those fires is a counterfactual
only, because performance preservation was not measured. Adaptive group
size is therefore not promoted to a recommended policy (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_abstract.tex).

P5 and P6 supply the corpus's methodological claim, and they contain its
sharpest internal tension. P5's headline exhibits are a nominally
matched-configuration backend audit whose final training rewards span
5.0\% to 85.6\% --- a 17× same-label swing the paper declines to assign
to the backend, because the managed stack also silently pinned a
different base checkpoint, and that undisclosed bundling is itself part
of the exhibit --- and a pair of runs carrying the same ``DAPO'' label
in which an open trainer with true dynamic sampling produced mean ZVF
0.00 while a closed stack running an asymmetric-clip surrogate produced
mean ZVF 0.58. Alongside them sits a third exhibit: a 12-cell
head-to-head of four labelled algorithms on one fixed stack, landing
within a 0.034 band with mean last-10 reward 0.710--0.744, a spread the
paper calls comparable to seed-level variation (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p5\_abstract.tex). P5's framing is that
a label-only comparison can hide stack-level variation, and that the
converse also holds: when the stack is genuinely fixed, the labels stop
separating. P6 reads the same exhibit in the reverse direction, using it
to motivate a schema while explicitly declining to re-claim it as novel
empirical science; on the twenty seed entries the MIN-REPORT auditor
badge spans 43--96, and \emph{stackdiff} flags the open-versus-closed
``DAPO'' pair as an R5 label-flip risk from manifests alone (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p6\_abstract.tex).

Two gaps should be named. The first is that P7's controller audit and
P5's stack-attribution programme have not been joined: the controller is
evaluated on open trainers where the update rule is auditable, while the
standard is motivated by a closed-stack divergence that cannot be
audited. The second is P8, the credit-card fraud side-probe, absorbed
here as a measurement-discipline case study and nothing more. Its two
AUCs are not a like-for-like ranking: XGBoost's 0.7955 and the
Qwen3.5-4B SFT arm's 0.48268 on a positive-enriched 500-row held-out
evaluation sit on different held-out splits by construction (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/archive/absorbed/P08\_fraud/sections/p8\_abstract.tex).

The E1--E14 campaign is a different instrument and should be read as
one. A single Tinker-trained actor --- base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…}, with adapter
\texttt{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
at commit \texttt{64444133…}, in bfloat16 --- was evaluated against
fourteen held-out suites, each through its own native evaluator at a
pinned revision. Four scopes closed strictly complete: LAB-Bench public
at 1,967/1,967 across eight categories, AgentDojo benign utility at
97/97, VerilogEval at 312/312 with 129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1 canonical,
and Omni-MATH with all 4,428 dispositions recorded and the official
accuracy of 2,271/4,428 = 51.31\% reproduced. Two suites carry full
verified scores under their original contracts: SWE-bench Pro at 2/731 =
0.274\% pass@1 and VerilogEval. The rest are partial, quota-blocked or
externally blocked, each with a terminal state and a named gate (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md;
outputs/PES\_Phase2\_Review\_2026-09-12/finish/Pending\_Experiments.md).

What the campaign establishes is descriptive, and its scope matters. For
\emph{this} actor, at \emph{this} revision, under \emph{each suite's
own} evaluator and denominator, these are the scores. It is not a
post-training delta: no per-suite base-model comparator appears in the
campaign ledger, so no campaign number, high or low, is evidence about
what the GRPO step contributed. Where the campaign does carry a
base-model arm it is the separately authorised E4 BankerToolBench rerun,
and there the mean reward was 0.0 across 100/100 completed trials, with
the audited mechanism recorded as agents dying after 2--14 steps with no
\texttt{deliverables/} output so that the verifier short-circuits 0.0;
the ledger's verdict is that this 0.0 ``measures tool-dialogue collapse,
not finance reasoning'' (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §9.7).

What governance bought is visible in the ledger itself: eleven of eleven
deterministic checks passing on 2026-09-19, and a historical snapshot
recording E7 as ``1/28 errored'' sitting visibly beside the superseding
table's ``1/46 attempted'' rather than being quietly reconciled (source:
outputs/PES\_Phase2\_Review\_2026-09-12/finish/Pending\_Experiments.md).

\section{Rigour and Threats to
Validity}\label{rigour-and-threats-to-validity}

\textbf{What rigour was exercised.} Diagnostics are recomputable from
stored raw tensors, and headline structural claims were re-verified by
deterministic code checks on 2026-09-19, 11/11 PASS, covering exact
divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash equality and
lost-source absence probes (source:
outputs/verification/LEDGER\_CODE\_CHECK\_2026-09-19.json). Held-out
\emph{n} is reported alongside every gain, and failed runs are counted
rather than hidden: approximately \$30--35 of Tinker spend went to
failed runs, disclosed because concealing it would understate
reproduction cost by roughly 25\% (source: LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md
§3).

\textbf{Statistical power: single-seed Tinker experiments.} Each Tinker
configuration ran once, because cost precluded repetition, so there are
no variance estimates and no Tinker-side significance tests. The
limitations document records that Henderson et al.~(2018) recommends at
least ten seeds, that this bar was met only for the TRL baseline and the
held-out GSM8K evaluation, and that compute constraints then forced that
evaluation to five seeds (source: LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §7.1);
both are reported rather than one chosen. The 30B MoE scaling analysis
uses three seeds; the bootstrap confidence intervals assume independent
observations, so correlation from shared initialisation or data ordering
could narrow the true intervals; and secondary analyses carry
uncorrected p-values. Quantitative conclusions therefore come from the
open-source side, where every hyperparameter is auditable, while Tinker
figures are descriptive (source: LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §6.2).

\textbf{Horizons of 30--50 steps.} The Tinker runs are early-training
snapshots; long-horizon effects such as reward hacking, catastrophic
forgetting and late-stage policy collapse are unlikely to manifest, and
the source conclusion is direct: ``Asymptotic claims are unsupported''
(source: LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §7.1). This bounds results rather
than vitiating them: P1's failure to recover a scaling law across
roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude is a bounded negative, and P4's drift
from 194.4 to 183.5 mean completion tokens is bounded by the 200-token
cap.

\textbf{Closed Tinker internals.} The Tinker SDK exposes a custom loss
path, a limited optimiser surface and standard LoRA hyperparameters, but
not the exact server-side GRPO loss formulation, the reward
normalisation or baseline-subtraction scheme, minibatch or
gradient-accumulation strategy, hardware configuration, or system-level
telemetry (source: LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §6.1). The consequence is
best quoted: Tinker results measure ``the \textbf{platform's}
implementation of GRPO, not an abstract algorithmic specification''. The
illustrative case is a reader asking why Tinker GRPO scores 99.9\% on
GSM8K while TRL GRPO scores 73.4\% on the same task; seed variance and a
model-size confound can be ruled out through the same-model Qwen3-8B
comparison, but the implementation itself cannot (source:
LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §6.2). The threat propagates into the
campaign, whose actor was trained on Tinker. The SDK is also a moving
target: Phase 1 pinned \texttt{tinker==0.16.1}, while the September 2026
bridge required \texttt{tinker==0.30.0} after the older SDK produced a
no-longer-supported error (source:
outputs/TINKER\_SAMPLER\_STATUS\_2026-09-20.json).

\textbf{Per-suite native denominators are not comparable.} Every E1--E14
result is expressed on its own suite's denominator, and those
denominators differ in size, in what they count and in what they
exclude. SWE-bench Pro is 2/731, whose denominator retains 14 generation
failures and 4 artifact losses alongside 713 native evaluations;
APEX-Agents is 7/480; E9 MLE-bench is 40/75 competitions natively
graded, stated as 53.33\% coverage with a null suite score, because
coverage and score are different quantities; E13 BALROG is 13/255
episodes; E6 WebArena is 0/812; E9's replacement MLDevBench is 0/34
graded; E1's replacement SWE-bench Multilingual is 35/300 graded; and
E14's Omni-MATH records 4,428 dispositions of which 4,426 were accepted,
with the official accuracy computed over all 4,428 (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md). A 41.35\% on one
denominator and a 0.274\% on another do not license a comparison, an
average or a ranking, and none is offered; the rule holds within lanes
too, and the ledger states it flatly: ``N/A means unverified; no pooled
results'' (source:
outputs/PES\_Phase2\_Review\_2026-09-12/finish/Pending\_Experiments.md).

\textbf{No cross-suite aggregate.} No mean, median or composite index
over suites appears anywhere in this report or in the campaign materials
it draws on: an aggregate over native denominators would be a number
with no referent, mixing suites that measure different capabilities at
different difficulties under different scoring rules. The cost is that
the campaign has no single headline; the four strictly complete scopes
plus the two original-contract scores are the honest maximum.

\textbf{The E10 benign-utility scope says nothing about adversarial
robustness.} The E10 lane's completed evidence is a benign-utility
evaluation of 97/97 tasks under AgentDojo's benign scope; its original
contract, private AgentHarm tasks, is externally blocked and carries a
closure record with a named reopen condition. The completeness result
therefore carries no adversarial-robustness measurement, and this report
makes no such claim for the trained actor. The lane has a dual-use
dimension, though the limitations analysis finds the marginal uplift of
the released artefacts small, because the base model's capability is
overwhelmingly pre-existing: the Qwen3-8B base-model control already
scores 82.0\% held-out on GSM8K, and full GRPO adds only +1.3 points to
83.3\%, not significant at p = 0.26 (source: LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md
§2.2, §2.3).

\textbf{The retained E11 result came through the original sampler.} The
VerilogEval lane's paid-run receipt records
\texttt{model.sampler\_path\ =\ tinker://cf0ad8c1-1f1b-5ff3-8bd7-2a0bf232657b:train:0/sampler\_weights/step\_seed809\_40}
with \texttt{model.source\ =\ tinker\_sampler\_checkpoint}, so the
scores come from sampling the trained adapter through the Tinker managed
sampler rather than from a locally served copy of the weights (source:
outputs/e11\_verilog\_eval/e11\_trained\_step40\_receipt.json). That
route no longer exists: the sampler status record of 2026-09-20 reports
a bridge deployment booting to a sampler-weights 404,
\texttt{list\_checkpoints} on the \texttt{cf0ad8c1} run returning 404
with the run purged, no \texttt{last\_sampler\_checkpoint} on any of
twenty training runs, and the status string
\texttt{TINKER\_SAMPLER\_WEIGHTS\_DELETED\_E9\_AND\_BRIDGE\_AFFECTED};
the recorded recovery path is to serve the HuggingFace mirror
\texttt{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal} at commit
\texttt{64444133…} through vLLM on Modal (source:
outputs/TINKER\_SAMPLER\_STATUS\_2026-09-20.json). The threat is not
that the number is wrong --- it is receipt-bound, provenance recorded
--- but that the sampling path is not re-runnable, so a re-run would be
a same-weights, different-sampler comparison whose divergence is
unmeasured. The receipt's \texttt{outstanding\_blockers} field further
records that the decontamination receipt ID does not exist and that the
split-manifest validator still fails on it (source:
outputs/e11\_verilog\_eval/e11\_trained\_step40\_receipt.json).

\textbf{Known evidence loss in the finish-era recovery source.} The
record is explicit: the \texttt{.codex-run} deletion recorded in
\texttt{finish/STATE\_RECONCILIATION\_2026-09-19.md} removed the
execution sources of the finish-era recovery and build code --- the E1
wave-10 controller, the E9 restored dependencies and the E6 validator
--- while sealed requests, validation receipts and test logs survive
with matching hashes, and the replacement lanes' native runners survive
under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/} (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §6). The lost-source
policy was applied rather than waived: a faithful re-implementation was
written into a tracked path under \texttt{zvf-program/e1\_wave10/},
where it verifies 10/10 targeted regressions offline
(\texttt{test\_process\_group.py} 4/4,
\texttt{test\_cleanup\_regressions.py} 6/6) (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §9.5). Two consequences
follow: a sealed request without its execution source is not a rerun, so
the E9 MLDevBench runtime recipe reconstruction is still owed; and the
policy response --- execution sources henceforth live under tracked
paths --- cannot restore what was already lost. A related item: the Jev
(TypeSafe) judgment harness lost reliability from roughly 13:10Z on
2026-09-19, both blocker-classification batteries returning degenerate
distributions and a controlled yes-probe falling from about 1.0 to 0.3;
the receipts are retained as incident evidence and the batteries must be
re-run once the health check passes, so the ledger's
first-uncleared-blocker column stands on document evidence rather than
judgment output (source: outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md
§8).

\textbf{Scope and confound inventory.} Several Phase-1 threats remain
live. Many early runs logged train-set rewards only, so time-series
comparisons spanning old and new runs must account for the
instrumentation change. Initial phases used LoRA at ranks 8--64
exclusively; historical baseline comparisons use default hyperparameters
from the Tinker cookbook and the TRL documentation; and no full
learning-rate × rank × batch × group-size grid was swept. Tinker, Modal,
Colab and the PES A100 node use different accelerators, memory
hierarchies and training stacks, so observed differences in reward
dynamics are not purely algorithmic, and the work was not
pre-registered. The model-scale range is 0.6B--235B, so dense-70B and
trillion-parameter MoE extrapolations are unreliable; benchmark coverage
is narrow and biased toward verifiable rewards such as exact match, unit
tests and schema validity, with no human preference data, so findings
should not be extended to reward-model-based RLHF; and every author is
based at one institution in Bengaluru, so benchmark choices and
evaluation design reflect that context (source:
LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §7.2, §7.3).

The resource accounting is itself a threat. Authors' out-of-pocket spend
was \$130--140, with roughly 60 A100 GPU-hours of in-kind PES University
compute valued at approximately \$270 and projected carbon of about 776
kWh and about 296 kg CO₂-eq; the dominant uncertainty is the Tinker
GPU-hour count, inferred because the platform exposes no hardware
telemetry, giving a pessimistic Tinker contribution of about 540 kg
against an optimistic one of about 130 kg. No offsets were purchased;
the mitigation offered is reproducibility by artefact (source:
LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §3, §4).

\section{Conclusions}\label{conclusions}

This report set out to build a multi-framework benchmark for GRPO-style
post-training of large language models and to study that post-training
under a single methodological rule: hold the stack fixed, change one
factor, report what happens. Both halves were completed, and the honest
summary is less flattering, and more useful, than a positive result
would have been.

On the analytical side the contribution is a measurement layer and a set
of bounded negatives. The Zero-Variance Fraction is a reproducible
diagnostic of advantage-signal starvation, recomputable from stored
tensors, measured at roughly 0.72--0.77 with a weak ρ ≈ 0.27 association
with final held-out outcome --- descriptive, not predictive. Its theory
has been made falsifiable, and two of its three consequences reproduced:
the U-shape in accuracy across reward levels and the monotone group-size
effect; the gradient link remains toy-scale and directional (source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p7\_abstract.tex). Group size therefore
has a mechanism and a shallow optimum rather than a universal best
value. Length bias is real at the level of run-level trajectories ---
four of eleven GRPO runs peak before 65\% of training with terminal
reward below 90\% of peak (source: LIMITATIONS\_AND\_IMPACT.md §2.2) ---
but it does not separate GRPO from Dr.~GRPO on held-out gain. The levers
we could test --- a naive curriculum, a matched-token-budget
intervention, an integer group-size sweep --- did not produce reliable
held-out gains. The strongest original contribution remains P5, the
reporting standard, whose status the record states precisely: a targeted
search of the GRPO, model-reporting and provenance literature through
July 2026 did not identify an equivalent GRPO-specific machine-readable
run datasheet with rollout provenance, and this is ``an evidence-bounded
novelty claim, not proof of non-existence'' (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).
The earlier second bet --- predictive layer freezing from step-1
behaviour --- is weakened rather than withdrawn: it held at 1.5B and
collapsed to chance at 3B across four seeds, at a mean of 0.083 ± 0.048.

On the empirical side the E1--E14 campaign delivered what a
governance-first campaign can deliver: complete, receipt-bound results
on four suites; full verified original-contract scores on two; and an
explicit, auditable terminal state for every remaining lane, with a
named gate and a named reopen condition instead of an indefinite
pending. Its discipline is negative, which is why it is credible:
original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are never pooled, no
cross-suite aggregate is computed, and no claim of improvement over a
baseline or over the base model is made anywhere in this work. The
strongest complete positive result --- 129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1 on
VerilogEval, at full 312/312 coverage --- is reported as a measurement
of one actor on one suite, with its provenance recorded down to the
sampler path.

The report's overall conclusion is about method rather than algorithms.
On this compute scale, and with these instruments, the measurements are
informative while the algorithmic levers are noise-limited; the stack is
a material part of the result, so a label without its stack is not a
reproducible experimental specification; and the most durable artefacts
produced here are the ones that make other people's results checkable
--- the ZVF/PCD diagnostic layer, the eight-item minimum reportable
stack, the registry and its CLI, and a campaign ledger in which every
number is bound to a receipt and every gap has a gate.

\section{Future Work (Phase-2 Plan)}\label{future-work-phase-2-plan}

Phase 2 is not a blank slate. Four launches are prepared to the point
where each fails only a user-level gate, the completion envelope for
every lane not externally blocked is approximately \$108, and each
blocking condition is known, not estimated. Spend authorisation is on
record at
\texttt{outputs/e1\_e14\_paid\_authorization\_unlimited\_2026-09-20.json},
unbounded in cap terms while retaining per-run checks and all gates, and
recording explicitly that no paid work had started under it (source:
outputs/UNBLOCK\_CARRYOUT\_2026-09-21.md); an earlier receipt of
2026-09-12 records \texttt{budget\_mode:\ unlimited} with a null total
cap, superseding the prior \$75 cap while keeping per-run controls in
force (source: outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §9.2).
Unlimited budget unblocks nothing by itself: each launch is gated on a
technical chain and a preflight, so the plan below is a list of gates,
not experiments.

\textbf{E1 wave-10, \$16}, is blocked on three items: no Weights \&
Biases login (no \texttt{WANDB\_API\_KEY}; \texttt{wandb\ verify}
fails), no sealed reservation --- only a v12 draft --- and no live actor
endpoint. The re-implementation is complete, verifying 10/10 offline,
and the driver's validation-only mode is green, so the next actions are
a \texttt{wandb\ login}, sealing the v12 hold, binding a live actor
endpoint, and authorising the driver with \texttt{-\/-execute} (source:
outputs/UNBLOCK\_CARRYOUT\_2026-09-21.md). \textbf{E5 successor27,
\$80}, is blocked because no lead-issued bound authorisation receipt
exists --- \texttt{assess\_launch\_authorization} refuses and will not
self-mint --- and because no real \texttt{NativeRuntimeAdapter} exists
in the repository, so there is nothing to bind even with a receipt. Its
\texttt{verify} and \texttt{plan} paths are green live against
twenty-seven tasks, PLAN\_READY\_NOT\_EXECUTED, and its offline suite
passes 52/52 after a halt-test repair that left controller safety
properties unchanged (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §9.5). \textbf{E2
CORE-Bench, approximately \$4}, is blocked on a hard user gate alone: no
fresh owner IAM binding exists, while the rebuilt driver needs
create-and-delete permissions and the prior proposal is stale and
watchdog-only. The offline launch gate passes 12/12, forty-five capsule
setups are complete with zero graded, and what remains is the
exact-resource time-conditioned binding and its receipt file, sealing
v14, and issuing the \$3 and \$1 holds (source:
outputs/UNBLOCK\_CARRYOUT\_2026-09-21.md;
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §9.4). \textbf{E13
BALROG, \$8}, is blocked because its resource request is a draft marked
PROPOSED\_NOT\_ALLOCATED with \texttt{launch\_authorized:\ false}, still
needing the actor05 reservation, a connectivity re-proof, a
supported-route gate change and supervisor deployment; its
hosted-supervisor chain verifies 17/17 offline and twenty-one
never-started receipt candidates are mapped (source:
outputs/UNBLOCK\_CARRYOUT\_2026-09-21.md).

One lane sits outside that envelope by decision: E4's only funded path
to a full BankerToolBench score is a fresh rerun whose grader-only lower
bound is approximately \$59.45, exceeding every authorised envelope to
date and requiring separate scope sign-off as a new-runtime experiment
(source:
outputs/PES\_Phase2\_Review\_2026-09-12/finish/Pending\_Experiments.md).
That lane now has a calibration point: the separately authorised
base-model rerun completed 100/100 trials at mean reward 0.0, spending
\$15.07 against a \$55.91 cap (source:
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §9.7). The Modal sampler
bridge the path requires was down as of 2026-09-21, \texttt{/health}
timing out, so a redeploy or a Colab Pro fallback is a precondition.

Two lanes are blocked on cloud quota rather than on project decisions.
E9's MLDevBench runtime builder needs 4 vCPUs in us-east-1 against a
live quota of 1/8, with case \texttt{178982528000009} open, and a local
build is deferred because 31 GiB free against a builder requirement of
at least 30 GiB leaves no margin for the recipe reconstruction it would
also need. E6's WebArena lane needs 16 vCPUs in us-east-2 against a live
quota of 1/16, with case \texttt{178921342800925} open under the
one-open-request rule. Both were re-checked live on 2026-09-21 and
remain NO-GO; the remedies are escalation of the two AWS cases, or
freeing at least 30 GiB and reconstructing the E9 runtime recipe
(source: outputs/UNBLOCK\_CARRYOUT\_2026-09-21.md;
outputs/E1\_E14\_FINAL\_RESULTS\_2026-09-19.md §9.3).

Each externally blocked lane has a closure record and a reopen condition
--- a provider response, not an internal milestone. Seven lanes are in
this class: E3 (SDAB private bundle), E7 (BinaryAudit private payload),
E8-original (LifeSciBench package), E10-original (AgentHarm private
tasks), E12 (AppBench deployment), E13-original (OpenReward held-out
games) and E14-original (FrontierMath hosted evaluation). Terminal
close-out records were written for six of them with an index, and a
contact round followed on 2026-09-21: GitHub issue \#22 on
\texttt{QuesmaOrg/BinaryAudit}, verified live at 2026-09-21T00:42:10Z;
one email each to the E3, E12 and E14 contacts through the local mail
transfer agent; and the E10 HuggingFace discussion \#9 already sent by
the lead on 2026-09-20T16:00Z. The mail caveat is material: the Gmail
API authorisation is stale, so the sends went direct-to-MX with
unaligned SPF, may be spam-filtered, and their remote delivery is
unverified (source: outputs/UNBLOCK\_CARRYOUT\_2026-09-21.md).
E8-original has no send route, so it stays CLOSED\_EXTERNAL with no
pending action.

Beyond completing the campaign, the plan inherits five Phase-1 items,
each answering a threat identified above: adequately powered evaluation,
moving from held-out \emph{n} of 8--20 to the full GSM8K test split or a
large stratified subset at five or more seeds; a token-budget-optimal
curriculum with staleness bounds, tested multi-seed; re-examining P1 to
ask whether the emerged mid-to-late training band supports a FLOP-saving
freeze schedule even though step-1 predictability did not survive
scaling; hardening the P8 classifier on grouped run-level and temporal
splits rather than row-wise cross-validation; and publication,
consolidating the diagnostic findings and the reporting standard into a
paper draft (source:
platform\_tinker/reports/esa\_phase1/Phase1\_Project\_Report\_ZVF.tex).
Semester 4 adds three extensions: the pre-registered controller bakeoff
against static G = 16 and naive boundary heuristics, the test P7
declines to claim in advance of; a cross-framework divergence study
executing an identical task, reward, decoding and seed configuration
across TRL, veRL, OpenRLHF and Tinker, converting P5's attribution
argument from an audit of existing runs into a designed experiment; and
the joint programme connecting them --- dogfooding the registry schema
on the campaign's own runs so that every future benchmark result ships
with a verifiable provenance record.

Two commitments carry forward, because the threats above are structural
rather than incidental. The first is the never-pool rule:
original-contract and replacement-scope numbers stay separate, per-suite
native denominators are never averaged, and launching a lane changes its
coverage, not the meaning of the numbers already recorded. The second is
candour about failure, which here is not a stylistic preference but the
mechanism that made the ledger trustworthy: the E11 sampler path, the E4
zero, the lost execution sources, the seed-count discrepancy and the
failed-run spend are all reported, and any Phase-2 result that turns out
badly will be reported the same way.



\part{Appendices}


\chapter{Appendix A. Complete Run
Registry}\label{appendix-a.-complete-run-registry}

This appendix is the auditable index of every training and evaluation
run on which the claims of this report rest. It does not present results
--- those live in Chapters 6--9 --- but makes each claim traceable to
the artefacts that produced it: run identifier, library and platform,
base model, algorithm, group size, dataset, seed, steps, and terminal
status. Where a claim cannot be traced through this appendix to a run,
the project's evidence rules require it to be withdrawn rather than
stated.

\textbf{The registry covers 1,708 runs: 1,662 in the machine-readable
ledger and 46 more recorded only in the Semester-4 opening artefacts of
§A.3.1.} The ledger,
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/experiment\_ledger.tsv},
holds one row per run with the columns
\texttt{source,\ project,\ run\_id,\ model,\ task,\ algo,\ G,\ steps,\ created,\ headline\_metric}.
Its 1,662 data rows decompose into \textbf{844 runs inventoried from the
Tinker training service} and \textbf{818 runs inventoried from Weights
\& Biases}. The Tinker inventory was built by paginating
\texttt{RestClient.list\_training\_runs} and \texttt{list\_checkpoints};
the W\&B inventory was built per project and validated against the live
W\&B API. The ledger's provenance document,
\texttt{EXPERIMENT\_LEDGER.md}, records the same grand total and notes
that the W\&B workspace holds fourteen projects, not the thirteen
previously believed (\texttt{tinker-new-research} was the omission).

Two other totals appear in the repository and are deliberately
\emph{not} used here: the identity-audit manifest records 949 Tinker and
940 W\&B runs, and \texttt{tinker\_runs.txt} reports ``20 training runs
(963 more not shown)''. These are snapshots of the same platforms at
different times and are not reconcilable without re-querying them. The
ledger is the only artefact carrying per-run identifiers for both
platforms in one file, so it is the figure used throughout for ledgered
runs. The 46 runs outside it are counted separately, from the per-run
\texttt{name} and mode fields of the artefacts that recorded them, and
are named individually in §A.3.1.

The remaining Semester-3 runs are the exploratory blocks of Table A.1:
\texttt{tinker-rl-lab-world-class} (174 runs over fourteen base models
from Llama-3.2-1B to Qwen3-235B-A22B), \texttt{tinker-rl-scaling} (88,
the material behind the cross-scale study),
\texttt{tinker-structural-ceiling} (72), \texttt{tinker-rl-webarena}
(40) plus the separate \texttt{webarena-eval} scoring pass (26), the
agentic smoke block (10), three SkyRL-on-Tinker runs, and three Hugging
Face runs. These are indexed by identifier in the ledger but given here
in aggregate, because for most of them fewer than half the required
columns have values.

\section{A.3 Semester-4 P1--P8 study
runs}\label{a.3-semester-4-p1p8-study-runs}

The P1--P8 series is the individual Semester-4 contribution, and its
runs are indexed by \emph{claim} rather than by project. The audit
ledger at \texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/claim\_to\_run/}
maps each published claim to its supporting run identifiers, the W\&B
URLs where they can be inspected, whether the model named in the paper
matches the model actually trained, the seed and step counts, the
held-out metric, and an evidence tier. It holds \textbf{18 claims}
carrying \textbf{713 claim-to-run links} --- a count of links, not of
distinct runs, and the 368 links on P7-C1 are the \texttt{zvf-audit}
project count rather than 368 separable experiments.

\textbf{Table A.5 --- P1--P8 claim-to-run ledger.} Tier vocabulary: A =
≥5 seeds and ≥100 steps; B = 3--4 seeds and 50--99 steps; C =
completed/descriptive; R = deterministic resource or schema claim; X =
unlinked, contradicted, or provenance-conflicted.

Three facts here are load-bearing. First, \textbf{no empirical headline
row in the P1--P8 ledger reaches Tier A or B} under the
statistical-rigor thresholds; the rectified tally is C = 12, R = 2, X =
4, and the registry's only Tier A block belongs to P11, an extension
described below. Second, the four Tier X rows are X for specific,
checkable reasons: \textbf{P3-C2} rests on G = 32 \emph{sampling} cells
with zero optimizer steps, so no matched G = 4/G = 32 training
replication exists; \textbf{P4-C1}'s abstract names Qwen3-8B while every
mapped run is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct}; \textbf{P5-C1}
compares \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base} against \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, so
the base checkpoint changes together with the backend and the pair must
not be cited as a backend-only causal effect; and \textbf{P8-C3} is a
proposed architecture with no execution, hence zero linked runs. Third,
of 41 exact Tinker identifiers in the identity audit, 33 are
model-consistent with their W\&B counterpart and 8 conflict; five
conflicts were resolved as W\&B mislinks by three-way arbitration
against the Hugging Face adapter listing, and three remain unresolved
(\texttt{0ef59237}, Qwen3.5-4B recorded against a Nemotron run;
\texttt{78e2d35b}, Qwen3.5-27B; \texttt{0dfee749}, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct
recorded against a DeepSeek V3.1 run). Those three \texttt{model} fields
are contested.

Two column caveats travel with the table. The dataset cell for P7-C1 is
\texttt{—} because the ledger's only dataset-adjacent field for those
368 runs is a prompt/tokenizer family (\texttt{llama3},
\texttt{qwen3\_instruct}, \texttt{gpt\_oss\_low\_reasoning},
\texttt{nemotron3\_disable\_thinking}), not a dataset name. And the P3
sweep here is \textbf{not} the Semester-3 sweep of Table A.4: it is a
different project (\texttt{rlvr-openings}), a different model
(Qwen3.5-4B), and it reports deltas of 0.0000 rather than held-out
accuracies near 0.99. The two share a group-size grid and nothing else.

The extension ledger added in August 2026 carries five rows besides P11:
P10-C1 is Tier R (an algebraic identity, not an empirical result),
P10-C2 and P12-C2 are Tier X (placeholder figures and unlinked
prospective claims, zero runs), and P12-C1 is Tier R as a lemma and Tier
X as an empirical headline.

\textbf{P11} is the only block in the registry meeting the report's
strictest evidence bar: 40 arm--seed units at
\texttt{local:zvf-program/audit/results/full/\{grpo,dapo,gspo,drgrpo,aero\}-seed-\{11,23,37,53,71,89,107,131\}.json},
five arms × eight seeds on one stack (Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, 30 steps, Colab
A100, held-out n = 500). Its headline contrast, DAPO versus GRPO, is Δ =
+0.001, CI {[}−0.0045, +0.00675{]}, MDE₈₀ = 0.01012, all raw p
\textgreater{} 0.21, with Benjamini--Hochberg rejecting nothing. The
mechanism is visible in the diagnostic: DAPO's mean ZVF is 0.000 against
GRPO's 0.693, bought with 3.61× the rollouts (1,734 versus 480) and
1.44× the wall clock. It is the only place in the work where a claim
rests on five or more seeds over a hundred or more steps, and it
supports a null.

\subsection{A.3.1 Opening and null-result
runs}\label{a.3.1-opening-and-null-result-runs}

Chapter 6 reports four null families --- a mixed-variance curriculum, a
matched token budget, hard-prompt filtering, and step-1 layer selection
(§6.4.3) --- and each rests on runs that are not in the ledger. They are
absent for a checkable reason: the string \texttt{rlvr-openings} occurs
nowhere in the ledger, and no file in the fourteen-project
\texttt{wandb\_inventory/} covers it, although every Tinker-managed
block below is logged to that project under the groups named in the
table. The rows are counted from the per-run \texttt{name} (or mode)
fields of each artefact's JSON --- \texttt{results.json} for the
curriculum, token-budget, hard-prompt and loss-formulation blocks,
\texttt{result.json} and its siblings for P1 --- not from an inventory
API, which is why this cohort is reported as its own total. Three
neighbouring blocks add nothing to it: the P3 group-size artefacts are
already indexed --- \texttt{groupsize\_zvf/results.json} reproduces the
per-group deltas of Table A.5's P3-C1 row exactly, and the second sweep
file is discussed below --- and the P2, P7 and P8 ``openings''
(\texttt{p2\_openings/collapse\_baseline\_analysis.json},
\texttt{p7\_controller/sim.json}, \texttt{p8\_openings/*.json}) are
zero-compute re-analyses that launch no runs.

\textbf{Table A.6 --- Semester-4 opening and null-result runs (46
runs).} Model is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B} and dataset GSM8K unless a row
says otherwise. All rows are Tinker-managed except the P1 layer-freeze
block, which ran on Colab for per-layer gradient access. ``Curriculum''
means training only on non-collapsed (mixed-variance) groups. Group
sizes marked \emph{default} are the launch defaults of the scripts in
\texttt{platform\_hybrid/experiments/openings/}; only the campaign
artefact records a group size per run. Δ is the change in held-out
accuracy.

Three provenance notes belong with this table, because two of its blocks
carry superseded siblings and one carries a refuted claim. First,
\texttt{campaign/FINDINGS.md} describes a nine-run, three-seed version
of the campaign on a twelve-prompt held-out set with arm means of +0.028
and −0.028; \texttt{campaign/results.json} holds fifteen runs over six
seeds on a thirty-prompt held-out set (0.8667 = 26/30), and the table
follows the per-run file. The FINDINGS warning that
\texttt{zero\_loss\_frac} was invalid (an empty-metrics-dict bug,
``since fixed'') belongs to the nine-run batch, not to that file.
Second, the P1 layer-freeze block has the same shape: the two-seed 3B
pass (overlap 0.1111 ± 0.0000) is superseded by the four-seed pass
(0.0833 ± 0.0481), and both sit near chance, so the toy-scale overlap of
1.0 must not be read as a transferable layer-selection signal. Third,
the two single-seed training blocks flagged SUSPECT by independent
verification (kimi, 2026-07-06) are superseded by the multi-seed blocks
above: the curriculum opening's +0.05 (campaign curriculum and baseline
arms separate by 0.006 over six seeds, at 3.3--7.2× oversample cost),
and a single-seed G = 4 gain of +0.125 in
\texttt{p3\_groupsize/sweep\_results.json} --- the campaign's G4 seed-0
baseline gains 0.0000, not 0.125, and that sweep file retains only two
of its twelve entries, so the W\&B group is its record.

the ledger of record,

headline.

\section{A.5 Completeness
limitations}\label{a.5-completeness-limitations}

The registry is complete as an \emph{index} and incomplete as a
\emph{dataset}, and the distinction governs how it may be used. Coverage
is measured over the 1,662 ledger rows only --- the 46 opening runs of
§A.3.1 are logged per run in their own artefacts and are not part of
this table:

The largest gap is structural: \textbf{all 844 Tinker-source rows record
\texttt{task}, \texttt{algo}, \texttt{G} and \texttt{steps} as
\texttt{UNKNOWN}.} The Tinker pagination API returns training-run
identity, base model, LoRA rank and checkpoint metadata but not the
training configuration, so those runs' configurations cannot be
recovered from the surviving inventory, and every algorithm label in the
registry comes from the W\&B half of the ledger. The raw snapshot
\texttt{tinker\_runs.txt} shows the same boundary differently: run ID,
base model truncated to ``Qwen\ldots{}'', LoRA rank 4, timestamp and
status ``OK'' --- no configuration at all.

Four further limits apply.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Aggregate presentation.} This appendix names 105 runs
  individually: the 4 cross-framework runs of Table A.2, the 20 Modal
  campaign runs of A.3, the 12 sweep runs (in 4 rows) of A.4, the 46
  opening runs (in 16 rows) of A.6, and the 23 E-lane rows of A.7 ---
  plus the 18 claim rows of Table A.5, which index claims rather than
  runs. The remaining \textasciitilde1,600 runs are indexed by
  identifier and project in \texttt{experiment\_ledger.tsv}, presented
  here in aggregate because for most of them fewer than half the
  required columns have values.
\item
  \textbf{Non-runs inside the count.} Three entries are not training
  results: \texttt{verl-dryrun} and \texttt{openrlhf-dryrun} are seeded
  deterministic fallbacks, and the E4 base-model rerun performs zero
  training steps; to these add §A.3.1's P1 gradient probe, which takes
  no optimizer steps at all. They are counted because they are runs in
  the registry's sense, and flagged so no reader treats them as
  measurements.
\item
  \textbf{Identity conflicts.} Eight Tinker identifiers were linked to
  W\&B runs whose recorded model did not match; five were resolved
  against the Hugging Face adapter listing, three remain unresolved, and
  those \texttt{model} fields are contested.
\item
  \textbf{Snapshot disagreement.} The 844/818 split used here, the
  949/940 split in the audit manifest, and the ``983 runs'' implied by
  \texttt{tinker\_runs.txt} are three observations of the same platforms
  at three times. No reconciliation was attempted and none should be
  inferred.
\end{enumerate}

None of this changes a reported result, because every result in Chapters
6--9 is bound to named artefacts rather than to the registry as a whole.
It does bound what the registry can be used for: it establishes
provenance and identity, shows that the run population is far larger
than the reported claims, and shows that those claims rest on a small,
explicitly tiered subset --- one Tier A block, a majority Tier C, and
four Tier X rows that must not be cited as evidence.



\chapter{Appendix B. Reproducibility and
Provenance}\label{appendix-b.-reproducibility-and-provenance}

\section{B.1 What this appendix
establishes}\label{b.1-what-this-appendix-establishes}

This appendix records three things that should not be confused with one
another. The first is what a reviewer can independently re-run: a pinned
container, a pinned dependency set, a headline training command, and the
tolerance within which a rerun is expected to land. The second is what
the repository verifies about itself without re-running any model: an
audit suite driven by a single entry point, and a deterministic ledger
check that recomputes the reported arithmetic directly from surviving
evidence files. The third is provenance: which parts of this work were
produced by the Semester 3 group, which by the individual contribution
reported here, and what was lost along the way.

\section{B.2 The pinned runtime}\label{b.2-the-pinned-runtime}

The container definition is \texttt{Dockerfile}, built from
\texttt{nvidia/cuda:12.4.1-cudnn-devel-ubuntu22.04}. It installs Python
3.12 through the deadsnakes archive rather than the distribution
default, with the in-file comment that Python 3.12 ``matches the frozen
runtime validated 106/106''. It sets \texttt{PYTHONHASHSEED=42} and a
fixed \texttt{LANG}/\texttt{LC\_ALL} of \texttt{C.UTF-8}; stamps the
build commit into an OCI \texttt{org.opencontainers.image.revision}
label and a \texttt{.build\_info} file; fixes \texttt{SEED=42},
\texttt{WANDB\_MODE=offline}, \texttt{HF\_HUB\_DISABLE\_TELEMETRY=1} and
\texttt{OMP\_NUM\_THREADS=8}; self-checks by importing the core stack at
build time; and runs under \texttt{tini}.

The authoritative pin set is \texttt{requirements.txt}, headed as
``pinned to the frozen E1 runtime lock \ldots{} validated 106/106 on
this stack''. Its exact pins include \texttt{torch==2.7.1},
\texttt{numpy==2.2.6}, \texttt{transformers==5.5.4},
\texttt{trl==1.2.0}, \texttt{datasets==4.8.4},
\texttt{accelerate==1.13.0}, \texttt{peft==0.19.1},
\texttt{huggingface-hub==1.11.0} and \texttt{wandb==0.21.0}, with
bounded ranges for scipy, safetensors, tokenizers, rliable, math-verify,
latex2sympy2-extended and pytest, alongside the framework clients
\texttt{tinker}, \texttt{tinker-cookbook} and \texttt{atroposlib}.

The 106/106 figure is the recorded outcome of the frozen-runtime
validation, which \texttt{execution-notes.md} states as passing the
complete pinned pilot/preregistration suite on Python 3.12.13 with the
pinned versions listed above, and the focused amendment, protocol,
launcher and verifier suite passing 55/55. The same record binds five
artefacts by SHA-256 --- protocol document, screening manifest, launch
manifest, launch fingerprint and source binding --- and states that a
mismatch in package, Python, A100, FLOP, token or gradient conditions
fails closed rather than proceeding.

\section{B.3 The reviewer's entry
point}\label{b.3-the-reviewers-entry-point}

\texttt{REPRODUCE.md} is the reviewer-facing document. Its fastest path
is the structural smoke test at
\texttt{platform\_modal/scripts/smoke\_test.sh}, which runs in about two
minutes offline and about eight when a live Tinker key is supplied so
that the wire protocol is exercised; the offline path needs no GPU and
no account.

The headline result is GRPO on Qwen3-8B over GSM8K for 30 steps at LoRA
rank 32, group size 8 and seed 42, recorded as a peak accuracy of 62.5
\% with a last-10 average of 34.4 \%. Reviewer-side reruns are accepted
within ±5 percentage points on the last-10 figure and ±10 points on
peak, justified in the same document by the residual non-determinism
discussed in §B.10. Per-seed reference values and W\&B run identifiers
are tabulated for seeds 42, 123, 456 and 789, with seed 1024 marked as a
reviewer-only rerun; the canonical multi-seed set is
\texttt{\{42,\ 123,\ 456,\ 789,\ 1024,\ 2048,\ 4096,\ 8192,\ 16384,\ 32768\}}.
The group-size ablation is expected to reproduce a peak of 50.0 \% at
G=2, 75.0 \% at G=4, 100 \% at G=8 and 71.9 \% at G=16, with last-10
values of 37.5 \%, 52.1 \%, 84.4 \% and 38.0 \%. Five-seed aggregation
runs through \texttt{utils/stats.py} with rliable intervals and 10,000
bootstrap samples; \texttt{utils/verify\_results.py} checks a rerun
against the recorded expectations at those tolerances.

The cost is stated plainly: about five GPU-hours on a single A100-80GB
for the headline run, against roughly 446 GPU-hours for a full paper
reproduction. Dataset provenance is tabulated with pinned revisions ---
GSM8K at \texttt{740312a} with 7,473 training and 1,319 test rows, plus
MATH-500, HumanEval, ArenaHard Auto, NoRobots and OpenThoughts3 each at
a named snapshot --- with exact shas in \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} §3.4.

\section{B.4 Where the documentation has drifted from the
code}\label{b.4-where-the-documentation-has-drifted-from-the-code}

Four discrepancies exist between the repository's prose and its live
tree, and a reviewer following the prose literally would hit them. The
training driver has moved: \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} and
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} both invoke \texttt{grpo\_gsm8k\_base.py} at the
repository root, and the file now lives at
\texttt{platform\_tinker/grpo\_gsm8k\_base.py}, though the documented
command still works because the CLI in
\texttt{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo\_cli.py} carries a compatibility
entry point, \texttt{legacy\_main}, that translates the documented flag
spellings onto the current ones --- what changed is the path a reviewer
must type, not the flags the documents specify. \texttt{ARTIFACT.md}
names the smoke test at \texttt{scripts/smoke\_test.sh} rather than at
\texttt{platform\_modal/scripts/smoke\_test.sh}, though
\texttt{REPRODUCE.md} gives the correct path. The pins quoted in prose
are stale: \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} §9 states that
\texttt{requirements.txt} pins
\texttt{transformers\textgreater{}=4.56.2,\textless{}4.60.0} and a
\texttt{datasets\textless{}3.2} bound, whereas the live file pins
\texttt{transformers==5.5.4} and \texttt{datasets==4.8.4}, and
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} §3.3 quotes a third, older set; similarly,
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} describes the environment as Python 3.10 from the
Ubuntu 22.04 archive, whereas the \texttt{Dockerfile} installs Python
3.12 via deadsnakes and the frozen-runtime record validates on Python
3.12.13. The \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} items are explained by that document's
frozen status --- it records a Semester 3 artefact at commit
\texttt{9019b5c946f17ffe4761b5f4eac13b86d1e51cc0}, dated 19 April 2026
--- but \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} §9 is live guidance, not a frozen record,
and carries the same defect.

\section{B.5 The audit apparatus}\label{b.5-the-audit-apparatus}

The integrity suite is driven by a single entry point,
\texttt{platform\_local/run\_all\_audits.py}, which imports a shared
result interface from \texttt{utils/audit\_utils.py} and registers its
audits as a tuple of name-and-function pairs. As it stands in this tree,
that tuple contains \textbf{nine} audits: \texttt{claim\_issues},
\texttt{sync\_issues}, \texttt{anon\_issues}, \texttt{strength\_issues},
\texttt{package\_issues}, \texttt{workflow\_issues},
\texttt{export\_guard\_issues}, \texttt{caveat\_issues} and
\texttt{scientific\_issues}. Each is implemented by a corresponding
\texttt{platform\_local/} module carrying a \texttt{\_\_main\_\_} block,
so each can also run on its own; \texttt{tests/test\_audit\_runner.py}
asserts that the registered modules and the
\texttt{platform\_local/*\_audit.py} files are the same set.

Some repository documents give different counts, and the difference is
historical. At the blind-review sweep the suite was thirteen audits,
registered as a list of script filenames at the repository root and
executed as subprocesses; the committed log of that run records
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total=13} with ten passing and three failing,
and the same sweep states that all seventeen \texttt{*\_audit.py}
scripts then at the root were executed, with the suite covering thirteen
of them. The registry now holds nine audits, all passing: a pruning
commit removed obsolete audits, and a later restructuring replaced the
subprocess runner with the in-process interface, so the seventeen-script
description has narrowed by the same route. No script now prints a
metric line directly; the line is constructed in one place,
\texttt{AuditResult.metric\_line()} in \texttt{utils/audit\_utils.py},
and emitted by the nine audit modules plus the runner. A repository-wide
scan finds several dozen files matching \texttt{*\_audit.py}, but that
population is not a suite: it includes unrelated analyses, test modules
and a third-party file in the virtual environment.

The output contract is uniform. \texttt{AuditResult.metric\_line()}
emits
\texttt{METRIC\ \textless{}name\textgreater{}=\textless{}issue\_count\textgreater{}},
so every audit reports one machine-readable line whose right-hand side
is the number of issues it found, and an audit passes exactly when that
count is zero. \texttt{render\_suite} prints each audit's metric line
and issue codes, then three suite-level lines ---
\texttt{METRIC\ suite\_issues}, \texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total} and
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_passing} --- before either listing the failing
audits by name or stating that all audits are passing. The entry point
exits \texttt{0} when the suite passes and \texttt{1} otherwise, which
makes it usable as a gate; the packaging step runs the suite before
exporting and refuses to package if it fails. The runner's own test
asserts this rendering against a synthetic two-audit suite, checking for
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total=2} and \texttt{METRIC\ suite\_issues=1}.

\section{B.6 The deterministic ledger
check}\label{b.6-the-deterministic-ledger-check}

Where the suite above audits the submission package's internal
consistency, a second instrument audits the campaign's reported figures.
\texttt{outputs/verification/LEDGER\_CODE\_CHECK\_2026-09-19.json},
under schema \texttt{ledger-code-check-v1}, records eleven checks of
which eleven passed and none failed. Each recomputes a claim from the
underlying evidence rather than trusting a summary of it, and the four
classes of check prove four different things.

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\caption{The deterministic ledger check by class, listing each specific

Exact divisions & E1 \texttt{2/731}; E11 \texttt{129/312}; E11
\texttt{67+62=129}; E14 \texttt{4426/4428} and accuracy
\texttt{0.5131043831902395}; E9 \texttt{40\ of\ 75} & That each reported
ratio is the quotient of the two integers the report claims, and that a
partition sums to its whole \\
Receipt presence & E8 eight categories summing to 1967 with every
\texttt{evaluated\ ==\ expected\_total}; E9 graded-row counts; the
strict-completion set & That every claimed unit of coverage has a
surviving graded row behind it, and that the set of strictly complete
suites is exactly \texttt{{[}E8,\ E10,\ E11{]}} \\
Sealed-artefact hash equality & E1 v6 sealed request and fixture receipt
& That the sealed request and the validation receipt are byte-identical
to the artefacts recorded at sealing time \\
Lost-source absence probes &
\texttt{codex\_run\_execution\_sources\_absent} & That four
\texttt{.codex-run} paths are genuinely absent, so a later re-creation
cannot be mistaken for the lost original \\
\end{longtable}

The distinction matters under questioning. An exact division proves the
arithmetic is not a rounding convenience or a transcription error; it
cannot prove the underlying evidence is sound. A receipt-presence check
proves coverage claims are backed by artefacts; it cannot prove those
artefacts were produced correctly. A hash-equality check proves identity
between two artefacts --- here, between what was sealed and what was
later validated --- and is the strongest of the four because it does not
depend on re-running anything. An absence probe proves a negative: it
prevents a later reconstruction from being passed off as the original.
Two instances show the classes in use. The E1 records show the sealed
request's SHA-256 beginning \texttt{7adc6e2616f61bc7} matching
\texttt{validation\_v6.json}, with the fixture receipt matching on the
same basis. The E9 records show 41 graded rows resolving to 40 unique
competitions against a denominator of 75 in the streaming arm and
exactly one valid grade in the merged-VLLM arm, which is the check that
confirms the two serving arms were never pooled.

\section{B.7 The receipt model}\label{b.7-the-receipt-model}

A receipt in this campaign is a durable JSON artefact binding an outcome
to the inputs that produced it, and the binding carries the evidential
weight rather than the file's existence. The clearest statement of the
principle is the receipt-bundle auditor's docstring, which records that
the campaign and the receipt builders are kept deliberately separate
from the auditor, and that the auditor ``never imports a provider SDK,
contacts a service, launches a job, or treats
\texttt{status}/\texttt{online}/a URL/ID alone as provenance''. The
auditor reconstructs contract coverage, receipt predicates, canonical
hash rules, budget caps and cross-artefact bindings, carrying its
expectations as constants rather than reading them from what it checks
--- suite counts of 12 training, 14 primary-evaluation and 26 total, and
budget constants of 18.00 USD maximum, 16.50 USD operational cap and a
1.50 USD safety reserve --- so a receipt set that quietly loses a suite
fails the audit rather than redefining it.

Receipts bind four dimensions of a run: protocol, runtime, source and
event order. The frozen-runtime record applies that discipline to the
runtime dimension alone, binding five artefacts --- protocol document,
screening manifest, launch manifest, launch fingerprint and source
binding --- by SHA-256 and failing closed on any mismatch. The judgment
layer for subjectively scored questions follows the same pattern: one
durable receipt per judgment binds the state bytes, the question text
and the raw answer, and battery execution is gated on a health check
rather than the service's availability, so a degraded answer cannot be
laundered into a valid one. Where a lane could not be scored, its
terminal state is recorded in the ledger of record with a named reopen
condition, so the absence of a result is itself receipted.

\section{B.8 Evidence loss in the finish
era}\label{b.8-evidence-loss-in-the-finish-era}

One loss of evidence occurred and is recorded rather than concealed.
During post-review disk cleanup the untracked \texttt{.codex-run/}
working tree was deleted. Four paths were confirmed absent by the
absence probes described above:
\texttt{finish\_20260912/e1\_recovery\_v6/bounded\_sdk.py}, holding the
E1 wave10 repair code;
\texttt{finish\_20260912/e9\_completion/restored/}, holding 1,613
hash-verified restored dependency sources from the OpenHands and
Composio forks used to build the MLDevBench runtime;
\texttt{finish\_20260912/e6\_continuation/checks\_v1.py}, an offline
receipt validator the reconciliation record describes as structure-only
and of low impact; and
\texttt{public\_native\_improve\_v2/e1/metadata\_wave08.py}, the driver
for the E1 multilingual run that graded 35 of 300 tasks.

The loss does not invalidate any figure reported in this thesis, for
three reasons that hold independently. First, none of the four paths is
the source of any reported score: the E1 wave10 repair and the E9
runtime build are execution sources for lanes that remain unlaunched,
whose status is \texttt{REBUILD\_READY\_LAUNCH\_PENDING} and whose
values appear in no results table, and the E1 multilingual figure of 35
of 300 graded is reported as an incomplete replacement-scope state
rather than as a suite score. Second, the sealed requests, validation
receipts, regression fixtures and the 10-of-10 targeted-test log from
the lost E1 code all survive, so a faithful re-implementation is
constrained by surviving fixtures rather than free to invent behaviour;
that re-implementation has since landed under a tracked path and
reproduces the lost suite's record exactly, four process-group
regressions and six cleanup regressions. Third, the loss is confined to
finish-era iteration code: the replacement-lane native runners survive
in tracked locations, including a 515-line multilingual runner carrying
no reference to the deleted tree. The policy adopted in response ---
that execution sources live in tracked repository paths and never in
untracked scratch trees --- is the durable outcome of the incident.

\section{B.9 The Semester 3 and Semester 4 provenance
boundary}\label{b.9-the-semester-3-and-semester-4-provenance-boundary}

The boundary between inherited and individual work is a git tag.
\texttt{capstone-final-2026-04-25} resolves to commit
\texttt{21a99ef766543a1ca86bfbad188445d3596ea73c}, dated 23 April 2026,
whose subject line records the final metric-separation rewrite that
segregated training rewards, held-out capability and proxy diagnostics.
Two hundred and fifty commits precede that tag. A reviewer can reproduce
the state at the boundary by adding it as a worktree,
\texttt{git\ worktree\ add\ ../tinker-rl-lab-sem3\ capstone-final-2026-04-25},
which materialises the Semester 3 tree without disturbing the current
checkout.

The Semester 3 group comprised six students --- Arvind C R, Sandhya
Jeyaraj, Madhu Kumara L, Mohammad Rafi, Dhruva N Murthy and Arumugam
Chetty K --- under guides Anwesh Reddy Paduri and Narayana Darapaneni.
Their work is the multi-framework benchmark infrastructure and the
initial GRPO baseline experiments. Semester 4 was carried out
individually under Ramesh Prakash Guledgudd, and the declaration facing
this report assigns to that contribution the research direction, the
experimental expansion, the statistical analyses, the P1--P8 paper
series and the E1--E14 campaign. The solo-authorship commit
\texttt{282e6b4f0f158de11b3f5bb4656e2d871f68f5c0}, dated 7 July 2026,
records that division at paper level. The provenance record states that
584 commits and 5,050 changed files separated the boundary from the tree
as it then stood, and that the eight paper PDFs were compiled at commit
\texttt{006e90a38e4dabe2d0b4778be01dc7b27f2d3462} on 9 July 2026, one
recompiled the following day after a findings-ledger rename. Those
counts carry an as-of date rather than describing the current tree, so a
reviewer recomputing them will obtain a larger number.

One consequence of this history shapes the repository's structure: the
implementation remains shared, with experiment drivers, result tables,
figures, LaTeX sources and audit scripts referencing one another across
the boundary, and the provenance record is explicit that this is why the
code is not split into separate trees. What the boundary separates is
authorship of the research contribution, not access to the machinery.
Two reporting rules follow and are enforced throughout this report:
original-contract and replacement-scope results are never pooled, with
no cross-suite aggregate, which the ledger check independently verifies
through the arm-separation and component-split checks; and no
replacement benchmark's score is presented as the score of the benchmark
it replaces, with every substitution named as such at its point of use.

\section{B.10 Residual non-determinism and the limits of
reproduction}\label{b.10-residual-non-determinism-and-the-limits-of-reproduction}

Not everything here is reproducible in the strict sense, and the
tolerances exist because of it. Three sources of residual
non-determinism survive even with fixed seeds and deterministic cuDNN
settings: remote LoRA training is not bit-deterministic across scheduler
decisions, with runs replayed months apart drifting by two to four
percentage points on GSM8K last-10, documented through three reruns of
the same seed-123 configuration; some matmul operations on H100 and A100
hardware introduce non-determinism through CUDA and cuBLAS; and sampling
at temperature 0.8 with top-p 0.95 executes in a remote virtual machine,
so a fixed seed does not fix the sampled trajectory. The record states
the consequence itself: run-to-run variance on this setup is of the same
order as the effect reported in the paper's PPO-versus-GRPO comparison,
which is why that comparison is reported as statistically
indistinguishable rather than as a difference.

Three limits remain. Lanes blocked on external access, cloud quota or
authorisation cannot be reproduced from this repository, because the
blocking resource is not in it. The evidence-loss paths cannot be
reproduced in their original form, only re-implemented against surviving
fixtures, and that distinction should be preserved in any future
citation. And figures originating at the Semester 3 boundary describe
the tree at that tag, so reproducing them requires the worktree checkout
rather than the current HEAD.

\section{C.3 Four collisions worth
flagging}\label{c.3-four-collisions-worth-flagging}

The symbol \(\varepsilon\) carries three unrelated meanings. In the
hyperparameter mapping it is the PPO/GRPO clip range, default 0.2, swept
over \(\{0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5\}\). In the ZVF definition it is a
numerical tolerance, \(10^{-6}\), deciding whether a group's sample
variance counts as degenerate. In the optimiser configuration it is the
Adam epsilon, \(10^{-8}\). It also appears additively in the advantage
denominator, \(\sigma_g + \varepsilon\).

\(K\) is used two ways: in the formal treatment of ZVF it denotes the
rollouts in a group, a synonym for the group size elsewhere called
\(G\), whereas in the derivations appendix \(G\) is the group size and
\(K\) is the number of successes --- a design parameter in the one case,
an outcome in the other. The letter \(r\) is similarly the reward, the
LoRA rank (swept over \(\{4,8,16,32,64\}\)) and the Pearson correlation.

Finally, ZVF is expanded two ways in the sources. The formal definition
and the abstract both read ``Zero-Variance Fraction'', the expansion
used here; one figure caption reads ``Zero-Value-Filtered''. The caption
variant is noted only so that a reader meeting it is not misled.
